The fix stores BOTH semi-axis vectors, the representation `EllipseSeg` already carries, so a rotated ellipse round-trips with nobody re-deriving an angle from a vector and back. **Optional `[Param]` values** work too: a nullable parameter (`double?`, `int?`, `bool?`, `enum?`) was silently DROPPED on load, because `Convert` had no nullable case and threw "unsupported parameter type Nullable`1", which `ApplyParameters` swallows into a warning — everything else in the seam already handled it (`SerializeValue` passes null through, `Feature.FormatValue` renders it `"null"` in the cache key, `Validate` skips a range test on a value that is not there, and `ParamEditors` already unwraps `Nullable.GetUnderlyingType`). **The backlog's framing of that was half right and the other half is the finding**: a sentinel 0 is NOT purely a serializer workaround, because `ParamEditors.KindFor` offers a SLIDER exactly when `[Param(Min=, Max=)]` is finite at both ends and a slider is a total function onto its range with no way to say "unset" — so an optional parameter behind one could be moved off "inherit" and never back. Hence the stated rule, applied to `EdgeFlangeFeature`: **a parameter whose editor can express absence takes the nullable type; one whose editor cannot keeps a sentinel outside its own legal range** — `Width` and `BendRadius` (unbounded above, hence text boxes, which show an unset value as empty and take `null` back) became `double?`, while `KFactor` keeps its 0 (the one with a finite range; `SheetMetalSpec` refuses 0 as a K-factor anyway, so the sentinel costs no legal value, and the slider's own minimum IS 0 so "inherit" stays one drag away). **Whole-document persistence** (`DocumentFile.cs`): a `Document` is a `Scene` plus the `MateSet`s a scene cannot own, saved as ONE versioned JSON envelope — tabs, parts (name/colour/transform/display mode/`ClippedBySection`/debug modifiers), each part's `FeatureHistory` via `SaveHistory`, assemblies with occurrences and explode offsets, mates via `SaveMates` (parsed back in rather than restated, so the mate vocabulary stays in `MatePersistence`), annotations, results and `FieldDisplay`. **A document is its construction history, not its geometry** — a history-backed part regenerates on load and is still parametric — and the parts with no recipe (raw `HalfEdgeMesh`, imported `.stl`, `Sdf`, a `Shape` graph built in code) are handled EXPLICITLY rather than dropped: their display mesh is embedded as a binary-exact base64 **snapshot** and `DocumentLoadResult.Snapshots` names every part that came back that way — as **`"tab/part"`**, the qualified spelling every part-taking tool already accepts, because part names are unique per TAB rather than per document and a bare name would print twice for a document whose tabs share one, leaving a host to act on whichever it found first (a part that ended up in no tab keeps its bare name; there is none to give). Qualifying is why the list is collected as `Part` REFERENCES and resolved after the tabs are wired: parts are read before the tabs that reference them, so at the moment a snapshot is recorded its tab does not exist yet — so a host can say "these are not parametric" instead of a UI discovering it when a parameter changes nothing (`EmbedGeometry = false` writes a recipe-only file where those become an explicit "no geometry" record naming the reason). Embedded rather than an external path, deliberately: a document that points at its neighbours is a *manifest*, and the reference breaks the first time the file is moved — filed rather than built for the one case it wins (a scan mesh too big to inline). Loading returns warnings and never throws (opaque features via the `ResolveOpaqueFeature` hook, selector-backed annotations — `Annotation.SaveJson` mirrors `Feature.SaveInputs`, returning null for a lambda — catalogue parts whose geometry loads but whose `HardwareComponent` cannot); only a bad envelope or an unknown version throws. **The fixed point is the test that earns its keep**: `save → load → save` being byte-identical is the ONLY check that catches a field written but never read, a default that reloads as a different default, or an ordering that is not a function of the model — all of which pass a volumes-and-poses comparison. It caught one immediately: a snapshot's `"source"` field naming the geometry type it came from is useful to a human and *cannot* round-trip, because a `BoxShape` snapshot reloads as a `HalfEdgeMesh` and the second save writes a different name — hence the rule that **an informational field either round-trips or does not belong in the file** (the actionable half is computed at load, in `Snapshots`). Scope stated honestly: a file carrying opaque records is smaller the SECOND time by exactly the records the load warned about, then a fixed point — "a record was reported and dropped" and "the file is drifting" are different things and are asserted separately. **Undo/redo** (`DocumentEdits.cs`/`UndoStack.cs`) is `MeshChange` at document granularity: a `DocumentEdit` captures whatever it is about to overwrite and restores that on `Revert`, an `UndoStack` runs them with `Group(...)` collecting several into ONE user-visible step (nesting; a group of one keeps its own label; an empty one pushes nothing), and the vocabulary is what a UI performs — `SetParameter`/`SetParameters`/`Suppress`/`AddFeature`/`RemoveFeature` (all through the SAME JSON seam as `SaveParameters` and MCP `set_param`, ending in `Part.Regenerate()`), `Rename`/`SetColor`/`SetTransform`/`SetDisplayMode`/`SetClippedBySection`, `AddOccurrence`/`RemoveOccurrence`/`Repose`/`SetExplodeOffset`, `AddMate`/`RemoveMate`, `AddAnnotation`/`RemoveAnnotation`. **The document SERIALIZER is the test oracle** — every undo asserts `Save()` is byte-identical to the pre-edit save, because a hand-written state comparison agrees with a broken revert as happily as with a correct one, and that is what forced `Assembly.Insert`/`MateSet.Insert`/`Part.InsertAnnotation` beside the `Remove`s (re-adding appends AND re-derives the occurrence name, so an undone removal would come back last and possibly renamed — which a field-by-field check passes). **A refused edit is not history**: `Part.Regenerate` keeps the previous complete body on failure but the bad parameter is still set, so a failing edit takes its own value back, rebuilds, and throws `DocumentEditException` (carrying the `RegenerationResult`) without being pushed or discarding the redo stack; a `CompoundEdit` reverts the members that already succeeded. Regeneration caching survives undo **by construction** — the cache key is the parameter snapshot, so restoring the old value restores the old key and re-runs exactly the prefix a forward edit would (asserted, not assumed). The stack is **session state, not document state**, which is also why it stores edits rather than scene snapshots: the OCAF assessment's "a document snapshot is a value" is the wrong granularity here, since a `Scene` snapshot is not cheap (parts cache meshes, B-Rep and SDF lowerings, feature edges, occlusion) and would either discard those caches or share them into two documents that diverge — hot reload keeps its whole-scene swap because it genuinely rebuilds from source; interactive editing does not. New document-model surface it needed: `Scene.Rename(part, name)` (the tab-uniqueness invariant lives on the scene, the only object that sees every tab), `Assembly.Remove`/`IndexOf`/internal `Insert`, `MateSet.Remove`/`IndexOf`/`Unground`/internal `Insert`, `Part.RemoveAnnotation`/internal `InsertAnnotation`. **Topological naming v1 — face PROVENANCE** (`BrepFace.Provenance`/`DescendsFrom`/`AddProvenance` in BRep; `Shape.Tag(name)`/`TagShape`; `FaceSetRef.Tagged(name)`/`Within(scope)`): the semantic selectors stay the default and answer "what IS this face", but they have one structural blind spot — two identical bosses are indistinguishable to every query in the vocabulary — so `Shape.Tag` says where a face CAME FROM. The B-Rep lowering stamps the name onto every face of its child's solid and faces inherit it wherever one is derived from another; `Tag` is geometrically transparent in all three reps and adds no `Explain` row. **Three decisions carry it.** **(a) Set-valued by construction** — a boolean can split one face into several, so "the face this step made" is not well-formed; narrowing to one is an explicit `FaceRef.One`/`Extreme` claim that fails its cardinality contract loudly, and a scheme promising "the" face would have to guess which fragment, which is how naming schemes come to misresolve silently. **(b) The failure direction IS the safety argument** — inheritance lives at the sites that derive a face from a parent, so a site building from scratch simply does not tag and a query returns FEWER faces, never a face from somewhere else. Landed: the whole boolean pipeline (untouched faces pass through by reference; every `FaceSplitter` fragment and both `BrepBoolean` re-wound-tool sites inherit), `BrepSolid.Clone`, hence `Drill`, patterns, transforms and `Shape.From(solid)` — **and now every REBUILD site too**: `Draft`, `Shelling`, `Filleting`'s `FilletAllEdges` and rim surgery, plus `ShapeHealing`, each of which discards its input's face objects and constructs replacements from a **positional parent**. `DescendsFrom` was already the one rule; the fix was that six derive points had to ASK it (the recurring lesson, here in its cheapest form — no new API was needed, and inventing a second inheritance helper would have been the mistake). **The filed framing had one thing wrong and one missing.** It named four sites; there are SIX derive points, because `Draft` and `Shelling` each have a planar path AND a curved one and the curved halves share `CarrierBody.Rebuild`/`Shell` — so two of the six live in a file that names neither operation, which is exactly the shape of thing a per-operation checklist misses. And `Shelling`'s `Dictionary<BrepFace,int>`, filed as the reason the work was easy, was never used: every one of these sites already walks its parent array positionally, so the index map would have been a second spelling of the loop counter. **What still carries nothing is a statement about the geometry rather than a gap** — a fillet band, a corner patch and a partial run's termination face descend from no single face, so attributing them to one of the two surfaces they join would be a guess; STEP carries no provenance (a format limit). The one case that answers with MORE faces than the design named is shelling, where a wall and its cavity twin both inherit from one parent — honest rather than surprising, since the cavity wall exists only because the outer one does, and it is representable precisely because decision (a) made a tag set-valued. Each boundary stays PINNED by a test, and the new `FaceProvenanceTests` measures each site by tagging exactly ONE face and asserting **where the tag landed** rather than that some face has it — an off-by-one in a parent array leaves the count right and the meaning wrong, which is the one failure mode a naming scheme must not have. **(c) A tag is REFUSED, not sanitized, when the descriptor grammar cannot spell it** — the descriptor is the cache key and the serialized form and is parsed back by `RefLexer.ReadIdentifier`, so sanitizing (the rule an opaque LABEL follows, being human-read only) would turn `"boss top"` into a descriptor resolving to nothing: a silent misresolve, the one outcome a naming scheme must not have. `FaceSetRef.Within(scope)` was forced out by the first real use — a tag names a boss's cylinder AND its plane while rim surgery wants a planar face, so composing provenance with a semantic query is the normal case. Two assertion traps re-learned while measuring it: `IsPlanar`'s origin is an ARBITRARY in-plane point and a circular loop's face frame origin is its single seam VERTEX (both read the rim, not the centre) — locate a face by `Bounds().Center`. Selector-based references are the topological-naming story (semantic queries re-run per regeneration), and they now have a **typed vocabulary** (`GeometryRefs.cs`): a feature says "I need a plane" by declaring a property — `PlaneRef` → `SketchPlane`, `FaceRef` → one face, `FaceSetRef`, `EdgeSetRef`, `AxisRef` → `Ray3d` — each built from nesting named `BrepQueries` queries (`PlanarWithNormal`/`Cylindrical(r?)`/`RimOf`/`Convex`/`Circular(r?)`/`One`/`Extreme`/`On`/`RimFacesOf`/`OfCylindrical`), an explicit frame as the escape hatch, or a lambda as the last resort, and **each carrying cardinality in the TYPE** — which is what none of the five incumbent selector shapes could express. Three properties make it more than sugar. **(a) The `Descriptor` is the cache key AND the serialized form**: one canonical parseable term (`topPlane`, `extreme(planar([0,0,1]),[0,0,1])`) that `ToString` returns, so `FormatValue` picks it up for the regeneration snapshot with no special case and JSON round-tripping needs one line on each side of `Convert`/`SaveParameters` — one string, three jobs, so they cannot disagree. Lambda-backed refs print `opaque(label)`, are `IsSerializable = false` and load as a WARNING, staying sound as cache keys because the snapshot also carries instance identity and a fresh instance always re-runs. Two traps paid for here: the opaque label is sanitized to characters `System.Text.Json` will not escape (a quoted marker came back from a saved file as `'` noise), and an explicit `AxisRef` keeps an ALREADY-unit direction verbatim rather than dividing again — re-normalizing a unit vector moves it by an ulp, and the descriptor would stop being a fixed point under round-trip. **(b) Resolution is per-`Apply` and NEVER memoized on the reference** — it caches on the `FeatureContext`, which is constructed fresh per applied feature, so validation and `Apply` share one query while an edited model always re-resolves; this is the deliberate opposite of `Mates`, which pins at construction because a mate is a numerical constraint rather than a query, so eager-vs-lazy is a consumer choice, not a rule. **(c) `Feature.ValidateInputs` resolves every declared reference BEFORE `Apply`, all-or-nothing, naming the property** ("Plane: expected exactly one cylindrical face, found 0." — `Filleting.RimFacesFor`'s style, not the operation-named message the deferred rim selector gave), reported as `Failed` with the last good prefix intact. The cost note shaped the design: resolving forces `Lowered`, so a reference needing no body (an explicit plane or axis) never triggers one, a feature declaring none pays nothing, and `[DeferredInput]` opts out inputs handed to the `Shape` graph's own late-resolved selectors (the rim features' face sets) where an early resolve would buy a whole extra B-Rep lowering per regeneration and learn nothing — `AsSelector` carries the input name down so the late failure still names it. **Second-wave queries**: `PlaneRef.Offset(distance)`/`Rotated(degrees, inPlaneAxis)` build a construction plane FROM a resolved one (base resolved first, so `TopPlane.Offset(30)` re-finds the top face every regeneration; in-plane axes carried VERBATIM so sketch coordinates keep their meaning, rotation axis stated in the base plane's own coordinates, exact-zero returns the base so a no-op never reaches a descriptor), `FaceSetRef.LargestByArea`/`SmallestByArea` (over `BrepSelection.Area`, ordering-grade and said so), `Touching(point)` (carrier projection THEN the face's own trim test, so a point over a bore matches nothing where a bounds test would say otherwise; set-valued because a point on an edge legitimately matches two), `AdjacentTo(set)` in solid face order, and `CylindricalBetween(min, max)` — the FILTER an exact radius deliberately is not. **Edges carry both range shapes now**: `EdgeSetRef.CircularBetween(min, max)` is the radius twin (`Circular(r)` is weld-tier exact, right for exactly-constructed geometry and useless for "every rim under 5"), and `LongerThan`/`ShorterThan`/`Between` filter on `BrepQueries.Length` — exact for lines and circular arcs, a 64-chord polyline otherwise, so it is honestly a filter on a MEASURED length and there is deliberately no exact-length query beside it to be mistaken for the same thing. An open-ended range gets its own descriptor TERM (`lengthAtLeast(2)`) rather than an infinite bound: `"R"` does print infinity as `Infinity` and `TryParse` would read it back, but `RefLexer.ReadNumber` is a digit/sign/exponent scan SHARED by every reference type, so admitting letters at a number position would change all five parsers to spell one range. The tests assert the three jobs a reference has (it resolves to the right geometry, its descriptor is a round-trip fixed point, an inverted range is refused by name) plus a PARTITION check — the three length bands cover every edge of the fixture exactly once — which is what stops a range query agreeing with a broken one on a fixture that happens to be symmetric. The **`Shape` API speaks the vocabulary too**: `Fillet`/`Chamfer`/`ChamferAtAngle`/`FilletEdges`/`ChamferEdges` in constant and variable-law forms take `FaceSetRef`/`EdgeSetRef` bridged through `AsSelector` with the parameter's own name, so a design outside a feature history gets the same readable failure a `Feature` gets; `Shell` is the one deliberate omission because its `openings` parameter is a NULLABLE `Func` and a reference-typed overload would make the existing `Shell(t, null)` ambiguous at every call site. `FeatureContext.TopPlane` is now `PlaneRef.TopPlane` resolved against the context (the hard-coded special case is gone; its world-axis-aligned (0,0,z) origin, which drill coordinates depend on, is unchanged), with `PlaneRef.OnTopFace` as the face-frame variant — so todo.md's open behaviour question became a per-feature OPTION rather than a fork. `HoleFeature.Plane`/`ComponentFeature.Face` keep every incumbent spelling (implicit conversions from `SketchPlane` and from `SketchPlane?`, whose null still means the top plane); the rim features' hard-coded `Direction` became a `FaceSetRef` defaulting to the same query; `CircularPatternFeature`'s loose origin/direction pair became one `AxisRef`. Rim-feature numeric lessons from the feature work: arc corner offsets must be computed exactly from the arc's radial (finite-difference tangents carry ~1e-9 angular error that rotates band generators past weld tolerance at scale — only *some* corners crack, whichever exceed 1e-9), and rim features validate that interior loops (holes) stay clear of the shrunk boundary.). **Design studies** (`DesignStudy.cs`; docs `examples/design-studies.md`; design.md §6b): `DesignStudy.Minimize(part, variables, objective, constraints)` drives `[Param]` values by an optimizer against a MEASURED objective — everything under the loop already existed (`FeatureHistory` regenerates with prefix caching, `[Param(Min=, Max=)]` already declares the box, and any measurement a caller can write over a `Part` is an objective), so **the feature is the loop** and every decision in it is about what the loop may assume. **Derivative-free is FORCED, not preferred**: a parameter change can alter TOPOLOGY (a hole breaks through, a fillet stops fitting), so a finite-difference gradient across such a step is meaningless rather than merely noisy — the two sides are different solids. **Hooke–Jeeves over Nelder–Mead** for three reasons that are about this problem rather than about convergence rates: the box IS the point and a simplex cannot rest on a bound (clamping collapses it) where a compass poll clamps and keeps polling; a refused design is just a poll that does not improve, where a simplex vertex needs a fictitious value and can degenerate; and the step size IS a distance in parameter space, so the stopping rule bounds the ANSWER — `StudyResult.OptimumTolerance` is `2 × StepTolerance` per axis, derived (steps halve together, so the last failed poll ran at `tol < s ≤ 2·tol` and moved the incumbent ±s along each axis with neither better), and returns **infinity** when the search stopped on its evaluation budget because then no claim exists. **Constraints are a feasibility FILTER, not a penalty**: a penalty needs a weight that silently trades a gram against a micrometre, but the deciding argument is that a penalized search RETURNS the minimizer of `f + mu·g`, infeasible for any finite mu — so the study would hand back a beam that fails its own limit and merely scored well; `(violation, objective)` is compared lexicographically with violation relative to each constraint's OWN limit (the only dimensionless ranking needing no weight), and while nothing feasible is known the search descends on violation alone, which is what gets it in from an infeasible start. **What stopped the search is a MEASUREMENT**: a bound is binding when the answer's value IS the bound (exact — the clamp assigns it verbatim), and a constraint when it refused a neighbouring design whose objective was strictly better in the final COMPLETE poll round (which is why the poll is complete rather than opportunistic — an opportunistic one would stop before measuring the directions that say what stopped it). **Three findings.** (1) **A `Shape` graph is LAZY, so a geometric refusal usually does NOT surface at `Regenerate`** — a feature's `Apply` typically only builds a graph node, so the kernel's refusal is raised by the LOWERING, i.e. the first time something measures the part (measured: `FilletRimFeature { Radius = 11 }` on a 30×20 plate regenerates with `Succeeded == true` and throws from `GetMesh`), and a study watching only `Succeeded` would call that design fine — hence two outcomes (`RegenerationFailed`/`MeasurementFailed`) both carrying the kernel's own words, and no forced lowering, since only the caller's measures know which representation they need. (2) **Halving every step together freezes the diagonal poll directions' SLOPE**, and on an active constraint coupling two variables the descent direction runs ALONG the boundary (a beam wants to be deeper AND narrower), so every diagonal is either always too steep or always too shallow: measured, a two-variable beam stopped at depth **21.92** against an analytic ceiling of 25. Searching the RATIO — halve ONE axis's step at a time, making the reachable slopes a dyadic grid that refines as the search does — reaches it (depth exactly 25, width within tolerance of the closed-form 4.876), and it runs ONLY when a constraint refused an improving poll, the only situation the ratio can matter in; both halves are pinned by test through an internal `AdaptStepRatios` seam (the 21.92, and bit-identical trajectories where no constraint holds the answer). The set is still finite, so a boundary whose slope never lands between two reachable ones can still stop the search — OrthoMADS (whose Halton generator is deterministic, so it costs no randomness) is filed rather than guessed at. (3) **A study is an ANALYSIS, not an edit**: it restores the part to the values it started from, because a search evaluates hundreds of designs and none of them is history, and adopting the answer is a deliberate act — `StudyResult.Edits(part)` is one undoable `SetParameters` per driven feature through the SAME JSON seam as `SaveParameters` and MCP `set_param` (the study writes through that seam internally rather than composing `DocumentEdits`, since one edit per feature would rebuild once per feature), and `Part.Regenerate` keeping the previous BODY but not the previous PARAMETER is why every refusal writes the incumbent back and rebuilds before moving on. **Verified against closed forms, not against "it improved"**: the cantilever's minimum-mass depth for a 1 mm tip-deflection limit lands on **15.6201 mm against the analytic 15.6179**, from the FEASIBLE side and inside the optimizer's own criterion, with "tip deflection" named as binding; a second limit lands on its own closed form (which separates agreement from coincidence); a monotone objective rests EXACTLY on the box edge (asserted with `==`) with the bound named; an unreachable limit is `NoFeasibleDesign` by name with the miss quantified; a kernel refusal appears in the trajectory and the search converges onto the refusal's own analytic boundary (`Shape.Drill`'s "centres must be more than one diameter apart" — an M8 clearance bore is 9 mm and the answer is 9.012); and determinism is asserted on the WHOLE trajectory bit for bit rather than on the answer, since two searches can reach one point by different routes and only the routes would show it. Stated v1 boundaries: discrete (`int`) parameters refused by name rather than rounded, no memoization of repeated points (so the trajectory IS the list of evaluations performed, which is what makes the determinism comparison mean what it says), and no `Maximize` (negate the objective, so a report cannot disagree with itself about which way is better). **Configurations / family tables** (`Configurations.cs`; docs `examples/configurations.md`; design.md §6b): **one `FeatureHistory`, N named parameter sets** — a `Configuration` is a NAME plus a `[Param]` value dictionary in exactly the `FeatureHistory.SaveParameters` JSON, so applying one is `LoadParameters` and **the seam is the whole design**: a saved parameter file, MCP `set_param`, `DocumentEdits.SetParameter`, the properties-panel typed editors and a study's `StudyResult.Edits` already write through that one seam so a value cannot mean two things, and a configuration joins them as a CONSUMER rather than becoming a second way to apply one. `Part.Configurations` is non-null exactly when the part has a history; a set may be PARTIAL (an M4…M12 family states the bolt size and says nothing about the plate thickness), `Capture` snapshots the current values and `Add(name, (feature, param, value)…)` states individual ones. **Which pattern a switch wants follows `DesignStudy`'s measurement**: `Activate` is one `LoadParameters` over the whole set plus ONE `Part.Regenerate` (composing it out of per-feature `SetParameter`s would rebuild once per feature), and `DocumentEdits.SetConfiguration` wraps THAT as one undoable edit whose undo restores the model's **live** values rather than the previously active configuration's stored ones — the `MeshChange` complete-journal rule, and the two differ exactly when someone had edited without capturing. It lives on `Part` rather than on `FeatureHistory` because a configuration is only meaningful once something can REBUILD from it and `Part.Regenerate` is the one call that clears every derived cache. **A configuration carries VALUES only** — it cannot add, remove or suppress a feature — which is what makes the switch exact and is also why per-configuration suppression is FILED rather than smuggled into a vocabulary that does not have it. **The bit-identity verification needed a correction and the correction is the finding**: switching away and back does reproduce the geometry vertex for vertex, but NOT by the cached body coming back — the prefix cache holds ONE entry per feature INDEX, overwritten each regeneration (the same property that makes memoizing a design study's repeated points impossible), so the feature whose parameter moved RE-RUNS and returns a fresh structurally identical `Shape`; what the cache buys is the PREFIX (the plate above the change reports `Cached` on every switch, so a switch costs the tail of the history rather than all of it) and what makes the result identical is the contract the cache is BUILT on, `Apply` being a pure function of its parameters — so the test asserts a `Cached`/`Applied` outcome pair AND every vertex through `DoubleToInt64Bits`, where an object-identity assertion would simply have been wrong. **The active configuration is DOCUMENT state**, settled by the undo stack's own reasoning: a stack is session state because it records HOW the document got here, while the active configuration records WHERE IT IS — it names the values the model currently carries, which are saved with the history either way — so dropping it would leave a reloaded document whose values match "M6" exactly, unable to say so. It round-trips (the informational-field rule) and the LOAD restores the NAME **without re-applying**, which is not an optimization: a document may legitimately be saved MODIFIED (active "M6", one parameter since edited) and re-applying would silently snap it back and discard the edit. Activating likewise does NOT write back — an edit leaves the model modified against its configuration (`ActiveIsModified`, an EXACT comparison since both sides come from one serializer, and comparing only the keys the set STATES) and `Capture` is the deliberate act of storing values, which keeps a configuration's values a function of the document rather than of the order in which someone clicked. Staleness follows the `HistoryLoadResult` convention with one deliberate split: a configuration naming a removed feature is a WARNING at `Activate` (`LoadParameters`' own messages, surfaced verbatim) plus a `Validate()` pre-flight a UI can show without applying anything, and it is KEPT rather than dropped (the feature may come back from an undone removal), while the TYPED authoring overload refuses by name because its caller holds the feature OBJECT. Write-only-when-stated in the document envelope, so a file using no configurations is byte-identical to what it always was, with the values riding as a nested OBJECT (diffable, and `Configuration` normalizes whatever text it is handed through the shared JSON options, which is what makes the fixed point trivial). **`Bom.ByConfiguration(part, scene|tab|assembly)` is the family table** and "per configuration" had to be defined for a document whose parts are SHARED: a BOM groups by part REFERENCE and a configuration changes parameters rather than replacing the object, so the configured part is one line in every row and what CAN differ is the rest of the model — the hardware a `ComponentFeature` places, an occurrence a suppression drops — which is why each row is a fresh FLATTEN rather than one captured instance list. Like a study it is an ANALYSIS and restores the part in a `finally`. One trap the API shape had to answer: **`BomLine.UnitMassGrams` is a LAZY projection over the part's CURRENT geometry**, so a mass read off a returned row after the walk has restored the part reports the restored configuration's mass for every row — item names, quantities and paths are captured and safe, the mass is not, so `ConfigurationBom.TotalMassGrams` is measured INSIDE the loop and the caveat is stated on both types rather than left as a footgun (that the BOM's own note already calls mass "the only part of a bill of materials that evaluates geometry" is exactly why it is the only field that could go wrong). **Threads**: `StandardThreads`/`ThreadSpec` (ISO 68-1 basic profile with exact formulas — H = √3/2·P, d2 = d − 3H/4, d1 = d − 5H/4; ISO 261/262 coarse M2–M12 plus the **fine-pitch series** — `Fine(size)` for the first choice (M8×1, M10×1.25, M12×1.5), `Metric(size, pitch)` for second/third choices, `Pitches(size)` listing what a size carries, and `StandardHoles.Tapped(ThreadSpec)` cutting the pilot for any spec; the fine rows store no tap-drill column ON PURPOSE, since every fine tap drill is exactly d − P where the coarse chart rounds to a stock drill (6.8 for M8, not 6.75), and a second column there could drift with nothing to catch it; tap drills consistent with the `Tapped` chart), `Helix3d` (BRep: exact analytic derivatives, closed-form arc length, `Frame3d`-based), `Sdf.Thread` (`ThreadSdf`: solid = 2D trapezoid profile membership in (r, wrap(z − P·θ/2π)) — **sign exact everywhere**, magnitude = exact 2D profile distance scaled by cos(lead angle at root) — lower-bound-style near the surface; clearance = exact profile-normal offset; 45° start/end chamfer cones), `Shape.ExternalThread`/`ThreadedHole(spec, points, depth, plane, clearance)` (implicit-**Native**; mesh via Surface Nets for chamfered/clearance forms — the 3D-printing route with flank-normal clearance, typical FDM 0.1–0.25 mm; **B-Rep-Native for the unmodified profile** — zero clearance, no chamfers — via `HelicalSurface` (co-rotating linear-generator sweep; u NOT periodic since z advances, so the whole wrap-split machinery doesn't apply) + `SpiralArc3d` (exact cap-plane cuts) + `SolidFactory.MakeThreadedRod`: **the entire lateral boundary is ONE boolean-free helical sweep** (root flats are part of the sweep — no coaxial tangent seam ever exists); threaded holes subtract ONE clipped-profile tool (void = max(basic form, r_pilot)) so the only intersecting pairs are helical-band×plane = exact spiral arcs, chain-split via `SplitByClosedCurveChain`; any length works (phase washes out — V = L·(2π/P)·∫½R² ds, Pappus per pitch); **a SUB-DEPTH 45° lead-in chamfer is B-Rep-Native too** — `Shape.ExternalThread(..., chamferLength:)` subtracts `SolidFactory.MakeThreadEndChamferTool`, a coaxial cone whose cut on every band is the exact conical `SpiralArc3d`, giving a Validate-clean two-manifold solid whose volume converges (a 0.5 mm chamfer on an M8×1.25 6 mm rod measures 0.7100 / 0.7889 / 0.8195 / 0.8271 below the plain rod at 32/64/128/256 segments per circle, settling near 0.83) and whose every tessellation vertex reads |sdf| ≤ 1.14e-15 against `Sdf.Thread`'s own chamfered field, so the two representations are one geometry; the `chamferEnds: true` DEFAULT asks for a chamfer of the full thread depth, which puts the cone's base exactly on the minor diameter and tangent to every root band — coincident curved-surface boolean input — so it alone stays B-Rep-Impossible with a named Explain message; **a printing CLEARANCE is Native now too, on both the rod and the tapped hole**, because a clearance is a distance-field offset of the (radius, axial) profile and eroding a thread MITERS its crest corners while ROUNDING its root corners into arcs of the clearance radius — still ONE boolean-free helical sweep, of a generator that mixes straight pieces and circular arcs (`HelicalSurface` takes either; `SolidFactory.OffsetPitchProfile` is the erosion, whose corner rule is the single expression `offset × turn`, so growing the tool that cuts an internal thread is the same code with the opposite sign). The miter-only alternative is refused rather than unexplored: it needs no arcs and is a legitimate convention, and it would make the B-Rep and the field two geometries. `HelicalArcCut3d` is the cut a coaxial straight-profile carrier makes on such a band — the arc twin of `SpiralArc3d` from the same substitution, where a straight generator makes v LINEAR in u and an arc makes the generator ANGLE a shifted arc-cosine of an affine function of it; which acos branch is a property of the arc rather than a parameter, which is exactly why an arc generator whose axial coordinate is not strictly monotone is refused BY NAME (the branches separate precisely when the arc stays inside one half-turn about the carrier's own angle, and a cap plane's is π/2, so "single-branch cap cut" and "z monotone" are one statement). A flat whose offset half-plane goes REDUNDANT is dropped and its neighbours mitered — an M6×1's crest is gone by a clearance of 0.108, well inside the 0.1–0.25 an FDM printer wants, and the eroded thread is correctly a pointed ridge — with the drop allowed only where both its corners miter, which IS the statement that the region is locally convex there. Verified as one geometry rather than by shape: every lateral tessellation vertex reads |sdf| ≤ 2.0e-15 against `Sdf.Thread`'s own clearance field at clearances 0.02–0.25 where the SAME vertices read up to 0.2495 against the uncleared one, and the volume converges quadratically (6.007e-3 / 1.512e-3 / 3.783e-4 / 9.562e-5 at 32/64/128/256, ratios 0.2517/0.2502/0.2528) onto the Pappus integral of its own generator. **Two findings rode along.** `BrepArchive` had been silently FLATTENING a conical `SpiralArc3d` — a chamfered or runout thread reloaded with its cap cuts in the frame plane — because the entity carried four arguments where the general form has six; the axial pair now rides as optional trailing arguments, so every existing archive is byte-identical. And **v is not linear in u on an arc band**, so sampling its cap cut at uniform u (what every other curve wants) and pairing those samples with band-grid rows at uniform v shears every quad against the cap it neighbours: measured 308 folded facets on a 0.05 clearance rod at 16 segments/circle, worst normal agreement −0.366, and a residual that GREW with density (0.230/0.529/0.801 at 32/48/96) rather than converging — sampling at uniform generator ANGLE fixes it (0.365/0.690/0.916), the corpus member at a 0.2 clearance reading 0.457/0.979/0.995 against floors of 0.383/0.924/0.981, with the interior-column shear taking the matching correction (the incumbent lerp IS the exact shear for a straight generator and a chord for an arc, whose sagitta exceeds one column). The alignment-dependent folds that used to appear in the cone's strip zip are **fixed** — they were never loud, and the tie-break the backlog blamed was innocent; the defect was the monotone sweep's turn test reading the exact sign of a cross product that is zero by construction on a constant-v rim (see the round-off lesson below), now clean at all 76 scanned depths with the residual reduced to those cones being coarse rather than folded; **left-hand threads are Native in all three representations** — `ThreadSpec.LeftHanded()` shares every diameter (handedness is not a different thread), the designation becomes `M8×1.25-LH` which `ThreadCallout` picks up for free, `ThreadSdf` spends one sign on it, and `MakeThreadedRod(leftHand:)` takes a signed axial rate: since every formula there is written in the band's own phase u with z = z_generator + rate·u, negating the rate makes u descend as the rod ascends and three things follow mechanically (a rail's helix anchors on the TOP cap — `Helix3d`'s domain always starts at its own frame's plane — the u=min/u=max band edges swap which cap they are, and both cap loops chain the other way), with counts, Euler and the Pappus volume unchanged and the result the EXACT mirror of the right-hand rod (0 residual on band surfaces, 9e-15 at vertices). **`Mirror(thread)` is therefore Native too**: a reflection across a plane containing the axis IS the handedness flip, so writing a mirrored placement as m = (m·FlipY)·FlipY leaves a proper similarity placing a rod of the opposite handedness, and Mirror(Mirror(x)) is right-handed again (FlipY, not the implicit path's FlipZ, which would reverse the rod's own axis and the profile's axial order); the refusal that remains is a genuinely different one — a sheared or non-uniformly scaled placement cannot re-place a helix at all. Verified cross-representation rather than by shape: every vertex of the mirrored B-Rep tessellation reads 2.4e-15 against the mirrored field where the unmirrored field reads up to 0.47, so the check can actually see a handedness or phase slip. **Mirrored threaded HOLES ride the same identity, applied per placed point** (the last holdout, which still refused with the retired "a mirrored thread is left-handed" rationale): the tool's improper placement effective∘flipDown factors as a proper frame times FlipY — concretely, the per-point frame recipe (X, −Y) becomes (X, +Y) with Z still down the transformed normal — and FlipY∘rod is the opposite-handed rod, so the lowering XORs `spec.LeftHand` with the reflection and the per-point placement and depth validation carry through the reflection unchanged (`BottomLiesInFacePlane` reads |n̂·axis|, indifferent to an improper frame). Verified the same way: 20 093 mirrored-tessellation vertices read ≤ 2.31e-15 against the mirrored field where the handedness-slipped construction (the mirror of the LEFT-hand hole, exactly what forgetting the XOR would produce) reads up to 0.4727. Helical lessons: the sheared grid makes cap cuts the grid's first/last columns (boundary points verbatim from shared edge polylines); angular sampling must epsilon-guard Ceiling (ulp-different equal spans must not round to different segment counts); helical surfaces not STEP-exportable (swept bucket). **The analytic family for a helical band is COAXIAL carriers with a straight (radius, axial) profile, and that is one derivation covering four cases**: substituting the band's r = r₀ + dr·v, z = z₀ + dz·v + rate·u into r = a + b·z gives v·(dr − b·dz) = (a + b·z₀ − r₀) + b·rate·u, so **v is LINEAR in u** and the cut is a conical spiral — radius and axial coordinate both linear in the angle. `SpiralArc3d` is now that general shape, and the cap cuts `MakeThreadedRod` already built are its b-in-z = 0 member; a coaxial CONE (a 45° end chamfer) is the general form, a coaxial CYLINDER gives a constant v, i.e. one complete iso-v helix (the runout-diameter case), and parallel profiles (dr = b·dz) never cross transversally and report nothing. Verified at machine precision on BOTH carriers (residual ≤ 1e-12 over 128 samples). **Trimmed helical faces now tessellate**: `IsFullHelicalBand` gates the sheared grid on the face's two spiral cuts being PLANAR, not merely on their being `SpiralArc3d`s — the third occurrence of "a tier's gate should BE its correctness condition", since a chamfer's conical spiral is a `SpiralArc3d` too and the grid interpolates its columns assuming both cuts are the ends of u. Trimmed helical faces take the trimmed path's NON-wrapping tiers, because a helical band's u is not periodic; `NaturalSteps` gives u the circle density and v an INFINITE step (the generator is straight, so `PointAt` is affine in v and a v-chord is exactly on the surface). **Landing the chamfer took four fixes and only ONE was about chamfers.** The recognizer never declined the cone: it declined the tool's coaxial **ANNULUS**, which `TryCoaxialProfileLine` refuses by design (a disk's b is infinite) and whose own doc comment said it was "handled by `PlaneHelical` after the caller synthesizes the plane" — except no caller ever did, so the pair fell to the marching tracer and its polyline ended strictly inside the band, three faces from the cause. A coaxial annulus is now recognized (`TryCoaxialDisk`) and cut as the axis-perpendicular plane it is, clipped to its own radial extent. Second: **a cone's cut on a CONSTANT-radius band is a circle, exactly** — a crest or root flat has dr = 0, so the cut's axial coordinate is the single z where a + b·z = r₀ — but the general v-linear expressions reach it only up to rounding, and `SpiralArc3d.IsPlanar` is an exact-zero test every downstream tier reads, so the SAME chamfer came out planar at one end of a rod and not at the other with nothing geometric between them. Third: **`CurveSegment`'s WRAP is only meaningful on a CLOSED base** — a segment straddling a circle's seam legitimately runs past its domain end, but an OPEN base has nothing on the other side, and the clipping arithmetic that builds these segments overshoots by an ULP routinely, so the wrap did not nudge the sample, it TELEPORTED it to the base's START (measured 0.375 mm off the face, after which the trimmed band tier stopped recognizing a band, the ear clipper ran and folded 244 of 3562 facets — three stages downstream of one ulp, reported as a non-manifold weld). Fourth: **`SampleEdge`'s angular rule must read the turning angle through the curve's own parameter mapping** — a `CurveSegment` reparameterizes to [0, 1] while `Underlying` still points at the spiral, so reading `domain.Length` there measures a segment FRACTION as radians and gave every split spiral edge the same count at any density (11 at segmentsPerCircle 64 and 11 at 256: a density FLOOR), putting two cuts of the same 0.785 rad span at 8 and 11 samples. That last one is the THIRD occurrence of one rule: `Underlying` is a TYPE hint and says nothing about the parameter mapping. **NON-coaxial pairs — a cross-hole or a tilted face — are genuinely transcendental and belong to the marching tracer, and BOTH halves of that are now fixed**: the anisotropic second seed pass finds the branches (see the performance mandate below), and `TryLandOnDomain` TERMINATES them exactly on the band's rails, which is what a face splitter needs. A cross-drilled M8×1.25 rod went from refusing at 13 of 13 bores between 0.6 and 3.0 to Validate-clean closed solids at 8 of them, and a tilted-plane cut from 0 of 4 to 1 of 4. **And a branch stopping at a FOLD rather than at a boundary now traces THROUGH it** (`RetryThroughFold`): where the corrector refuses a step it was not trying to take out of the domain, the curve has turned back within one step and the constraint plane a whole step ahead has no solution near it, so the step is retried at successively halved lengths and then walked back up (the standard continuation rule, so a fold early in a long branch cannot spend its step budget). **The scope is what makes it landable, and the fold identifies itself**: the refused step's own linearization either leaves a bounded domain or it does not, which is exactly the test `TryLandOnDomain` makes first — asked rather than restated, so the retry is only ever reached where the trace previously stopped with nothing appended. Scoping it by the surface pair's ASPECT instead (the gate the second seed pass carries) halves at rail exits too, reaches whole-solid fillet bands and broke seven Interop tests — the recorded revert. Measured on the same sweep: TWO bores stranded a branch inside a face and now ONE does, the fixed one's failure moving DOWNSTREAM to two unpaired edges where a helix rail and its coincident cut segment run between the same two points (a different defect the fold refusal had been hiding), and the remaining one is not a corrector refusal at all — raising the halving budget from 5 to 14 leaves it byte for byte. **Thread RUNOUT is Native in both representations** — `Shape.ExternalThread(..., runoutLength:)` truncates the crests over that length down to the PITCH diameter through `SolidFactory.MakeThreadEndConeTool`, the general member of the family a 45° chamfer belongs to, with `Sdf.Thread` learning the same cone SLOPE so one `ThreadShape` stays one geometry; the pitch diameter rather than the minor is what keeps it exact, since a cone reaching the minor diameter is tangent to every root band along the end plane. And **`ThreadAnnotations.Sites`/`AutoAttach`** give a modelled thread the callout a drawing carries — the spec from the construction graph, the ANCHOR from the geometry, matched on the two numbers a thread IS (major diameter and pitch, read off the `HelicalSurface` bands) rather than by pairing the n-th node with the n-th group of faces, which is free to put a correct-looking M6 callout on an M10 thread; it is also the first callout an EXTERNAL thread has had, a hole table structurally being unable to carry one. **Standard components ("smart" hardware)** (`Modeling/Components/`): a catalogue item is more than geometry — placing it **modifies the host model AND assembles itself**. `HardwareComponent` carries (a) its own parametric `Body`, (b) a seating convention, (c) a **host preparation**; `ComponentAssembly.Place(component, points, face)` cuts the host and records the occurrence in one call. **One local-frame rule makes it composable**: a body is modeled +Z out of the host with its origin at the *seating datum* (the surface it bears on), `SeatDepth` = how far below the host's face that datum sits, `InsertedLength` = how far it reaches below — so a counterbored screw is the *same geometry* as a proud one, just posed deeper (one `Part`, N occurrences). **The preparation is a `Feature`**, which is the whole point: it regenerates, caches and suppresses, so *suppressing a placement removes its bore AND its occurrence*; omit the face and it seats on `FeatureContext.TopPlane` re-resolved per regeneration, so a thickness change re-seats the fasteners and re-cuts their holes (the selector story, not persisted indices). `ComponentSite` resolves depths explicit-first, with `ThroughDepth` = host extent below the face ×1.05 (so a through tool never ends coplanar with the far face, which `Drill` rejects) computed from the context's already-cached lowering. **The full fastener stack works**: `PlaceThrough` prepares BOTH bodies, engagement computed geometrically (grip = seating datum → anchor face; engagement = `InsertedLength` − grip, plus two pitches of tap runout), points *projected* onto the anchor face along the fastener axis, screw placed once. Catalogue = ISO 4762 SHCS / ISO 7380 button (exact spherical-cap dome — the profile carries the arc) / ISO 10642 csk (head diameter DERIVED from the hole table's countersink column via `StandardHoles.CountersunkHeadDiameter`, so screw and hole cannot drift; lengths overall, datum = flush head top) / ISO 4032 hex nut (exact hex extrude + nominal bore; its preparation drills the BOLT's clearance — a nutted joint taps nothing) / ISO 7089 washer (prepares NOTHING, deliberately) / 608-family deep groove bearing (two disjoint concentric rings — radial thirds; placement cuts a flush flat-bottom press-fit pocket) / Tappex Trisert / ISO 2338 m6 dowel; each body is ONE exact axis-touching revolve or extrude+drill (chosen over unions to avoid coplanar/tangent boolean input), no modeled thread, transcribed tables flagged verify-against-datasheet. **The hex socket recess is the assessed exception to the one-revolve doctrine** (`HexSocketRecess`): a hex is not a revolve, so it is a boolean, offered ONLY where exact — a pocket rimmed in a PLANAR face. Three findings scope it to `CapScrew(hexSocket: true)`: a full-turn revolve's flat cap is a `RevolvedSurface` with a POLE (the hex rim would wrap it — machinery the boolean lacks), so the socketed body is REBUILT from cylinder primitives with the shank overlapped INTO the head so every boolean is transverse; a csk head's rebuild makes cone and shank tangent along the shared rim (v1-boolean refusal, filed); a button socket would rim on the dome (traced curve). **Thread providers**: `ProvidesThread`/`CarriesThread`/`MinimumEngagement`/`MaximumEngagement` on `HardwareComponent` let `PlaceThrough(..., anchorInto: placement)` anchor a screw into a PLACED insert or nut — the far body gets no new cut; the overload checks thread match by designation, engagement against the provider's limits (a nut wants its full height, a blind insert bottoms out), and that every fastener point projects onto a provider point at the weld tier (skipped with a documented trust when the provider's face is a per-regeneration semantic reference). `ToPart()` sets `ClippedBySection = false`, so a sectioned assembly shows the housing cut open with its fasteners standing whole, as every drafting standard requires. **Remeshing through the Shape API**: `Shape.Remeshed(targetEdgeLength, iterations)` / `Remeshed(RemeshOptions)` — a **graph node, not a `Part` display option**, and the reasoning is the interesting part: a remesh looks like a rendering setting (only the discretization moves) but that is true only of the *tessellation*, so a remeshed sphere is faithful to the mesh it was projected onto rather than to the sphere. A `Part` flag would hide that behind a rendering knob; a node makes `Explain` state it — **Mesh Native, Implicit Bridged (a mesh SDF of the remeshed triangles, carrying their chord error rather than the child's), B-Rep Impossible** — and lets it compose through export, MCP and the construction tree. Projection defaults to the child's own mesh lowering (without a target, smoothing is curvature flow and the model shrinks); pass `SdfProjectionTarget` over `ToImplicit()` to project onto the exact field instead. **The `Part`-level display remesh filed beside it is DECLINED rather than pending**, and by that same reasoning rather than for want of effort: both use cases it named are already one public line — `new Part(name, Remesher.Remesh(part.GetMesh(q), options).Mesh)` gives display or FEA-export uniformity with the fidelity trade visible at the call site, where a flag on `Part` would have to negotiate `GetMesh`'s first-caller-wins cache and the `Scene` > `EngrCadOptions` > `MeshQuality` precedence ladder to buy exactly that line, and would put the trade back behind the rendering knob this decision exists to keep it out of. A uniform scale above the node scales the target edge length with it (a shear uses the volume-preserving cube root of the determinant). Measured on a Ø20×20 cylinder at a 2 mm target: edges inside the `[0.66L, 1.33L]` band **42% → 95%** — and note the metric, because the **distribution converges fast while the single longest edge lags badly**, sitting near 2L however many passes are spent, so judge a plain remesh by the share in band, never by its extremes. **`RemeshOptions.PreventLongEdgeFlips` is the bounded-maximum mode** (opt-in, so every existing remesh is bit-identical — 0 of 107 docs PNGs moved), and landing it **overturned the recorded diagnosis**: it is NOT that "each collapse can leave a fresh edge of up to 2L for the next pass", and it needed no within-pass re-visit queue (there is no cascade — one split-only round over a converged mesh clears every out-of-band edge and a second finds nothing). The cause is the **FLIP stage**, established by subtraction: switch flips off and nothing else and the same run ends at *exactly* 1.33L with nothing out of band, because the sweep already splits everything too long, while switching the smoothing and projection stages off instead leaves the maximum unmoved at 2.07L. The flip predicate is pure valence arithmetic that never looks at a length, so on an elongated quad it swaps the short diagonal for the long one and manufactures exactly the edge the split stage exists to remove. Docs: `docs/examples/remeshing.md`. **`Shape.Smoothed(step, passes)` is the fairing sibling** (`SmoothedShape` → `LaplacianMeshSmoother`), a geometry-changing graph node under the same contract as `Remeshed` — mesh-Native, implicit-Bridged (a mesh SDF of the FAIRED triangles, carrying their chord error), B-Rep-Impossible, with `Explain` and `MeshRoute` saying so — and the honesty stated rather than hidden: a closed solid has NO boundary to pin, so the whole surface fairs (curvature flow that rounds corners and shrinks the body a little each pass). The `step` is dimensionless/scale-free (λ = step·h̄²), and because the node bakes the accumulated transform into the child before fairing, "fair then scale" and "scale then fair" are the SAME geometry, not merely similar (asserted). Docs `remeshing.md` §Fairing. **Modeled text** (`Modeling/Text/`): a hand-rolled dependency-free TrueType reader (`head`/`maxp`/`cmap` 4+12/`loca`/`glyf` simple **and** composite/`hhea`/`hmtx` + optional `kern` 0/`name`/`OS/2`; `.ttc` and composite point-matching rejected loudly; **OpenType/CFF `.otf` fonts now load too** — `CffOutlines` parses Type 2 charstrings hand-rolled (INDEX/DICT, local+global subrs with the count-dependent bias, the whole curve family incl. flex, CID via FDArray/FDSelect), contours carry `GlyphContour.IsCubic` → `BezierTo`, and the CFF decoding trap is that hintmask/cntrmask carry one data byte per eight stems INCLUDING implicit vstems from stack arguments — pinned by exact decoded coordinates in a synthetic CFF font, seac/CFF2/arithmetic operators rejected by name; **GPOS pair kerning** — `GposKerning` reads the `kern` features' PairPos 1+2 through Extension lookups with both coverage and class-def formats, first-match-per-lookup accumulating across lookups, and per the spec a GPOS kern feature makes the legacy `kern` table invisible rather than merging) feeding `Shape.Text(text, font, size, height, plane?, style?)`. Glyph contours become `Sketch` segments **exactly** — `glyf` outlines are lines + quadratic Béziers, CFF outlines lines + cubics, and the sketch builder has `LineTo`/`QuadraticTo`/`BezierTo`, so nothing is flattened and text is Native in all three reps. Size = em size (`font.EmSizeForCapHeight(h)` converts a drawing's letter height); origin = the first line's baseline; layout does advance widths, `kern` pair kerning, tracking, multi-line and per-line alignment; a missing character throws rather than silently dropping. **Vertical alignment** (`TextStyle.VerticalAlign`) moves the whole block off that baseline, measured from the FONT's ascender and descender rather than from the ink — so two labels centred on one point line up whether or not either contains a descender or a capital, which is exactly what an ink-measured centring gets wrong; `Baseline` adds nothing at all (an exact-zero semantic test, not a zero addend), so every existing layout stays bit-identical down to the sign of a zero. **Text on a curve** (`Shape.TextOnPath`/`TextOutlines.SketchesOnPath`, `GlyphPose`): one line laid along any `Curve2d` — a dial's rim, a bezel. **Glyphs are placed RIGIDLY, not bent, and that is a correctness decision rather than a simplification**: only the CONTROL POINTS are mapped, which IS the mapped curve because a Bézier is an affine combination of them at every parameter (the property that also makes `TransformedCurve(NurbsCurve)` a lossless STEP export), whereas a warp following the curve's curvature is not affine and has no exact Bézier image — so bending would mean flattening, and text on a path would stop being Native in all three representations. The oracle is therefore an AREA identity, not a picture: a rotation preserves area exactly, so a curved glyph must enclose exactly what the upright one encloses, an assertion a distortion cannot pass where "the letters look right" passes either. Pen positions are ARC LENGTHS (`ArcLengthTable2d`), so spacing is what the font asked for whatever the parameterization, and a glyph anchors at the MIDDLE of its advance (SVG's rule) so it leans about its own centre instead of pivoting off its left edge. **A glyph's "up" is the path's LEFT normal** — the only choice making a straight left-to-right path reproduce ordinary layout exactly — and the consequence is worth stating because it inverts the naive expectation: a COUNTER-CLOCKWISE circle's left normal points at its centre, so lettering hangs inward and a dial's outward-standing rim text wants a CLOCKWISE path; both windings are pinned by test, since "which side does the text go" is precisely the convention a one-sided test lets drift (the first docs render caught the same thing in the documentation rather than the code — the prose claimed outward and the geometry was right). A closed path is a ring so a run may cross its seam; an open one may not run off either end (extrapolating a curve past its own domain is inventing geometry) and text longer than the path is refused with both lengths named, the fit test carrying the weld tier so a run that exactly fills its path is not refused by an ulp. Multi-line on a path is refused BY NAME — a second line sits on an OFFSET of the path, a different curve that can self-intersect, so the caller builds it rather than the layout inventing one. Types live flat in `EngrCAD.Modeling` because `Shape.Text` takes a `TrueTypeFont` — a sub-namespace would cost every call site a second `using`. Two lessons worth keeping: **the implied on-curve midpoint between consecutive off-curve points is THE TrueType subtlety** — pin it by asserting the region's signed distance at that point is exactly zero, never by eyeballing a rendered shape; and **never trust a font's contour orientation** — the spec says outer CW / counters CCW but real fonts violate it, so counters must be nested by containment (an exact point-in-region vote), which is why 'O' and '8' come out right. **Text follow-ups**: `TextFeature(text, font)` (`StandardFeatures.cs`) is modeled text as a parametric `Feature` — `[Param]` Size/Height/LetterSpacing/Engrave/Plane driven through the SAME JSON seam configurations/studies/the properties panel use — the text and font riding as CONSTRUCTOR inputs so a fresh instance re-runs and the regeneration cache covers the font WITHOUT the snapshot naming it (the sketch/hole-spec rule, which is why the item's "the snapshot must cover the font" concern needs no new machinery), emboss/engrave overshooting the surface (the Drill doctrine) so the boolean stays transversal, and opaque to whole-history persistence BY NAME since a font is a binary blob with no data form (`SaveInputs` returns null — the non-catalogue `ComponentFeature` rule). `Shape.TextOnPath`/`TextOutlines.SketchesOnPath` gained an `upright:` argument — glyphs translated to their path point and left un-rotated (the banner/label placement, so an argument rather than a `TextStyle` field), still mid-advance-anchored and arc-length-spaced so a straight horizontal path reproduces ordinary layout bit-for-bit, with `VerticalAlign` lifting along world +Y (the glyph's own up) rather than the path normal. **Heightmap colour PNGs**: `Heightmap.ReadPng`/`PngGrayReader` learned truecolor (PNG color types 2/6, alpha ignored) mapped to height via **Rec. 709 relative luminance** `0.2126 R + 0.7152 G + 0.0722 B` — a documented DECISION, not an inferred rule (a grey pixel reads its own value since the weights sum to 1) — and now VERIFIES CRC-32/ISO-HDLC on CRITICAL chunks (the bytes that become heights) so a corrupt IHDR/IDAT is named rather than inflated into nonsense, ancillary chunks exempt per the spec; palette (color type 3) still refused by name. **Assemblies v1**: `Assembly` = named `Occurrence` list (each references a shared `Part` OR nested `Assembly` + a mutable `Frame3d` pose; DAG with cycle rejection at Add; names auto-suffix per level, `/` reserved for paths); **`PartInstance(Part, World, Path)` flattening is the seam** (`Assembly.Flatten()`/`Tab.Instances()`/`Scene.AllInstances`, World = frame chain × part transform, paths like "stack/clamp.2/bolt") — viewers/exporters only see instances; `Tab` holds assemblies beside loose parts; `Scene.AllParts` dedupes by reference so `PreMesh` meshes each distinct part ONCE (N instances share one mesh + GPU buffer set + pick BVH, drawn per-instance with composed matrices; `ViewportControl.SetInstances` is the host API, `SetParts` wraps it); viewer tree shows the hierarchy indented-flat with subtree visibility (effective = own AND ancestors), occurrence-path selection, and **assembly expand/collapse** (disclosure triangle, default expanded, state per assembly path; pure UI — collapsed rows are still built and registered so viewport visibility and instance indices never shift); **palette colors retro-assign on `Tab.Instances()`** for parts added to an assembly after `Tab.Add` (stability rule: `??=` plus a forward-only cursor — a color never changes once assigned, latecomers take the next entries in display order); OBJ/STL/`--render` flatten instances (STEP still exports distinct parts un-posed). **Assemblies v2**: **BOM** (`Bom.cs` — counts occurrences per distinct `Part` over `Flatten()`, so nested sub-assemblies roll up with no second traversal; `BomLine(Part, Quantity, Paths)` with `Item`/`Hardware` from the new **`Part.Hardware`** that `HardwareComponent.ToPart` sets, so `bom.Hardware`/`Manufactured` split bought-in from made; `Bom.Structured` is the indented per-level view whose leaf totals agree with the flat list by construction; text/CSV/`ByItem()` rollup; a viewer **BOM** button shows the table and drops a CSV. Lines group by part REFERENCE, not name — two separately-built parts sharing a designation stay two lines and `ByItem()` rolls them up, documented rather than silently merged). **Materials, and ONE unit convention** (`Material`/`Materials`/`ModelUnits`/`PartColor` moved down into `EngrCAD.Core`; `Part.Material` + `.Of(material)`; docs `examples/materials.md`): a part can say what it is made of, and it is the SAME `Material` the FEA solvers take — the density a BOM weighs a part with is the density a structural solve integrates. **The substance was the unit decision, not the type move.** Two conventions had shipped a **1000× apart** — `Materials.Steel.Density` = 7.85e-9 (tonne/mm³) against `PartMassProperties`' documented 7.85e-6 (kg/mm³) — and **neither figure is wrong on its own**, which is exactly why nothing could catch a caller mixing them and why a second catalogue in Modeling would have baked the disagreement in. The choice is settled by asking WHERE a conversion can live: **a density is either a number an equation consumes or one a report prints, and only the second can be converted afterwards.** An FEA mass matrix must balance against a stiffness in MPa and a length in mm with no slot for a factor — and the factor would have to reappear in the gravity load, the heat capacity, the frequency, the buckling load and the transient time constant, every one verified against a closed form — whereas mass properties form exactly ONE product from the density. So **the convention lives where it cannot be converted (tonne/mm³, mm/N/MPa/tonne/s, stated once in `ModelUnits`) and kilograms/grams are ACCESSORS** (`ModelUnits.MassToGrams`/`MassToKilograms`, `Material.DensityKilogramsPerCubicMetre`), which is not the same thing as two conventions. Two riders: catalogue densities are stored in tonne/mm³ but **asserted in kg/m³**, since the datasheet figure is the only form a human can check (7.85e-9 vs 7.85e-9 verifies typing, not physics); and `MeshMassProperties`/`BrepMassProperties` stay deliberately unit-AGNOSTIC, since the convention belongs to the layers that own materials, not to the integrators underneath. `Material` moved to Core because it is the one type Modeling and Fea both need and Core is their only common ancestor (namespace moved too — a modelling user must not need a simulation `using` to say what a part is made of); **the analysis properties became OPTIONAL with zero = "not stated"** (the convention the thermal fields already used), so a document material — a name, a density, perhaps a colour — is constructible where the old constructor refused `youngsModulus <= 0`; and **the hard refusal moved to the point of use**, where the analysis can name what is missing (`StructuralModel`'s ctor and `SetMaterial` on the modulus, `ThermalSolver` on conductivity/capacity, `ModalSolver` on density). The modulus refusal earns its place: without a modulus Lame's parameters are both ZERO, so the stiffness would be identically zero rather than merely wrong and the solve would report rigid-body modes for a model that has none. **The FEA verification suite was the regression oracle and every figure held** — cantilever tip 1.90494 mm (+0.01% Euler–Bernoulli, −0.76% Timoshenko), Kirsch K_tn 2.4216 (−0.44%), manufactured-solution orders 2.00/1.00 linear and 3.03/2.02 quadratic (thermal 2.01/1.00 and 3.05/2.02), modal bar 25 866.46 Hz (0.021% of exact) and cantilever bending 834.92 Hz (−0.07%), Euler column 15 438.52 N (−0.68% Euler, −0.19% Engesser), time orders 1.05/2.00, erfc 0.1838 K on an 80 K step — not one moved, which is what proves no unit slipped. Modeling's side is additive: `Part.MassProperties(density?)` takes the density as an OVERRIDE now (null reads the material, falling back to 1 so mass reads as volume — the honest answer, not a silent zero), so **`scene.AllInstances.MassProperties()` is the promised one-liner**; `Part.MassGrams()` is the exact route and `DisplayMassGrams()` the display-mesh one the properties panel and MCP `describe_part` read (so a readout can never lower a B-Rep on the UI thread and always agrees with the Volume beside it); `BomLine.Material`/`UnitMassGrams`/`TotalMassGrams` with a MATERIAL column that appears only when a line states one and **opt-in** mass columns (the only part of a BOM that evaluates geometry; an unstated material prints `-` and writes an EMPTY CSV cell, never a zero a spreadsheet would sum, and the footer total names how many items it covers); `DocumentEdits.SetMaterial` on the undo stack (leaving the colour alone — a material only ever supplied the DEFAULT at add time); and write-only-when-stated document persistence, so a scene using no materials saves byte-identically. A material's colour is the part's default and **does not consume a palette slot**, and **no catalogue entry carries a colour at all** (appearance is a finish, not a property of the stuff) — which is why assigning `Materials.Steel` to a part moves no pixels and all 104 committed docs PNGs stayed byte-identical. **Material follow-ups: catalogue hardware, per-region analysis, an editor, one unit statement.** (1) **`HardwareComponent.Material`** (`FastenerMaterials` in Modeling; `ToPart()` sets `Part.Material`) makes a BOM of bought-in parts weigh itself — an ISO 7089 M6 washer comes back at the analytic annulus mass 1.01645 g with nothing stated in the design. **The field carries the STUFF, not the strength grade**: ISO 898-1 property classes 8.8/10.9/12.9 are proof and tensile stresses, all three steel at 7850 kg/m³, so an M6×20 weighs the same whichever it is and the class lives in the designation — what moves a mass is a change of SUBSTANCE (stainless A2/A4 ~2% heavier, brass ~8% again), asserted as `CarbonSteel.Density == AlloySteel.Density` bit for bit. Not a second catalogue either: where `Materials` states the alloy the fastener entry DELEGATES and only renames (`StainlessA2` IS `StainlessSteel304`, `StainlessA4` the newly added `StainlessSteel316`, `Brass` verbatim), since two spellings of one density is the discrepancy the consolidation removed. **The bearing states NOTHING, deliberately** — its v1 body is two rings with the balls and cage missing, so density × volume is a confidently light number, and the BOM's own rule (an unknown mass is an empty cell, never a zero a spreadsheet sums) makes an honest "unknown" the better answer; `bearing.ToPart().Of(FastenerMaterials.BearingSteel)` takes the lower bound anyway, and is also how a design states a stainless variant of any entry (one cached part per component, so one assignment covers every occurrence). Transcription note: **ISO 2338 is the UNhardened parallel pin** (ISO 8734 is the hardened ground dowel) — easy to assume the other way round, and it happens not to change the density. No catalogue material carries a colour and `ToPart` already sets one explicitly, so nothing moved a pixel. (2) **`AnalysisBody`** (Fea: a closed surface + its `Material` + a name) is the seam the document model and the simulation layer share: `TetMesher.Mesh(bodies)` and `StructuralModel.For`/`ThermalModel.For` read the SAME list, so region ids are never restated and the materials cannot drift from the geometry — `parts.Select(p => new AnalysisBody(p.GetMesh(), p.Material, p.Name))` is the whole bridge, a LIST rather than a call on `Part` because Fea is a leaf that cannot see one and what the layers genuinely share is `Material` (which is why it lives in Core) and `HalfEdgeMesh`. Verified EXACTLY rather than by convergence: with **Poisson's ratio zero in both halves** a two-material bar's series stiffness δ = (F/A)(L₁/E₁ + L₂/E₂) is a piecewise-linear field IN the linear-tet space, so a solve reproduces 3.857142857e-3 mm to round-off at every density (the thermal twin likewise: q = ΔT/(L₁/k₁ + L₂/k₂) = 200 mW/mm² with the interface at exactly 80 K). A nonzero nu would put a boundary layer at the interface — the halves want to contract laterally by different amounts — and leave no closed form to check against. **The assertion with teeth is the INTERFACE value, not the total**: swapping the materials leaves the tip deflection and the through-flux unchanged (series resistances commute), so a test asserting only the total agrees just as happily with the regions the wrong way round. **v1 meshes DISJOINT bodies and now refuses MATING ones by name**, replacing a Delaunay-depth "points N and M are exactly coincident — weld the input surface" that was true and unhelpful: welding WOULD mesh, and the result would look right and be wrong, because `OffendingFaces` treats every inside-to-inside face as interior, so an inter-body face is never recovered onto the input plane and an element straddling it takes ONE region for its whole volume — the material boundary would be a jagged surface of the mesher's choosing rather than the plane the design drew. Conforming interfaces need the inter-body face treated as a constrained boundary plus a decision about whether a facet selector may name it (it is visited from both sides, so a pressure there would double-count); filed as a feature, not a weld. (3) A **material dropdown in the properties panel** — rows from the pure `ParamEditors.MaterialChoices` in Viewer.Core ("(none)" first, then `Materials.All`, then the part's OWN material when the catalogue lacks it, since a control that cannot show the current value reads as "not set" and one idle click would discard it) — writing through `DocumentEdits.SetMaterial`, so it is one Ctrl+Z step and never a second way to apply a value. Two placements carry reasons: it sits ABOVE the panel's `HasMesh` gate (a document property must not wait for a part to tessellate) while the Mass row stays below it (that one is a measurement), and it runs `RunEdit(..., republish: false)` because a material moves no geometry — re-running the tab's mesh loader would be wasted work AND would clear the selection out from under the control just used; undo and redo still republish, deliberately, since the stack does not know what it is taking back. (4) **One unit statement, cross-referenced**: `docs/examples/fea-*.md` and `EngrCAD.Fea/README.md` stopped restating the mm/N/MPa/tonne/s ladder and now point at `ModelUnits` (thermal keeps only its DERIVED table — film coefficient, flux, generation, diffusivity — which the base statement does not carry), and `BrepMassPropertiesTests`' two `kg/mm³` comments now SAY that `BrepMassProperties` is deliberately unit-agnostic and the density pair is the caller's, rather than reading as a stale convention. **Exploded views** (`Occurrence.ExplodeOffset` = the displacement at factor 1 in the PARENT's coordinates; `Flatten(factor)`/`Tab.Instances(factor)`/`Scene.Instances(factor)` compose it, so nesting works and offscreen renders get it free; factor 0 returns the frame untouched, so an un-exploded flatten is bit-for-bit unchanged. **The instance count and order are independent of the factor** — that is what lets `ViewportControl.SetInstancePoses` animate with matrices only, no GPU buffer touched, and picking follows because `HitTest` already reads the per-instance model matrix. `AutoExplode`'s heuristic carries the lesson: **the largest non-catalogue occurrence is the DATUM and does not move** — deriving the direction from the *assembly centroid* is wrong whenever parts are spread out, because the centroid sits in the empty middle and every part, base included, flies away from nothing (measured: a base plate moved 9.5 of a possible 10 units); hardware backs out along its own frame Z, which the component local-frame convention already knows; everything else is radial from the datum, scaled by how far out it sits so a stack keeps its order. **Dogleg paths**: `Occurrence.ExplodePath` puts waypoints between the assembled position and the offset (empty = the straight line, so nothing changes for an assembly that never sets one) — a screw comes straight OUT of its bore before it moves aside, because a diagonal reads as "insert it at an angle" and a fitter will try; the factor maps to **arc length** along the polyline, not to segment index, so the part crosses the corner at constant speed, and `Occurrence.ExplodeDisplacement(factor)` is the ONE rule the flatten walk, `ExplodeTrack` and a future leader-line renderer all read (exact zero at 0 and exactly the offset at 1 by DECISION, not by arithmetic that lands there). Paths round-trip through the document format and are written only when set, so existing files stay byte-identical. Viewer toggle + slider, `--explode`, `WithExplode`, `RenderToImage(explode:)`). **Mates** (`Mates.cs`/`MateSolver.cs`: `Coincident`/`Planar(gap)`/`Concentric`/`Distance`/`Parallel`/`Perpendicular`/`Angle` + `Ground`, refs from explicit local coordinates or **semantic `BrepQueries` selectors resolved ONCE at construction**; Levenberg–Marquardt with an **analytic** Jacobian — finite differences would cap accuracy near 1e-8, an order worse than the weld tier — angular residuals scaled by the assembly's characteristic length so every residual is a length and one linear tolerance is meaningful, rotation variables divided by it so every column is O(1), DOF from a **diagonally pivoted Cholesky of JᵀJ** (rank-revealing for PSD, which is what sees through the deliberately redundant rotational encodings: 3 rows carrying 2 constraints). **It refuses loudly**: a non-converged solve writes NOTHING and names the mates carrying the residual; remaining DOF is always reported (`RequireFullyConstrained` makes it fatal). Verified at residual ≤ 1e-9: coincident, coincident-to-world, planar with/without gap, concentric, **concentric+planar (5 DOF, spin free)**, distance, parallel, perpendicular, angle, 3-occurrence chains, sub-assembly-as-rigid-body, and 0.01×/1×/1000× scale. **One stationary-configuration lesson**: an `Angle` or `Perpendicular` mate whose directions start exactly parallel has NO first-order step (d/dθ cos θ = 0 at θ = 0), so the solver detects and names it rather than nudging at random and sometimes converging. **Mates now reach ACROSS assembly levels**: every `MateGeometry` builder has an `(Assembly, path, …)` overload — an occurrence path ("carrier/bolt", `Assembly.ResolvePath`) names a unique *placement*, the deep occurrence's local frame becomes the unknown, and the chain rule through the frame chain is the one-level formulas with the rotation moment arm taken about each free link's composed WORLD origin (variables are world-space rigid perturbations, which compose as Δ_ancestor∘Δ_target∘W — so no derivative-matrix products; `Apply` pulls the moved world frame back through the PRE-step ancestor frame, snapshotted before any pose writes). Untouched sub-assemblies stay rigid for free; one-level solves stay bit-identical (dedicated arithmetic, no identity composition); a deep target whose owning sub-assembly is placed more than once is refused naming the placements (its frame is ONE shared object — flexible sub-assemblies are the follow-up); `Ground(path)` pins deep occurrences; `MateSolveResult.OccurrenceFreedoms` reports per-occurrence block rank (honestly labeled an upper bound — shared mates pin relative motion). **Typed mate references + persistence**: `MateGeometry.PlanarFace/CylindricalFace/Axis` take `FaceRef`/`AxisRef` (the GeometryRefs vocabulary, still resolved ONCE at construction — the eager choice made explicit), and `MateSet.SaveMates`/`LoadMates` follow the FeatureHistory conventions (query descriptors round-trip and re-resolve eagerly at load; failed queries fall back to pinned coordinates with a warning; missing occurrences skip the mate by name; opaque lambdas save a marker and warn; saved JSON is a fixed point under save→load→save, which is why `MateRef` keeps an already-unit direction verbatim — the AxisRef ulp rule)). **Mechanisms** (`Joints.cs`/`Mechanism.cs`/`Couplings.cs`/`HigherPairs.cs`/`MateSolverRates.cs`/`MotionInterference.cs`; docs `examples/mechanisms.md`): **a mechanism is the same mate system with DOF > 0, driven** — no second solver.
