Joints (`Joint.Revolute/Prismatic/Cylindrical/Spherical/Planar/Screw/Fixed`) are NAMED mate combinations from the same `MateRef`s, each nominal DOF **asserted against the solver's measured rank at Add** (a wrong definition fails by name); axis joints carry unwrapped coordinates (`Angle`/`Displacement`, zero at construction — unwrap advances only when a CONVERGED solve commits, so the residual stays continuous within one solve) and `WithLimits` stops (violation = full frame rollback naming the joint; a sweep walks onto the stop and reports it). The solver gained one internal seam: `AuxiliaryConstraint` rows (analytic derivatives over evaluated end geometry) beside the mates — screw pitch, gear/belt ratios (Δθ₂ = ∓ratio·Δθ₁ on the change since construction), cams (`CamLaw` carries lift+slope+curvature; `FromSketch` bisects the sketch's exact signed distance into a C² periodic spline), and drivers, whose angle form is the wrap-free pair [c − cos τ, s − sin τ] because a θ̂ − τ row would jump 2π mid-iteration at the seam and stall LM. `Sweep` = continuation (seed from the PREVIOUS converged pose, never the assembled one — the four-bar elbow-flip lesson; halving retries are safe because a failed solve writes nothing); a stall runs a zero-iteration rank probe at 3% threshold vs the sweep-start baseline — a dead centre names the driven joint + parameter and refuses to guess a branch, an unreachable target says so, and the SAME pose driven from another joint passes (the singularity belongs to the driven variable, not the pose; verified all three ways on one slider-crank). `RatesAt` solves J·q̇ = −∂C/∂t then J·q̈ = −r̈₀ with second-order terms ANALYTIC (composed rigid flow: centripetal per free link + 2·ω_outer×(v+ω×r) Coriolis cross terms between chain levels), matching the slider-crank closed form in velocity AND acceleration; under-constrained rates are refused (a family is not an answer). `CheckInterference` = bounds broad phase + `MeshIntersection.Crosses` narrow phase (transversal only — resting contact is not a clash) with jointed pairs skipped by default (tessellated pins interpenetrate their bores) and mesh-boolean volumes opt-in per confirmed range; `Shape.SweptOver`/`MotionStudy.SweptVolume` is a graph node, implicit-Native (child field lowered ONCE, placed per pose), B-Rep honestly Impossible. **Several drivers at once**: `SolveAt`/`Sweep`/`RatesAt` take lists (`DriverTarget`/`DriverRange`/`DriverMotion`) — a 2-DOF mechanism has no answer under one driver, so the multi form IS the implementation and the single-driver overload is sugar over it; the same joint VARIABLE driven twice is refused by name (two rows demanding different values of one coordinate make the system inconsistent, and LM would report a residual rather than the modelling mistake) while two drivers on ONE joint driving different variables is the case it exists for; a multi-driver sweep is a STRAIGHT LINE through driver space rather than a grid, so there is still one parameter to halve and the continuation/dead-centre/last-good-pose machinery is unchanged; `MotionFrame.Values` carries every driver's value with `Value` and `FailedAt` staying the first driver's. `Coupling.RackAndPinion` is Δz = r·Δθ built as a cam pair with a straight law (`CamLaw.Linear`) rather than a fourth constraint class — the cam coupling already ties a slide to an UNWRAPPED spin through a law's exact slope and curvature, so a rack driven through three turns keeps advancing instead of resetting at every seam. The **dwell-rise-dwell catalogue** (`CamLaw.Cycloidal`/`HarmonicRise`/`ModifiedTrapezoid`/`Dwell`/`Linear` chained by `CamLaw.Segments`) is engineering rather than mathematics: the members differ in what happens where a rise meets a dwell (cycloidal and modified trapezoid end at zero acceleration and join C2; harmonic steps — the classic cam-noise source — and buys the lowest peak velocity), and the peak acceleration factors 2π / π²/2 / **8π/(2+π) = 4.8881** are asserted because ~22% under the cycloidal is the whole reason the compromise exists (the constant DERIVED by integrating the five-piece profile twice and requiring h(1) = 1, not transcribed); a rise **clamps outside its own span**, which is what lets `Segments` chain laws while continuity across a joint stays the segments' business. **Roller and offset followers landed with NO offset curve and NO root find** (`CamFollower` + `CamLaw.FromSketch(profile, follower)` + `CamLaw.PressureAngle`): the filed plan was parametric (build the offset curve q(θ), solve arg q(θ) = ψ per query with implicit-function derivatives) and dissolves against machinery `FromSketch` already stands on — the sketch's signed distance is a TRUE planar distance outside the profile, so the roller-centre curve IS the isolevel sd = R and the follower position is the outermost crossing of that isolevel along its travel line, the same march and bisection as the point follower (the isolevel-0 member, bit-identical). The radial shortcut r(θ)+R the entry warned about misses by 0.12 on the eccentric-circle fixture at θ = π/2 and the test asserts the shortcut DOES miss, or the fixture proves nothing. The pressure angle — the number an offset exists to improve — is tan φ = (slope − offset)/distance from the instant-centre construction, with ONE sign convention on `CamFollower` (offset positive RIGHT of travel, cam CCW: the textbook "positive offset reduces the rise-side pressure angle" holds) shared by placement and analysis, and verified against an independent oracle: the contact normal of a roller on a circle passes through both centres (cos φ = |t̂·(p−c)|/(a+R)), which is what pins the two conventions as consistent. `SweptVolume(path, maxTravel)` makes the sampling **adaptive** — rigidly interpolated placements until no point of the part moves further than a stated length, travel measured EXACTLY as the largest bounding-box-corner displacement rather than a rotation angle times an assumed radius (97%+ of the analytic disk from a 9-frame full-turn sweep at maxTravel 0.5; recorded frames all kept so refining can only add material) — over the new shared `MotionStudy.InterpolatePose`, which `MechanismTrack` delegates to so there is one answer to where a body was halfway between frames. `Mobility()` reports Grübler/Kutzbach BESIDE the measured rank, disagreement informative (the planar four-bar in space predicts −2, rank measures 1 — and the four-bar doubles as the redundant-consistent stress case: 20 constraint rows, rank 17, LM converges through the redundancy for a full crank cycle within 1e-6 of the elbow-up closed form). **Mechanism persistence** (`MechanismPersistence.cs`: `SaveMechanism`/`LoadMechanism(json, resolveOpaqueLaw?)`) round-trips the layer a `MateSet` file loses — grounds, raw mates, joints, couplings — under the MatePersistence conventions (warnings never exceptions; save→load→save byte-identical). Joint MATES are derived, never restated (a second copy could drift from the joints that spell them); what cannot be re-derived rides as data — the axis joints' perpendicular reference directions (re-deriving at load would move the angle's zero; the `MateRef` ctor's keep-already-unit-verbatim rule makes them bit-exact) and the sweep state (`AccumulatedAngle` is a HISTORY: a crank saved at 4π reloads at 4π and keeps counting). Loading re-ADDS each joint, re-asserting nominal DOF against measured rank, so a file valid when written can fail as a WARNING naming the joint (skipped, with its couplings skipped by saved index — indices never shift under a skipped neighbour). Couplings save their FACTORY (a gear as gear teeth, a rack-and-pinion as itself, not the straight-law cam it is built as) and deliberately NOT their construction zeros — each constrains the CHANGE since construction, so for any pose satisfying it (a saved converged pose does) re-zeroing at load is exactly the same constraint. Cam laws follow `Feature.SaveInputs`: catalogue laws save kind+args and re-run the factory, `FromSketch` saves its sampled lifts (the law IS the samples; the spline rebuild is deterministic, so the loaded law evaluates bit-identically), `FromFunction` lambdas save an `opaque` marker that loads as a warning unless the hook supplies the instance. **Involute gear geometry** (`Gears.cs`/`Gears.Rack.cs`/`Gears.Worm.cs`; docs in `examples/mechanisms.md`): `GearSpec` (module/teeth/pressure angle/profile shift + ISO 53 profile A rack coefficients 1.00/1.25/0.38 as overridable inits, verify-against-datasheet; derived properties state the base-circle identities as arithmetic — base pitch π·m·cos α, undercut limit 2(h_a* − x)/sin²α) + `Gears.Spur` → `GearProfile` (a `Sketch` of lines and arcs, so gears are exact in all three representations) + `SpurGear`/`HelicalGear` solids (helical = the twisted extrude with the spur profile as the TRANSVERSE section, mesh/implicit only per that contract). The flank enters the sketch vocabulary as a **biarc chain against the closed-form involute with the deviation REPORTED** (`MaxFitDeviation`; recursive bisection with EXACT endpoint tangents — the involute tangent at roll t is the base-radial at θ₀+t; default tolerance m·1e-4 costs ~16 arcs/flank, so the fit tier sufficed and no new 2D curve type was needed). The **root fillet tangency is CLOSED FORM** — |C|² = (r_b·t + ρ)² + r_b² makes |C| = r_f + ρ solvable as t* = (√((r_f+ρ)² − r_b²) − ρ)/r_b — falling to a radial stretch below the base circle that is tangent-continuous because the involute's CUSP tangent is radial; refusals by name (rack undercut limit — an honest refusal beats an unverified trochoid — pointed teeth as a LENGTH at the weld tier, fillets that overlap the root gap or consume the flank). **Conjugate action is verified from CONTACT, deliberately not via the mechanism solver** (`Coupling.Gear` ENFORCES the ratio it would be asserting): two generated gears at an EXTENDED centre distance (backlash makes drive-flank contact a transversal zero, and the involute's ratio is centre-distance-invariant) rotated into contact by bisecting the pinion sketch's exact signed distance over the wheel outline — ratio constant to 9.3e-6 rad through tooth handover, and the instrument mutation-checked: a 25° wheel against a 20° pinion reads 5.6e-3 rad, a 5e-2-tolerance flank 5.6e-4 ≈ deviation/r_b, so it sees flank FORM, not just ratio. `GearProfile.ClosedFormArea` (involute Green's term r_b²·t³/6) is held against `Sketch.Area()`. **The rack landed as the DEFINITION rather than as one more member** (`Gears.Rack`/`RackBar`, `RackSpec`/`RackProfile`): as z→∞ the base circle recedes and the involute flattens into a LINE at the pressure angle, which is why ISO 53 defines the tooth system by a basic RACK — so this is the one member with no fit tier at all (straight `Line2d` flanks, exact `Arc2d` root fillets) and `RackProfile` deliberately carries **no** `MaxFitDeviation` for there to be, which flows into the assertions rather than only the prose: pitch-line thickness and flank angle held to 1e-9 off the sketch's own region and the area an EQUALITY against the closed form at 1e-12, where the spur gear's equivalents carry the fit deviation as a band. `RackSpec.MatingGear`/`RackSpec.For` convert both ways so a pair cannot claim one tooth system and mean two (the profile shift does NOT travel — it says where a GEAR sits against this rack, not what the rack is), and `MaximumRootFilletRadius` is ISO 53's ρ_fP,max = (πm/4 − h_f·tanα)cosα/(1−sinα) = 0.4719·m at the standard pair — which is why 0.38·m fits AND why the same 0.38 is refused by name at 25°, where the maximum falls to 0.318·m. The bar spans a whole number of pitches from one tooth-SPACE centre to another, so bars tile; `ClosedFormArea` adds the two fillet corner FILLS ρ²[(1−sinα)/cosα − (π/2−α)/2] per tooth, since a root fillet rounds a REFLEX corner and so adds material (which is also why the extruded bar's mesh volume comes out fractionally OVER the closed form, 1.10e-5 relative — chording a concave arc cuts across the space). Conjugate action is measured the same way as the gear pair's and for the same reason (`Coupling.RackAndPinion` would enforce what the test asserts), with the pinion LIFTED 0.4 to open backlash — legal because an involute against a straight rack transmits the same ratio at ANY mounting height, the flank normal being fixed so its supporting line is tangent to the base circle and translates by exactly r_b·dφ: measured **6.94e-5 mm** of advance variation over 1.2 tooth pitches through handover, against **1.21e-1 mm** for a 25° rack on a 20° pinion (1740×, so the instrument reads flank FORM). **The worm is a thread and the wheel is honestly an approximation** (`Gears.Worm`/`WormPair`/`WormWheel`, `WormSpec`/`WormPair`): a ZA-form worm is straight-sided in the AXIAL plane, so its body is ONE boolean-free helical sweep of a trapezoidal (radius, axial) profile through `SolidFactory.MakeThreadedRod` — exact for the thread's own reason, the root lands being part of the sweep so no core cylinder and no coaxial tangent seam exists — B-Rep-Native, mesh/implicit bridged, not STEP-exportable (helical surfaces have no AP214 entity; `BrepArchive` round-trips it). **Multi-start needed no new machinery**, which is the finding: a helical sweep repeats every LEAD, so the profile covers one lead and simply contains z₁ teeth. `WormSpec` keeps STARTS and teeth apart (`AxialPitch` = πm_x, `Lead` = z₁·p_x, ratio = wheel teeth over STARTS — a two-start worm on 40 teeth is 20:1, not 40:1) and the pitch diameter is a FREE choice setting tan γ = lead/(πd₁) = z₁/q. **The WHEEL is where the honesty is spent**: a true worm wheel is throated and its flank is the ENVELOPE of the worm's motion — hobbing kinematics, no closed form — so `WormWheel` builds an ordinary `HelicalGear` at the worm's LEAD angle, the exact geometry of a crossed-helical pair, which meshes and transmits the stated ratio and touches at a POINT rather than along a line (right for a motion drive, a print or a layout; wrong for a load-carrying reducer). Two identities carry the pairing and both are asserted: the worm's AXIAL pitch IS the wheel's TRANSVERSE circular pitch, and at a 90° shaft angle the worm's axial plane IS the wheel's transverse plane at the central point, so the wheel's transverse pressure angle is the worm's axial one with nothing to convert (β₁ + β₂ = 90° is why the wheel's helix angle is the worm's LEAD angle; both members take the same hand). **The body is measured from its own FIELD with the tessellation bias DERIVED rather than allowed for**: the helical bands are chorded in PHASE only (the generator is straight, so a v-chord is exactly on the surface), so a measured axial thickness is narrowed by 2·r(1−cos(π/n))·tanα — 0.0267 mm predicted at r = 7.6 and the default n = 32 against 0.0275 measured, and the axial flank angle reads 20.10° against 20.00°. The consequence is the pretty part: **a tooth CENTRE is the mean of two flank crossings, so that bias cancels EXACTLY and the LEAD reads to 1e-14**, while a thickness is their difference and doubles it. Volume is Pappus over the sweep (V = L(2π/lead)∫½R² dz over one lead, so ANY length works and the phase washes out), cross-checked against a numerical integral over one AXIAL PITCH — a different decomposition, since the radius has period p_x while the sweep has period lead — with the mesh deficit one-sided and matched to the inscribed n-gon prediction 1 − (n/2π)sin(2π/n) within 20% at 32/64/128, converging quadratically. **The handedness cross-check earns its keep**: the worm is a helical sweep in the B-Rep kernel while the wheel is a twisted extrusion in the modelling layer, two independent constructions that must agree about what right-handed means or a correctly specified pair would be BUILT meshing the wrong way — both read off the geometry, the worm by the sign of its quarter-turn advance and the wheel by ONE probe on the pitch cylinder at +twist (inside for a right-hand wheel, outside for a left-hand one, because twice the twist exceeds the tooth half-angle). Refusals by name throughout: pointed rack/worm teeth, a rack fillet the space cannot hold (naming the maximum), a non-positive worm root diameter (naming the diameter factor), adjacent starts overlapping at the root cylinder. Filed: the throated (globoid) wheel, assessed-not-promised — reachable at mesh/implicit fidelity as a swept subtraction via `Shape.SweptOver`, never as a B-Rep. **Straight bevel gears** (`BevelGears.cs`): `BevelPair` solves the pitch cone angles (tan δ₁ = sin Σ/(z₂/z₁ + cos Σ), reducing to z₁/z₂ at 90°, with δ₁ + δ₂ = Σ asserted), `Straight`/`StraightGear` draw the heel section and loft it to the apex. The solid is EXACT given its section — every straight bevel flank is ruled through the pitch apex, so the body is a two-section ruled loft whose toe section is the heel section scaled ARITHMETICALLY (identical segment count by construction, which is what the loft's index correspondence needs; a re-fit at a scaled tolerance could spend a different number of arcs). What is approximate is only the section, and it is **Tredgold's back-cone approximation, stated**: the virtual spur gear on the back cone has z_v = z/cos δ teeth — generally NOT an integer, which is why it is a construction and cannot be handed to `Gears.Spur` — and its involute is wrapped onto the back cone and projected CENTRALLY from the pitch apex, giving the radial map R(w) = r·w·cos²δ/(1 − w·sin²δ) with azimuths scaled 1/cos δ (so arc tooth thickness is exactly πm/2 and the pitch exactly 2π/z). **The projection choice was settled by MEASUREMENT and two cheaper candidates lost**: central projection reproduces δ_a = δ + atan(h_a/R_e) and δ_f = δ − atan(h_f/R_e) as an IDENTITY (3e-16 rad, read back off the section's own radii) and measures **7.2e-4 … 2.9e-2 module** from the true spherical involute over nine gears (m 1–5, z 12–60, δ 20–70°), where AXIAL projection — which reproduces the textbook d_ae = d + 2h_a·cos δ directly — measures 5e-3 … 3.7e-2, and an "equivalent planar gear" (z teeth at arctan(tan α/cos δ) with proportions scaled by cos δ, which `Gears.Spur` would draw verbatim and which matches in pitch, thickness, tip/root radii AND flank slope at the pitch point) measures 1.4e-2 … 7.5e-2 — **2–8× Tredgold's own error, a second approximation DOMINATING the first**, which is why the cheap route was dropped after being built. The flank is biarc-fitted at module·1e-4 with the deviation reported, two orders below the method's own error; tip and root arcs map to exact arcs and the sub-base radial stretch to an exact radial line, so only the flank is fitted, and the root fillet is CONSTRUCTED in the section at ρ_f*·m unscaled because the map is anisotropic (radial and tangential lengths scale differently, by 2× at δ = 63°) so there is no single stretch to carry. Two limits are REPORTED: the end faces are planes rather than the back and front cones (a loft section must be planar), so the heel section is deeper than the real tooth — ×1.0 at δ = 10°, ×2.4 at 65°, with both `SectionTipRadius` and the standard `BackConeTipRadius` exposed — and that depth caps the cone angle near 68° with the ISO 53 profile-A fillet, the refusal naming the cause AND the verified remedy (`RootFilletCoefficient` 0.30 reaches 75°, 0.20 reaches 80°, so a 3:1 or 4:1 wheel is a setting away rather than a dead end). Undercut is decided by the VIRTUAL count, so a bevel tolerates FEWER real teeth than a spur gear (13 teeth is undercut as a spur at 20° and fine on a 45° cone). **Spiral bevel and hypoid are refused BY NAME** — a spiral flank is the envelope of a cutter under a generating machine's motion rather than a closed-form curve, and a hypoid has no common apex at all. **Two traps paid for**: the mapped involute's derivative VANISHES at the base-circle cusp (both polar components carry a factor of t), so a normalized derivative is 0/0 exactly where the fit starts — dividing the common t out first leaves a smooth field giving the cusp's radial tangent, the same direction `Gears.Spur` reports; and the measurement that ranked the projections initially reported **12 module** of error at steep cone angles, which was a probe bug rather than geometry — the radial map has a POLE at w = 1/sin²δ, and a "generous" bisection bracket walked past it into the negative branch and converged confidently on nonsense (**a bisection needs a monotone bracket, and a map with a pole has none**; closed-form inversions replaced it). **Planetary (epicyclic) sets** (`PlanetaryGears.cs`): an ARRANGEMENT, not a tooth form — `z_ring = z_sun + 2·z_planet` is DERIVED from coaxiality so an inconsistent set cannot be spelled, and the equal-spacing condition ((z_sun + z_ring) divisible by the planet count) and neighbour clearance (2a·sin(π/N) against the planet tip diameter) both refuse by name, the clearance test earning its place since 12/36/6 passes divisibility and only the chord test catches it. **The internal ring needs NO boolean**: an internal gear's tooth SPACE is bounded by involutes of the same base circle as an external gear of the same module, tooth count and pressure angle with the same arc thickness at the pitch circle — only tip and root swap roles — so the ring's bore is exactly the outline of a "cutter" gear whose addendum is the ring's dedendum and vice versa, and the ring is `Sketch.Circle(od).WithHole(that)`: lines and arcs, exact in all three representations. Stated rather than hidden — the ring's tooth TIPS carry the cutter's root fillet (rounded), its ROOTS are sharp, and the undercut check that fires is the CUTTER's (conservative for a ring, and NOT the internal-mesh interference check, which v1 does not do). **The phasing is the substance and is verified from CONTACT**: each mesh fixes a relation (a tooth opposite a space externally, one apart internally) and solving the pair gives γ_k = ψ_k − z_ring(ψ_k − ρ)/z_planet with ρ = (z_planet − 1)·π/z_ring, the (z_planet − 1) being a parity term nothing special-cases; measured with the sketches' own exact signed distance every planet touches the sun at +4.5e-5 and the ring at −3.5e-5 (inside the flank fit deviation) with all three agreeing to nine decimals, while a quarter-pitch phase error reads −1.476 — **33 000× apart**. **The Willis relation is EMERGENT, which is what makes asserting it a real test rather than a tautology**: `PlanetaryGears.Mechanism` adds one `Coupling.Gear` per MESH and states no train ratio, and what makes it work is that the sun and ring pin to the **CARRIER** rather than the housing, so every coupling is written on angles already relative to the rotating line of centres (pinning the sun to the housing would constrain ω_sun where the mesh constrains ω_sun − ω_carrier, and the set would run and return a plausible WRONG ratio rather than fail); the solver then reproduces (ω_s − ω_c)/(ω_r − ω_c) = −z_r/z_s, the held-ring 1 + z_r/z_s and the carrier-held −z_s/z_r to nine decimals, with Kutzbach predicting −6 DOF against a measured 1 (three planets carry one relation three times, which is how a planetary shares load). One solver characterization is pinned because the failure looks like a modelling error and is not: **a gear coupling constrains the CHANGE in each coordinate, so a cold LM step must cross the LARGEST change in the train rather than the driven one** — the planet turns 3.33× the carrier, so driving the carrier 0.8 rad converges (planet 2.67) and 1.0 rad fails (planet 3.33, past a half turn) while `Sweep` reaches it by continuation. **The meshing PHASE is now a derived value rather than a convention re-derived at each call site** (`GearMeshing.cs`: `GearMeshing.External`/`Internal`/`Rack` → a `GearMesh` of `Centre` + `Phase`, with `Place`/`Transform`/`Frame`/`RolledBy`): **the rule depends on the tooth COUNTS and the drawing datum, not on the tooth form** — two gears mesh when the pitch circles roll together and a tooth of one sits in a space of the other, and neither clause mentions the flank curve — so one helper serves involute, cycloidal and anything drawn to the same datum, with the datum stated (`Gears.Spur` a TOOTH on +X, `PlanetaryGears.RingProfile` a tooth SPACE) and MEASURED off the regions' own signed distance rather than assumed. One derivation used three times: a gear of z teeth turned by φ from a datum whose tooth centre lies at τ has the **tooth-index coordinate** `u(θ) = z(θ − φ − τ)/2π` in its own frame (integer at a tooth centre, half-integer at a space centre), and what makes a mesh a CONSTRAINT is that a combination of the two members' coordinates is invariant under rolling — an external pair counter-rotates so `u_A(ψ) + u_B(ψ+π) ≡ ½`, an internal pair co-rotates and engages along ψ from BOTH centres (the pinion's engaging tooth points OUTWARD) so `u_R(ψ) − u_P(ψ) ≡ ½`, and a rack ties x = −r·φ so the condition reduces to `φ = −π/2 − x/r`. **The finding is that `PlanetarySet.PlanetPhase` had solved the INTERNAL half and the external half runs the other way with the azimuth**: the two differ by 2ψ(z_A + z_B)/z_B, a whole tooth pitch — the same placement — for every planet exactly when N divides z_sun + z_ring, **which is what the planetary assembly condition IS**; that is why one number satisfies both of a planet's meshes there and equally why the sign was unobservable in the code that carried it (inside a legal set the two rules are indistinguishable). `PlanetPhase` now delegates bit-identically; three assembling sets agree to 1e-9 of a pitch and a five-planet set violating divisibility lands 16.8 pitches apart, while `RingPhase` keeps its (z_p − 1)π/z_r closed form with a test asserting it IS the general rule at azimuth 0. **Which flank drives is the caller's** — the phase returned is the SYMMETRIC one (tooth centred in the space, i.e. zero-backlash double-flank contact at the standard distance) and `RolledBy` moves onto one flank; the same reasoning gives the profile-shift refusal, since x₁ + x₂ = 0 is what KEEPS the standard centre distance and any other sum needs the involute-function solve, so the refusal names the tooth-count overload (the phase rule itself does not depend on the distance at all). **A helical pair needs nothing extra**: a twisted extrusion's section at height z is the drawn section turned by ψ(z) = z·tan β/r, so the condition holds at every section at once iff ψ_A·z_A + ψ_B·z_B is constant in z — which, since ψ·z = 2z·tan β/m, is exactly the equal-and-opposite helix requirement (measured 0.141 mm of clearance at 0, ¼, ½ and the full face width). Verified from CONTACT throughout and never through `Coupling.Gear`, which enforces a ratio and says nothing about phase: nominal external clearance 0.1413 mm at 0.4 mm of extra centre distance, a half-pitch phase error −1.66 mm, and the WRONG SIGN on the azimuth term −0.017 to −1.67 mm — the smallest being a deliberate near miss at ψ = 0.7 rad where the wrong sign lands 10.03 tooth pitches from the right answer and still bites by eighty times the flank fit deviation. **A still only has to be right at one instant** (`GearTrainMotionTests`, docs `examples/gears.md` §Running it with an `animate:` figure): a pair and a planetary set are DRIVEN through `MotionStudy.Sweep` and the exact profiles probed at every recorded pose, with mutations that fail in different ways — half a tooth pitch of phase error reads −1.66 mm at every frame (the ratio still exact, so the coupling is perfectly satisfied and only the geometry objects) while one tooth of RATIO error starts clear at +0.14 mm and walks into −0.39 mm by the sweep's end, the failure a single still structurally cannot show; `CheckInterference` is cross-checked on the same rig and agrees, staying the secondary instrument because it answers a boolean off the display tessellation where the probe answers a depth off the exact profile. **And a gear clip ALIASES, which misreports the ratio rather than merely looking rough**: the smallest seamless-looping planetary clip is 120° of carrier (the planets swap places, each having advanced whole teeth), which at 24 frames puts the sun at 1.17 and a planet at 1.08 tooth pitches per frame — both aliasing to a slow forward creep, so a viewer reads the sun as turning SLOWER than the carrier it drives at 3.5× — and restoring the same comfort takes z_planet + z_ring = 78 frames (bare Nyquist would need 52), three times the committed file; better tooth counts cannot fix it either, since the requirement z_sun + 3·z_planet ≤ 24 is below the undercut limit for any real pair. So the committed clip runs 30° and does NOT loop, as `animate-explode` does not: **the honest reading beat the seamless one**, the same family as "a mode does not animate at its own frequency", with the general rule being to check the fastest periodic detail in the scene against the frame step before choosing a clip length and to say which property was given up. **Herringbone and crossed helical** (`HerringboneGears.cs`/`CrossedHelicalGears.cs`/`HelicalGearGeometry.cs`) are the same geometry paired differently, and three things carry them. **A herringbone's apex is a WELD and the symmetry is what makes it one**: both opposite-hand halves share the transverse section at the mid-plane, so the twist law is Λ-shaped in z and the upper half IS the lower half reflected — turning the junction from a boolean over a large coincident planar region into a weld BY INDEX. Three exact facts do it and none is a tolerance (the apex ring's z is `Height·1.0`; z → 2a − z fixes it bit for bit since 2a is exact and 2a − a = a; every wall facet spans two rings, so "every vertex is at the apex" is an exact test for the two cap facets to drop), with reflected faces keeping vertex 0 in place per the fan-diagonal rule. Verified BY the identity — the vertex set is invariant under z → W − z bit for bit, where a tolerance would accept a weld that had drifted — plus the two halves' helix angles read off REAL transverse sections as +20.000/−20.000°, that instrument mutation-checked by seeding it from the 20° law on a 30° solid; volume = section area × face width, and the slice convergence measured on successive DIFFERENCES (the arc-flattening deficit is a constant that an error against the analytic value never escapes: ratios 3.41/2.97 that way against 3.63/3.39/3.07/2.73 differenced, and 3.86/3.76/3.57/3.29 at four times the flattening density). **The apex relief groove is filed rather than shipped and the entry carries its measurement**: a groove is material genuinely REMOVED, so it wants a boolean, and subtracting an axial band from a gear fails in BOTH engines — the exact mesh boolean's imprint at every relief diameter/width/density tried, and the B-Rep boolean as an unclosed solid with 1522 unpaired edges for the SAME band against an ordinary SPUR gear, which is what shows it is gear geometry rather than the herringbone's weld. **A crossed pair needs no geometry, only arithmetic, and the SIGNED form is one rule where the textbook states two**: Σ = β₁ + β₂ over signed helix angles reproduces both "β₁ + β₂ same hand" and "β₁ − β₂ opposite hands" once the second gear's hand rides in the sign of its own angle; `CrossedHelicalPair` then VERIFIES what it placed rather than trusting the formula, requiring the two tooth traces to be the same line at the contact point (the geometric content of Σ, and the one thing a sign slip breaks). Contact is a POINT, stated in the API docs and the docs page rather than buried; the tooth PHASE is deliberately not solved (a mate or a driver, not a property of the pairing); parallel shafts are refused by name as an ordinary line-contact helical pair. The trap worth a test is that **the ratio follows the TEETH** — on parallel axes z₂/z₁ and r₂/r₁ coincide and the habit is harmless, while r = m_n·z/(2·cos β) puts them cos β₁/cos β₂ apart on skew axes (46% for a 20°/50° pair). **And a defect came out of building it, of the "two conventions each individually right" family**: the ISO 53 rack coefficients AND the profile shift are quoted against the NORMAL module because a hob cuts them, while `GearSpec` reads them against the TRANSVERSE one — and every one of them is a RADIAL LENGTH, so `HelicalGearGeometry.FromNormal` must divide each by m_t/m_n. Unscaled, a 0.38 fillet reads 1.34× too large at 45° and a 24-tooth member is REFUSED outright for overlapping root fillets: a plausible-looking pair that cannot be drawn. Checked by identities rather than inspection (transverse thickness = normal thickness / cos β; undercut limit = the classical 2·h_a*_n·cos β/sin²α_t). **The helical pair's conjugate test is the transverse-section argument turned into a measurement of the half that could be wrong**: at every section a helical pair IS a spur pair, since each member's section is its own spur profile rotated by ψ(z) and rotating both members rigidly moves the PHASE of contact and not the ratio — so the spur pair's contact-measured conjugacy carries over, and what is left to check is that a real transverse section of the built solid, rotated back by ψ(z), lands on the exact spur region's zero level. The bound is DERIVED (arc-flattening sagitta + wall-panel chord + biarc fit deviation, summed because all three are systematic) and mutation-checked at a 5% twist error, which reads over 100× it. **Cycloidal gear geometry** (`CycloidalGears.cs`; docs in `examples/mechanisms.md`): the clock and instrument tooth form — an **epicycloid** above the pitch circle and a **hypocycloid** below, both entering the sketch vocabulary as biarc chains with the deviation REPORTED (the involute file's convention, and its recursive-bisection fitter is now the shared `Curve2dChains.Fit`). Both cusps at the pitch circle are RADIAL, so face and flank meet tangent-continuously with nothing arranged; the points are written as `r + (a term exactly zero at t = 0)` so the two hand over their shared point bit for bit, and the tangents come from the half-angle factorization, which is DEFINED at the cusp where the speed vanishes. **The shape parameter belongs to the PAIR, not to the gear**: an involute gear is conjugate to any other of its module and pressure angle, but a cycloidal wheel's face rolls against a pinion's flank only if ONE describing circle traced both — so `CycloidalGears.Mesh` refuses a mismatched pair BY NAME and a single `GeneratingCircleDiameter` (shared across a set, the interchangeable-set practice) satisfies conjugacy in both directions at once, which is why it is one field rather than a face/flank pair. `Pair` defaults to the classic clock choice, half the PINION's pitch diameter, i.e. the **ρ = r/2 identity that makes the hypocycloid a straight RADIAL line** — and nothing special-cases it: the general formula's y-term vanishes identically and the general biarc fit returns literal `Line2d` pieces, so the test MEASURES straightness off the generated sketch (crossings within 1e-12 rad of one ray) rather than asserting the formula it was built from, with a hypocycloidal flank reading 1e-3 on the same instrument. Closed forms carry the rest: both roll-angle inversions are one `Acos` (|E(t)|² and |H(t)|² each reduce to a single cosine of r·t/ρ) and `ClosedFormArea` integrates both cycloid families exactly — ½(r+ρ)(r+2ρ)[t − (ρ/r)sin(rt/ρ)] and ½(r−ρ)(r−2ρ)[…], **the second reducing to exactly zero at the radial-flank identity** since a line through the origin encloses nothing. The one thing with no closed form is the root fillet tangency (an involute's has one) and it is an honest bracketed root solve, said so in place. **Conjugate action is verified from CONTACT at the DESIGN centre distance** — a cycloidal pair's describing circle must roll on BOTH pitch circles, so unlike an involute pair it is not centre-distance invariant; backlash therefore comes from THINNING the teeth, which is exact because a cycloid rotated about its own pitch centre is the same cycloid at another phase. Measured 4e-6 rad of spread through tooth handover, against 1.3e-3 for the same pair mounted 0.3 mm long and 1.3e-3 for a wheel cut with a 1.6× describing circle — one instrument, two mutations, both seen at ~300×, and **the centre-distance sensitivity is DOCUMENTED as a property of the tooth form rather than warned about**. Refusals by name: a describing circle past half the pitch radius (the hypocycloid then curls back and UNDERCUTS — the cycloidal counterpart of z_min), an addendum past the 2ρ an epicycloid can reach, a dedendum past |r − 2ρ|, pointed teeth as a LENGTH at the weld tier, fillets that do not fit their gap. `ClockGearProportions` transcribes the BS 978-2 addendum columns (⚠ verify-against-datasheet, the `StandardHoles` convention) and stores ONLY the addenda — each member's dedendum is DERIVED as its mate's addendum plus a clearance, since a second stored column could only drift (the fine-pitch tap-drill rule). **Cycloidal drives** (`CycloidalDrives.cs`): the pin-wheel reducer's lobed disc, whose roller-centre curve is **DERIVED rather than transcribed** — substituting the orbit `e·(cos φ, sin φ)` and the disc rotation `λφ` into `Rot(−λφ)(P_j − O(φ))` collapses to `C(s) = R(cos s, sin s) − e(cos Ns, sin Ns)` for EVERY pin at once exactly when λ = −1/(N−1), because N·α_j is a whole number of turns. The lobe count N−1, the peak-to-valley depth of exactly 2e and the counter-rotating rate all fall out rather than being asserted — **and so does a scope decision**: repeating the derivation for a lobe difference d leaves the phase at 2πj/d, a whole number of turns for every pin only at d = 1, so the one-lobe-difference restriction is a THEOREM the refusal states rather than a v1 limit. The cut profile costs nothing extra because the cam roller-follower work already found the shape of the answer: **an offset curve's unit tangent IS the base curve's** (D′ = (1 − R_r·κ)·C′), so the biarc fit gets exact tangents free AND the same factor states the cusp refusal — the pin must be under the lobe tip's radius of curvature (R + eN)²/(R + eN²), itself closed form because both the curvature numerator and |C′|² depend on the parameter only through u = cos((N−1)s), making κ(u) = (A − Bu)/(P − Qu)^{3/2} a one-variable family with a single closed-form critical point (no scan). **Two ratios are named rather than one being called "the" ratio** — pins fixed with the disc as output is z_lobes/(z_pins − z_lobes) = N−1 counter-rotating (the sign rides on `DiscTurnsPerInputTurn` so a caller cannot lose it), the disc held with the ring as output is z_pins/(z_pins − z_lobes) = N co-rotating; the backlog named only the second. Verification is the derivation's own identity and it is exact: every pin lies ON the roller-centre curve at every input angle, so the disc sketch's signed distance reads exactly the pin radius at every pin through a full input rotation — **measured residual 3.06e-4 against a fit deviation of 3.06e-4**, i.e. the pose relation contributes nothing, asserted as that RATIO (1.002) and not only as a bound. That one number is simultaneously the clash check (no pin ever reads less) and the ratio MEASUREMENT: sweeping candidate rates −1/8, −1/9, −1/11, −1/12 and +1/10 drives the pins hundreds of times the fit deviation into the disc. Areas ride closed forms both sides — π(R² + e²N) for the roller-centre curve (Green's integrand reduces to one cosine that integrates away over a turn) and the inward-offset identity A − R_r·L + πR_r² for the disc, L being the one elliptic integral and taken by the uniform trapezoid rule, which is spectrally accurate on a smooth periodic integrand. v1 draws the lobe profile plus an optional central bore; output roller holes, a running clearance, the eccentric and lantern pinions are filed. **Animation** (`Animation.cs`/`PoseTracks.cs`/`CameraTracks.cs`/`DeformationTracks.cs`/`AnimationPlayback.cs` in Viewer.Core, `ApngWriter`/`GifWriter`/`AnimationExport` in Viewer; docs `examples/animation.md` with committed APNGs): **an animation must not touch geometry** — a timeline is a duration + easing + at most ONE pose track, one camera track and one deformation track (two full-instance-list producers cannot compose, and two factors on one scale have no composition either; sequencing lives INSIDE a track), each with a clamp-semantics window, and `Animation.At(t)` is a PURE function so scrubbing, window playback, APNG/GIF/frame-sequence export and DocsGen `animate:<id>` fences evaluate one code path. A track's answer is matrices, a camera or a SCALAR — never a re-meshed part, which is how a deformed structural result animates without weakening the rule (see the field-display paragraph). Lives in **Viewer.Core by dependency direction** (pose tracks speak Scene/Mechanism, camera tracks speak CameraState; Modeling cannot reference the camera types), so a Scene cannot carry its animation — hosts pass `WithAnimation(scene => ...)`, re-invoked per live reload on a background task (track construction may mesh for bounds). `MechanismTrack` plays a swept `MotionStudy` — recorded frames VERBATIM at sample points (bit-exact, locked), chordal rigid interpolation between (delta b·a⁻¹ is rigid whatever the part transform carries; rotation slerp via new `Quaterniond.FromRotationMatrix` (Shepperd), origin on the straight chord), never a re-solve (the branch-flip trap); the (study, scene) overload grafts by occurrence path so bystanders stay put. `ExplodeTrack.Stagger` = per-occurrence timing windows over the new `Flatten(Func<Occurrence, double>)` (same walk; exact-0 leaves frames bit-identical) — the sequenced explode. Camera tracks reuse the view cube's `Ease`/`ShortestYawTarget` (turntable loops seamlessly under LINEAR easing with whole turns; fly-through along any `Curve3d`, Z-up so RMF roll is documented as dropped; `CameraMath.DefaultCamera` is now the ONE first-visit framing). Export: **APNG first** (acTL/fcTL/fdAT over the shared PngWriter internals; first frame = the PNG default image so the file ships as `.png` and degrades to a still; delay = Duration/frames; loop sampling t = i/frames so turntables don't repeat their seam frame), frame sequence always (the ffmpeg escape hatch), **GIF second and honestly banding** on shaded renders (median-cut where the PARTITION is the mapping — ≤256-colour images reproduce exactly; LZW locked by round-trip against an independently-written decoder; no dithering, wireframe/flat GIFs better — said in the docs). One camera per clip when no track drives it, framed over first∪last bounds — never per-frame framing. SceneHost transport (Play/Pause/Loop/scrub) owns only a DispatcherTimer advancing by REAL elapsed time; state is UI-free `AnimationPlayback` (loop wraps the overshoot), poses matched to the tab's instances by path. **Batched export + stills**: `OffscreenRenderer.RenderSequence` renders a whole clip through ONE EGL context, one set of linked programs and one set of uploaded per-part buffers — legal for exactly the reason the contract states (an animation moves poses, so every frame draws the same parts and the cache keys on `Part` reference), measured **1069 ms -> 165 ms (6.5x)** on a 24-frame 480x360 export of a four-occurrence exploding assembly (win-x64) with the batched pixels asserted **byte-identical** to one `Render` per frame; `EngrCad.RenderToImage(scene, animation, t, ...)` renders one instant and the MCP `screenshot` tool takes the same `t`, both posing through the ONE `EngrCad.PoseAt` seam (camera precedence = track, then explicit, then first-union-last framing — never per-t framing, which would make a series of stills jump). **STEP assemblies** (`StepWriter.WriteAssembly` — one PRODUCT per distinct solid, `NEXT_ASSEMBLY_USAGE_OCCURRENCE` per placement, poses as `CONTEXT_DEPENDENT_SHAPE_REPRESENTATION` over `ITEM_DEFINED_TRANSFORMATION`; non-rigid instance transforms refused by name since a placement has no room for scale, and MIRRORED (improper) ones refused too — they pass every orthonormality test but AXIS2 axes are right-handed by definition (Y = Z×X on every reader's side), so a left-handed triple would silently re-pose un-mirrored on read-back; the writer's `Placement(Frame3d)` overload mirrors `StepReader.Axis2`/`Frame3d.FromZX` (origin + Z + X stored, Y derived, frames round-trip by construction) while the matrix path keeps emitting the matrix's own columns so the written text is ULP-stable; STEP string literals now escape apostrophes — and `StepReader` **reads it back**: `StepReadResult.Instances`/`HasAssemblyStructure`, nested sub-assemblies composed, reader paths starting below the root product so our own writer round-trips exactly. `StepAssembly` in Modeling builds the list from `Part.TryGetSolid` — no second lowering — and reports what it skipped; `--export part.step` now writes ONE assembly file instead of one un-posed file per part). GL-touching test classes must join the xUnit `"offscreen-gl"` collection (parallel EGL pbuffer creation is flaky). **3D annotations (PMI) v1**: parts carry their own manufacturing information (`Annotations.cs`/`Callouts.cs`) — `Annotation` base (`Label` override, `Offset` placement, lazy-solid `Resolve`), `LinearDimension` (point-to-point + auto-measuring `BetweenFaces` via semantic `BrepQueries` face selectors — the same topological-naming story as features: selectors re-run per resolution, so dimensions re-measure through parameter edits and `FeatureHistory` regeneration), `RadialDimension.OnEdge` (reads the actual `IsCircular` radius; radius/diameter text), `LeaderNote`, `DatumLabel`; `Part.Annotate` + `ResolveAnnotations`/`TryResolveAnnotations` (geometry lowered to B-Rep once and cached; the Try path caches diagnostics so a selector broken by an edit becomes a status message, not a crash; `Scene.PreMesh` pre-resolves off the render thread) produce part-local `ResolvedAnnotation`s that viewers pose by the instance transform (assembly instances show their part's annotations in place); `HoleCallout`/`ThreadCallout` generate standard text from `HoleSpec`/`ThreadSpec` (diameter/depth/cbore/csk symbols as unicode escapes — source files stay ASCII; cbore/csk continuations on their own `'\n'` line). **PMI v2**: `AngularDimension` (three points, or two planar faces measuring the INCLUDED angle — in-plane directions perpendicular to the shared intersection line toward each face's centroid, what a drafter dimensions, not the normals' angle; vertex in `ResolvedAnnotation.AnchorC`, drawn as extension rays + 5°-chorded arc + degree text), `ToleranceSpec.Symmetric/Limits` text sugar (Label wins), `LinearDimension.Chain/Ordinate` (consecutive pairs on one line / every point from the datum with stacked offsets), **multi-line stroke-font text** ('\n' stacks lines; single-line output bit-identical — the docs PNGs hang off it), `HoleTable.For(part)` (lettered rows GENERATED from the graph's `DrillShape`/`ThreadedHoleShape` nodes — the spec now rides on `DrillShape` — with per-hole `DatumLabel` balloons + a multi-line table note) and `HoleAnnotations.AutoAttach` (one callout per call, "N×" prefix), and **pickable annotations** (`AnnotationGeometry.Pick` — ray vs the same Build segments within 8 style px, depth-blind matching the always-on-top draw; gold overdraw batch, click-through never falls to the part behind; `ViewportControl.PickAnnotation`). **PMI v3 — occlusion-aware rendering** (`AnnotationDepth` in Viewer.Core beside `ShadingStyle`; `ViewportControl.AnnotationDepth` + the toolbar's Top/Depth cycler, `EngrCadOptions.AnnotationDepth`/`WithAnnotationDepth`, `RenderToImage(annotationDepth:)`, MCP `screenshot`'s `annotationDepth`, an `annotationDepth` docs fence variable, and the browser's `EngrCadViewport.AnnotationDepth`): `AlwaysOnTop = 0` stays the default and the incumbent look (the `ShadingStyle.Lit = 0` rule — a front end that says nothing renders what it always did, and all 132 committed docs PNGs are byte-identical), `Occluded` dims the stretches with material in front of them. **The mechanism is TWO DEPTH FUNCTIONS over one buffer, and it retires both shapes the backlog proposed** — no depth pre-pass and no second line batch, because the scene is already in the depth buffer by the time the overlay draws: the line-work range is drawn at `LEQUAL` in `AnnotationGeometry.Color`, the SAME range again at `GREATER` in `HiddenColor`, and the two comparisons partition the fragments with no overlap (LEQUAL takes equality, GREATER does not), so nothing is drawn twice, nothing is dropped and no CPU classification exists for three front ends to disagree about (`AnnotationLayer.DrawBatches` is the one rule; `ViewportFrame.AppendAnnotations` emits it as values, which is why `DrawCall` gained a `depthFunc` carried as a NAME — the GL enum values are the browser's, the choice is .NET's). **Three findings, each from a measurement rather than a preference.** (1) **The dimension's VALUE is exempt**, and the first build proved why: depth-treating the whole overlay turned "40" and "⌀5.5" on the docs plate into smudges — the two figures a reader is there for — while the lines it dimmed read exactly as intended. So `AnnotationGeometry.Build` takes an optional second list for the stroke-font glyphs and datum boxes and the front ends draw that range depth-off at full strength (ONE upload, two ranges — the field legend's trick); passing null is the incumbent single-list build in the incumbent ORDER, which is what keeps always-on-top and `Pick` bit-identical. A dimension's anatomy is a POINTER and a VALUE: which side of the material the pointer runs on is real information, while the text's 3D position is a placement, so occluding it destroys information rather than conveying it. (2) **The depth bias must move each point along its OWN EYE RAY** (`AnnotationCamera.PulledTowardEye`). It exists because the interesting annotations are COPLANAR with the face they document — a radial leader lies in the plane of the face whose bore it measures — so without a bias those fragments are classified by which of two rasterizations rounded further, and the overlay speckles along the whole run; a bias settles it by decision, *coplanar means visible*. But translating along the view direction, the obvious form, slides a perspective point off its ray: measured **134 changed pixels** in a render whose overlay had nothing in front of it at all, purely from an anti-aliased 1-pixel line's coverage redistributing. Scaling about the eye instead leaves the screen position exact and the depth reduced by exactly the bias — and the scale factor is a CONSTANT for the whole overlay, since a perspective pixel's world size is itself proportional to depth so the ratio cancels. The result is that the mode is a colour change and nothing else: **663 darker pixels and 0 lighter** on the docs plate, and a free-space annotation renders **byte-identically** in both modes (both asserted). One style pixel is the magnitude, which is scale-free for a screen-constant overlay and is over a hundred depth-buffer bits at any normal framing, because depth resolution falls as z² while a pixel's world size grows only as z. (3) **Dimmed rather than dashed, and DARKER rather than lighter.** A screen-space stipple keyed on `gl_FragCoord` — the backlog's "stippled shader variant" — is constant along some screen direction, so a line parallel to it comes out solid or vanishes entirely; there is no orientation-free fragment form, and a real dash needs an along-the-line vertex attribute reaching all three front ends (filed, with the note that the cheap place for it is CPU-side segment chopping in the shared build). Darkening is not a taste call either: a hidden fragment is by definition drawn over the occluder, and every part colour is a lit mid-tone brighter than the background, so darkening gains contrast in every case the mode can produce — a first attempt at (0.44, 0.46, 0.52) left a channel-sum contrast of 93 against the docs plate's top face and read as a smudge, where (0.28, 0.30, 0.36) reads 231. Picking stays DEPTH-BLIND and unchanged, which is consistent rather than a leftover: nothing is hidden, so an annotation you can see is still pickable — and the selection highlight stays always-on-top in both modes, a selection being UI state rather than documentation about the part. **Simulation results (fields) v1**: results are DATA on a mesh, carried by the document — `MeshField`/`FieldRange` (EngrCAD.Mesh, beside the writers, so a future solver produces one without referencing the modelling API; named scalar or vector values over a mesh's VERTICES with units, immutable so a field is a value, `ScalarAt` = the value for a scalar field and the MAGNITUDE for a vector one so "colour by displacement" needs no extra call, `Magnitude()`/`Component(i)`/`Sample(mesh, …)` derived, cell association a documented gap rather than a half-supported mode) attached via **`Part.Results`/`AddResult`** (same name REPLACES, so a re-solve updates rather than accumulating twins; attaching never meshes, which keeps `PreMesh` parallel) and displayed via **`Part.FieldDisplay`** (which result, which `FieldColorMap`, over what range, optionally displaced by a vector result) resolved once for every consumer by `TryResolveFieldDisplay` (deliberately does NOT mesh, so a properties panel or MCP tool can call it with no GL; a display naming a removed result becomes a status message naming what DOES exist). `FieldColorMap` follows `DisplayMode`'s precedent exactly — the enum is a document-model choice, the tables (`ColorMaps`: 17-stop viridis, monotone in lightness and asserted so by sampling the table; 9-stop Moreland diverging with a neutral grey midpoint, which only means what it looks like over `FieldRange.SymmetricAboutZero()` — deliberately asked for, never applied silently) live in Viewer.Core beside the shaders. **Colour is a vertex attribute under the baked-AO rule**: `aFieldColor` at slot 3, a context constant when no buffer is attached, and a `uFieldColor` strength of 0 making `mix(uColor, vFieldColor, 0.0)` exactly `uColor` — so a part with no results renders **byte-identically**, proved by the docs suite (all 89 rendered PNGs unchanged across the shader change; no unit test could have shown that). **A deformed shape LOOKED like the exception to the matrices-only animation rule and is not** — the displacement rides as vertex attributes 4–7 under the same constant-when-absent rule (a mesh with no displacement buffer reads zero, so `aPos + s*0` is `aPos` for ANY finite uniform), and the vertex shader applies `position + uDeformScale * displacement`, so **a whole result animation is ONE float uniform per frame**: nothing re-uploaded, no buffer touched, the instance list untouched, in all three front ends (`ViewportControl.DeformFactor`, `RenderToImage`/`RenderSequence`, `ViewportFrame`'s `deformFactor`). The CPU deformation path RETIRED from rendering rather than living on beside the shader one — two paths computing one displaced shape would disagree in the last bits forever, and the verification bar (*an animated frame at t equals a static render of the same configuration, byte for byte*, asserted at three factors) is only meaningful with one renderer. What made retiring it free is an identity: **a triangle whose vertices move linearly in s has a facet normal that is exactly QUADRATIC in s** (`(a×b) + s(a×β + α×b) + s²(α×β)`), so three coefficient vectors reproduce the displaced normal at every scale — the same normal the CPU path recomputed. Reusing the source normals was the cheap alternative and was rejected on a MEASUREMENT: a cantilever at its own 40× turns its surface **9.9°**, matching the analytic tip slope, a ~12% shading error under a 45° key light. Cost accounted honestly: exactly one committed render moved (the FEA bracket, **11 pixels of 1.79 million**, max channel delta 14 — float-vs-double rounding in the same formula on the only committed deformed geometry with curved faces); the cantilever in `fields.md` is byte-identical. `DeformationTracks` are the laws (`LoadRamp` 0→1→0, honest for a LINEAR solve since a linear result scales exactly so intermediate frames are the ANSWERS rather than a tween; `Oscillate` = the mode-shape law, with FOUR caveats of which the last is the one that misleads: no physical amplitude, a conventional sign, a well-defined direction only at a simple frequency, and **a mode does NOT animate at its own frequency** — `cycles = frequency × duration` is dimensionally correct and useless, since a steel blade 80×20×6 rings near 780 Hz and a 2 s clip would ask for ~1570 cycles, hundreds per frame, aliasing into blur that no frame rate fixes; stiff metal parts run hundreds of Hz to tens of kHz and only things like tall buildings are slow enough to play at true speed, so use a small fixed cycle count and STATE the slowdown factor. Rank caveats by what a reader will believe: arbitrary amplitude and sign are half-expected, whereas "it runs at the mode's frequency" sounds like something a solver would arrange. `Ramp`/`Constant`/`From`). The undeformed shape ghosts behind at `FieldRendering.GhostAlpha` from its OWN upload (it must look like the undeformed part, so it keeps that part's face normals and no baked occlusion). **Two things deliberately do NOT follow the factor, and both are stated rather than discovered**: a part carrying a displacement draws NO feature-edge overlay at any factor (the draw list must not depend on t, or a clip could not reuse one upload — so the factor-0 frame is the undeformed SHAPE without the undeformed part's chrome), and the pick BVH is built once at the part's OWN `DeformScale` via `FieldRendering.PickShape` (a spatial index cannot be a uniform, so a click is exact on a still and at a ramp's peak and off by the exaggeration difference in between; rebuilding it per frame is the cost the design avoids). The legend follows the effective factor, since its title states the number. **Transient thermal playback deliberately did NOT ride along**: temperature per step is a COLOUR animation, which has no single-uniform form, so it is scoped separately rather than assumed. `FieldLegend` (Viewer.Core, pure) is the colour bar — flat-coloured bands since the line program is flat-colour, stroke-font ticks and a title stating the units and any deformation exaggeration, on the left edge (the cube owns the top-right, the meshing panel the bottom centre) — drawn by all three front ends AND in headless renders, unlike the view cube, because a legend is documentation and a colour plot without its scale is a picture of nothing in particular; ONE legend from the first visible display, since several parts on different scales under one bar would be a legend that lies (that is what an explicit `FieldDisplay.Range` is for). **Log-scale fields read their own declaration**: a field whose units string says `log10(…)` — the `FatigueResults` life convention — gets anti-logged tick labels on integer decades where the range spans ≥ 2 of them (`FieldLegend.TryLogUnits`/`TickMarks`; end ticks always print the true range, an even five-tick fallback below two decades), and a title in the base units tagged LOG SCALE — the units string rather than a `LogScale` boolean beside it, because a flag next to a units string that also says log10 is two spellings of one fact free to drift, and the declaration already round-trips through the document format; colours stay linear over the log values (that IS log-colour), so a display with no such declaration is bit-identical (every committed PNG unchanged; the fatigue page gained a NEW aluminium life render — aluminium because every node is finite where a steel life plot is mostly NaN, which today paints as the map's BOTTOM stop, filed). Two smaller rules: a **zero-span range normalizes to 0.5** (a constant field has no position to report and an extreme colour reads as a hot spot), and **NaN is skipped when ranging** so one undefined value cannot collapse a legend. Viewer toolbar **Fields** toggle + properties-panel min/max readout; `RenderToImage(fields: false)` is how a geometry figure is taken of a model that carries results. **`VtuWriter`/`VtkCellType`** (EngrCAD.Mesh) exports ParaView-readable `.vtu` dependency-free (hand-written XML, ASCII arrays) over a seam that is deliberately **(points, cells, cell types, point data)** rather than a mesh type — a surface result writes triangles/quads/polygons today and a tet mesher writes `VtkCellType.Tetra` through the same call with nothing in the writer changing; the merged multi-part overload fills a part's missing array with **NaN** (VTK's own "no value": dropping it loses the result that exists, zeros show a fake safe region). Wired into `--export .vtu` (the log states how many result arrays came out); docs page `examples/fields.md`. `RenderMesh.SourceVertices` is what places a per-source-vertex field on the flat mesh's duplicates EXACTLY — hashing positions also works (the occlusion bake does it) but is ambiguous wherever two distinct source vertices share a position. Modeling also owns the document model: `Part` (name + any-engine geometry + color + transform; `GetMesh(quality)` produces/caches the display mesh, `Scene.PreMesh()` runs it off the render thread) grouped into named `Tab`s in a `Scene` (`scene.Add(part)` shorthand targets a default "Model" tab; part names unique per tab; `Part` stays a leaf)**. **The Viewer is a library, not an app**: design code builds a `Scene` and calls `EngrCad.Show(scene)` (blocking; one call per process — Avalonia lifetime constraint; `onViewportReady` callback + thread-safe `ViewportControl.SetParts` support custom hosts; the internal `SceneHost` renders the tab strip with per-tab cameras — auto-framed on first visit, remembered after, kept in place on live reload). Rendering is an OpenGL viewport (Avalonia `OpenGlControlBase` + Silk.NET over ANGLE/GLES3) with a laptop-friendly orbit camera (drag orbit, shift+drag pan, ctrl+drag/scroll zoom, keyboard fallbacks) and click-picking that reports part *names*. **CAD chrome (dark theme)**: toolbar (Fit, Front/Top/Right/Iso, perspective/orthographic toggle — ortho frustum sized to keep the target plane's apparent size), model tree (visibility checkboxes, two-way selection sync via `ViewportControl.SelectionChanged`/`Select`/`SetVisible`/`Frame`; rows expand into a part's **construction tree** — `ConstructionTree.cs` in Modeling: a `ConstructionNode` is a label + kind + **positional path + the graph node it came from**, `FromShape` walking the operation graph with operands as children and a sketch row under sketch-driven extrude/revolve/sweep (labels straight from `Describe()`), `FromHistory` listing features with suppression and `[Param]` values each targeting `FeatureHistory.BodyAfter(i)`. Selecting a row **previews** it: a sketch's curves flattened onto its plane, or the sub-shape's feature edges — a rollback view. Previews are line geometry only, memoized by `ConstructionPreviewCache` **keyed on the shape reference** (a shared sub-shape lowers once) while expansion/selection key on the *path*, built on a background task so lowering never touches the UI or render thread, and drawn by `PreviewLayer.cs` through the line program with depth off; the preview is **restored by path** after a live reload (node references change, occurrence + construction paths do not). **Feature rows are editable**: per-row suppress toggle and a **rollback marker** (`FeatureRollback`, UI-free — suppresses every feature below the marker, records what the BAR suppressed so restoring never un-suppresses a user's own suppression), and clicking a feature row opens its `[Param]` values as editable fields in the properties panel — Enter writes through the SAME JSON seam as `SaveParameters`/`LoadParameters`/MCP `set_param` and regenerates on a background task; **the fields are TYPED**, chosen by the pure `ParamEditors.KindFor` in Viewer.Core from metadata the registry already carried — a `bool` gets a checkbox, an enum a dropdown of its members, a numeric with a FINITE `[Param(Min=, Max=)]` range a slider beside its box (the box stays because a slider cannot express 12.7), anything else the text field; every editor still writes through the one JSON seam, so a typed editor is a better way to SAY a value and never a second way to apply one, and the slider commits on RELEASE because each application regenerates the part and is an undo step (success republishes the tab, failure keeps the previous geometry and names the feature). **Both editing paths — the per-row suppress toggle and the properties-panel `[Param]` fields — now go through `UndoStack`/`DocumentEdits`**, so the toolbar's Undo/Redo buttons and Ctrl+Z/Ctrl+Y take them back (Ctrl+Shift+Z redoes too; `SceneHost.RunEdit` serializes every stack touch behind an in-flight flag and reports on the UI thread, which is what lets a single-threaded stack drive an off-thread regeneration). **The keyboard handler is Bubble WITHOUT handledEventsToo — deliberately the opposite of the viewport's pointer handlers**, whose trackpad lesson was that nothing upstream may starve them: a focused `TextBox` (the parameter fields) has its own undo and marks the key handled, and stealing it would make typing unrecoverable, a worse failure than a missed shortcut. The rollback bar stays outside the stack (it carries its own per-part suppression bookkeeping) and keeps the direct `RegenerateAndRefresh` path. **`Part.TryGetSolid()` lowers a part's B-Rep at most ONCE** — mesh, feature edges, selector annotations, STEP export and previews all share it (measured `Scene.PreMesh` **32.8 s → 10.1 s** on a heavy Shape scene; a five-hole drilled plate went from three ~9.4 s lowerings to one), and a lowering that fails is remembered and replayed verbatim rather than retried per consumer), properties panel (kind/faces/closed/volume/area/size), status bar, gradient background, adaptive 1-2-5 ground grid + RGB axes, and a feature-edge overlay over polygon-offset fills — **sourced from the ACTUAL B-Rep edges for B-Rep-backed parts** (`Part.GetFeatureEdges`, cached and primed by `Scene.PreMesh`; `BrepFeatureEdges` in Interop samples the exact edge curves at display resolution ≥ 96 segments/circle via the tessellator's `SampleEdge` rules, so bore rims stay smooth circles at any tessellation; sharpness decided by adjacent-face outward normals at three interior probes — periodic-face seams and equal-carrier wrap-split junctions omitted, unprobeable edges kept: draw rather than hide; a Shape part's B-Rep now lowers only once for mesh + edges + annotations + export, via `Part.TryGetSolid()`), with `MeshFeatureEdges` (EngrCAD.Mesh: boundary + sharp-dihedral) as the fallback for SDF/mesh parts. **Per-part display modes** (`Part.DisplayMode`: Shaded/Wireframe/Translucent — wireframe via `WireframeEdges` over the line program, translucent alpha-blended with per-part back-to-front ordering and opaque silhouette edges; a per-tree-row cycler drives it), a **global view style** (`ViewportControl.ViewStyle` toolbar dropdown: Points/Wireframe/Shaded/ShadedWithEdges — one precedence rule in `RenderModes.Resolve` shared by both render passes: explicitly non-default part modes override the global style, default-Shaded parts follow it; Points = vertex point sprites via a dedicated point program, desktop GL needs ProgramPointSize enabled; Shaded = fills without the feature-edge overlay), and a **`Capture` screenshot button** (`ViewportControl.SaveScreenshot` → `glReadPixels` in the render pass → dependency-free `PngWriter`, path reported in the status bar). **Matcap shading** (`ShadingStyle` Lit/Clay/Metal in Viewer.Core; toolbar Shading dropdown → `ViewportControl.Shading`, `EngrCadOptions.Shading`/`WithShading`, `RenderToImage(shading:)`, MCP `screenshot`'s `shading`, `EngrCadViewport.Shading`, DocsGen fences declare a `shading` variable): the lit-sphere image a matcap samples is PROCEDURAL — Gaussian lobes over the view-space normal in `ViewerShaders.MeshFragment` behind an `uMatcap` int selector — because the render stack has no texture machinery and the parity rule would make a texture reach three front ends; the lobe constants ARE the material and live in the one shader file all three compile. `Lit = 0` is load-bearing (a linked program's uniforms initialize to 0, so a front end that says nothing renders the incumbent look — verified by the docs-PNG byte comparison), shading is deliberately NOT a `ViewStyle` member (the style says what is drawn, shading how a fill is LIT, and they compose), there is no per-part override (a scene lit two ways reads as a rendering bug), and the view-space normal cost no plumbing (`uView` is a program-wide uniform the fragment shader just declares too). AO multiplies the matcap sample exactly as it multiplies ambient+diffuse and section cut faces keep their flat cut material, pinned by a byte-level cut-pixel test. **Section planes**: a Section toolbar toggle clips everything beyond **up to 4 section planes** (`SectionPlane(Normal, Offset)` — general normals, `On(axis, offset)` for the axis-aligned ones the toolbar/CLI expose — combined by `SectionCombine`: **Intersection** clips only where EVERY plane excludes, giving the classic **quarter cut** from two perpendicular planes and an **octant** from three; **Union** clips where ANY does, which is the old single-plane behavior generalized; with one plane the two rules coincide, so single-plane output is unchanged. Toolbar axis cycler; `[`/`]` keys nudge 2% of the scene extent along the active axis; defaults to mid-bounds, and changing the axis re-centers) — implemented as fragment-shader `discard` (`dot(world, uSectionAxis) > uSectionOffset`) in the mesh, line, and point programs (edges/wireframes clip consistently with fills; grid/axes are scene furniture and stay unclipped), with exposed interiors detected via `gl_FrontFacing` and shaded as a flat darker cut material (axis-agnostic); custom hosts drive `ViewportControl.SectionEnabled`/`SectionAxis`/`SectionOffset` (`SectionHeight` kept as a delegating legacy alias); picking and hover **honor** the section planes (`SectionClip.Hides` holds the shaders' own discard rule in one place — generalized to N planes + combine mode alongside the shader, since otherwise picking and rendering would disagree about which corner a quarter cut removes — so a click cannot select through the cut-away part), and hiding a part hides its annotations. **View cube** (top-right): stroke-font-labeled orientation widget mirroring the orbit camera (own ortho mini-projection, depth cleared, drawn last so it appears in `SaveScreenshot` captures but is excluded from offscreen renders by design); face/edge/corner clicks animate a 250 ms smoothstep shortest-yaw-path move to the orthogonal/45°/iso poses (`ViewCubeMath.PoseFor`, locked to the toolbar values; `ViewportControl.ViewCubeClick` for direct testing — synthetic mouse never reaches Avalonia); dragging on the cube orbits and then **rotate-snaps** to the nearest standard orientation on release, as commercial cubes do (`ViewCubeMath.SnapToNearest`; snapping an already-snapped pose is idempotent) — and the SceneHost toolbar's Front/Top/Right/Iso buttons route through the same `ViewCubeMath.PoseFor`, so widget and toolbar can never disagree about what "Front" means. All logic in `ViewCube.cs`. **Hover highlights**: pointer-move re-picks via the existing BVH raycast throttled by 4-DIP travel (`HoverThrottle`); one shader knob does both states — `uHighlight` 1.0 = selection gold, 0.35 = hover tint (wireframe/point parts blend line color instead); cube-region hover brightens the face set under the cursor and suppresses model hover; hover inherits picking's ignore-the-section-plane v1 behavior. **SDF isolines on the cut** (automatic when available): parts whose geometry is an `Sdf` — or a `Shape` with an implicit lowering, lowered once and cached per part — get iso-distance contours overlaid on the section plane (gold d=0 = exact surface cross-section, cool positive/warm negative families at ±k·spacing, 1-2-5 spacing from the parts' bounds — wall thickness at a glance, blend/offset debugging); extraction is `SdfContours.OnPlane` in Interop (UI-free marching squares over one batch-`Evaluate` planar grid, arbitrary plane, crossing error O(h²·curvature), shared cell-edge endpoints bit-identical so loops chain by exact equality), viewer side is `SectionContours`/`SectionContourRenderer` (plane passed as clip rule `(axis, offset)` — plane-general ahead of the section-axis generalization; recompute only on height/scene/visibility change, never per frame — and in the window on a **background task** (`SectionContourWorker`, generation-stamped like `TabMeshLoader` so a scene swap or superseding nudge never adopts a stale build; previous contours draw until the new ones land, offscreen stays inline one-shot), so first-enable no longer stalls the frame on marching squares + the first `TryGetSdf` lowering; lines lifted 1% of spacing below the plane so the shader discard can't eat them, depth-tested like feature edges); offscreen renders don't draw them yet (parity backlog item). Hard-won viewer lesson: **GLSL source strings must stay pure ASCII** — an em dash in a shader comment made ANGLE's translator reject the whole shader, the compile exception aborted `OnOpenGlInit` before the other programs were built, and the entire viewport rendered black. **Web viewer (Blazor WebAssembly, kernel in the browser)** — `src/EngrCAD.Web` (a Razor component library) + `samples/EngrCAD.WebDemo`. **The kernel compiles to WASM unmodified and produces identical geometry**: a flange with a 6-hole bolt circle and a filleted rim built in headless Edge gave 1 560 triangles, a closed mesh and volume 41 573.0 — the same as the desktop run to displayed precision, so there is no WASM-specific code path. Measured on win-arm64 from **clean publishes**, best of three headless-Edge page loads each running best-of-five builds, with the desktop control **interleaved into the same window**: **desktop 88.7 ms total (36.4 lower + 52.3 tessellate); WASM without AOT 1 677.3 ms (18.9×) at a 1.9 MB brotli payload; WASM with AOT 385.2 ms (4.3×) at 4.6 MB**. So **AOT buys 4.4× for 2.4× the download**, a per-deployment product call rather than a default — the docs deployment declines it because AOT compilation adds minutes to every docs build. **Our own code is about a fifth of the payload**: all nine EngrCAD assemblies are 1.14 MB uncompressed / 0.41 MB brotli against a 1.9 MB total, the largest single items being `System.Private.CoreLib` (1.53 MB) and `dotnet.native.wasm` (1.43 MB), so trimming our own code could win a few hundred KB at most. **Two rules the measurements cost us.** The interleaving is not fussiness: this laptop returns 88.7 ms and 185.7 ms from runs of the SAME Release binary, so a ratio across sittings is noise with units. And **only ever measure a CLEAN publish** — republishing over a previous publish without clearing `obj`/`bin`/output ships a runtime that disagrees with the assemblies, which does not fail the build: it reads 1.6× slow (2 765 ms for a row that is really 1 677 ms) and then aborts with `MONO interpreter: NIY encountered in method EngrCAD.Core.Vector2d:.cctor ()` (a red herring — that cctor is four `static readonly` struct fields). That corrupted artifact almost got the whole table "corrected" downward in the public docs; the tells were that only ONE row moved while the others reproduced, and that the demo's beacon had quietly stopped firing. **Re-verify the artifact before believing a number that moved when the source did not.** CI is immune (fresh checkout), so this is a local-iteration hazard, which is worse because local iteration is where numbers come from. **The demo is live on the docs site** (`docs/examples/web.md` embeds it; `.github/workflows/docs.yml` publishes it into `_site/live/`), which works because the published app is **path-portable**: `<base href="./" />` plus the already-relative asset references the build emits mean no `StaticWebAssetBasePath`, no post-publish rewrite and no repository name baked into the artifact. `?embed` strips the page furniture for the iframe. **The viewport now DRAWS** (`EngrCadViewport.razor` + `ViewportFrame.cs`): shaded geometry with per-part colours, ground grid and axes, an orbit camera, **feature edges, per-part display modes (Shaded/Wireframe/Translucent) and the global view style (Points/Wireframe/Shaded/ShadedWithEdges)** — plus, as of the parity-ladder pass: **section planes with picking parity and SDF isolines on the cut, the view cube, 3D annotations, a toolbar (Fit/style/Section/Annot/Props/BOM), the properties panel (`PartFacts`, gated on `Part.HasMesh` so it can never mesh a part the loader is still on), the BOM overlay, and — latest rung — the measure tool, exploded views, animation playback, multi-plane sections through to picking AND to the isoline overlay, and debug-modifier parity**. **Multi-plane isolines have full desktop parity now**: `RebuildContoursAsync` runs one `SectionContours.Build` per active plane (the desktop worker's loop verbatim), each `ViewportContours` value carries the plane it was BUILT for (the desktop's self-consistency rule), and the frame clips each plane's contours by its sibling planes — `SectionClip.Siblings`, always applied with Union — via per-draw uniform overrides packed exactly as the shared uniforms are, typed markers included (the interop lets a call's own uniforms win); a single plane has no siblings and opts out of the clip entirely, keeping single-plane output bit-identical, and a plane whose build found no SDF-routed parts keeps an EMPTY entry because its plane still bounds its siblings' cut faces. `SetSectionPlanesAsync` is the direct-call path; the `?report` beacon carries the quarter-cut relationships (`bodySectioned < bodyQuarter < bodyWhole`, and `0 < goldQuarter < goldContours + goldContoursY` — an unclipped overlay would draw both planes' full extents and break the second bound). The last three collapse to ONE mechanism: `Explode`, `Animation` + `AnimationTime` and the transport row all end at new matrices over buffers already uploaded (the browser's `SetInstancePoses`), legal because the instance count and order never depend on the factor or on t — and picking follows because the pick instances take the same matrices. The matching rule is extracted as the pure `ViewportFrame.PoseByPath`: poses match by occurrence PATH, not index, so a whole-scene track carrying instances this tab does not draw is ignored and an instance the track says nothing about keeps its DOCUMENT pose (index matching gets both wrong the moment a tab shows a subset, and the symptom — a part wearing its neighbour's transform — reads as a modelling error rather than a viewer bug); asserted as values, including that the explode SLIDER and an `ExplodeTrack` give identical matrices at factor 1. No playback logic is written in the browser at all — `AnimationPlayback` is the state machine and the front end supplies a timer (advancing by REAL elapsed time, so a throttled tab resumes at the right position) and three widgets.
