Times run on the finest dyadic grid so coarse/fine steps interleave and land on the endpoint exactly (over-tolerance rejects and refines; comfortably under coarsens). **Oracle pinned in two exact parts + one measured**: `Levels==1` reproduces `Solve` BIT-for-bit through the SHARED step (the whole per-step body extracted into one `NewmarkStep` both call, so no second spelling to drift) with the same factorization count; a damped decay whose amplitude falls ~1000× is matched to the uniform-fine reference to **0.008%** while taking **816 steps vs the fine grid's 1920 (58% fewer)** and factoring **4 matrices** (3 sizes + the mass solve) where a continuously varying step would factor ~1000. Tuning finding: the tolerance is ABSOLUTE (set it to the response's scale) and the split is dramatic only when the ring-down goes quiet (an under-damped run oscillates the whole time; ζ=0.12 makes the saving big). A prescribed support motion and an iterative solve refuse on that path by name
- **`Material`/`Materials` live in `EngrCAD.Core`**, not here: `EngrCAD.Modeling`'s `Part.Material` needs the same type (mass properties, the BOM, the default display colour) and Core is the only assembly both see. Analysis properties are optional (0 = not stated), so the refusals live at the point of use and name the property — `StructuralModel` on the modulus, `ThermalSolver` on conductivity/capacity, `ModalSolver` on density. Units are `ModelUnits`' mm/N/MPa/tonne/s
- **Stress recovery + an error estimate**: `StressRecovery.Superconvergent` (Zienkiewicz–Zhu patch recovery — vertex patches at INTERIOR corners, boundary nodes filled from the nearest patch by a breadth-first walk, local coordinates so the fit's conditioning is the patch's shape rather than the part's position) and `StructuralResults.ErrorEstimate` (per-element + global energy-norm error — the answer to "is this mesh good enough", which a solve otherwise never gives, and the map an adaptive loop would consume). `Direct` stays the default for `FeaSolveMethod.Direct`'s reason. **A patch never spans a MATERIAL INTERFACE, and the argument is REACH rather than smoothing** (design.md §3i): averaging is local, blending only the shared node, while a patch is fitted over every element at its node and written to every node of every one of them — so ONE cross-interface fit puts a ramp into nodes a whole element layer inside each material, nodes that touch a single material and have an unambiguous right answer (measured on a parallel bi-material cube whose exact stress is piecewise constant: **34 such nodes wrong, the worst by 92.9%**, all in that one layer and none beyond it, against exact to 4.5e-15 with the rule; the ZZ estimate reads **20.6% on a field whose true error is zero**, booking the manufactured jump as discretization error). So patches are assembled at corner nodes touching exactly one region, contributions accumulate per **(node, region) slot**, the boundary fill walks WITHIN a region (without that the exclusion is undone one round later, by extrapolating the other material's polynomial in), and an element reads its own material's values in the estimate; `AnalysisMesh` owns the slot table (`RegionsAt`/`InterfaceNodeCount`) so the averaging pass and the recovery ask one rule. **The decision the entry left open**: `NodalStress` keeps ONE value per node — a display field must — and averages the materials at an interface node, with the blending REPORTED (`AnalysisMesh.InterfaceNodeCount`, zero for a single-region mesh and for disjoint bodies) and the honest value reachable as `NodalStressIn(region, node)`/`NodalVonMisesIn`; per-region whole ARRAYS (the entry's guess) were weighed and declined — nodes×regions of storage where only interface nodes are multi-valued, and no answer for a node outside the region — as was (element, node) indexing, which is always single-valued but makes every caller iterate elements to ask about a node. Away from an interface the blend IS the per-region value bit for bit (the sum starts from the first slot rather than from a zero tensor, since adding a zero is not the identity on a negative zero), a region a node does not touch is refused by name, and a single-region mesh takes the same code path either way bit for bit — asserted through an internal `respectRegions` seam that also makes the defect measurable. **Two boundaries, both stated**: this fixes the RECOVERY and not `Direct` averaging, because for a node-indexed field there is nothing per-region averaging could change (only the interface node is affected and it must still publish one value — what `Direct` gains is the accessor); and a connected multi-region mesh is **not reachable from the public API today** (`TetMesher` refuses mating bodies, disjoint ones share no node, `TetMesh`'s region-carrying ctor is internal), so this is a precondition for conforming interfaces rather than a live defect, pinned at both ends by test. **The recorded two-material oracle could not see it**, which is the fixture finding: a SERIES bar's stress is `F/A` in both halves by equilibrium — the strain jumps and the stress does not — so a recovery reproduces it exactly whatever it does at the interface (measured: the two recoveries differ by 2.1e-13 of 62.5 MPa). The arrangement with a stress jump is PARALLEL, and the same nu = 0 device carries it: `u = (eps·x, 0, 0)` is exact with no body force, since the stress `(E_i·eps, 0, 0)` has zero divergence and the traction across the interface is zero on both sides (a nonzero nu would make `sigma_yy` jump, i.e. not a solution at all). One trap paid for: **prescribing that uniform stretch on a SERIES bar is not the series problem** — it imposes the same strain on both halves, which is the parallel condition wearing the series geometry, and the control fixture duly measured a 25.4% difference and reported that the series bar DOES see the rule; driving it by a force put it at 1e-15. A fixture named after an arrangement must be loaded the way that arrangement is defined, or it is the other arrangement. **The THERMAL twin landed on the same slot table and it is genuinely the smaller half** (design.md §3d; `ThermalResults.NodalFluxIn(region, node)`, `ComputeNodalFlux(perRegion)`): heat flux jumps at an interface for the mirror-image reason — temperature is continuous so the TANGENTIAL gradient is, and the tangential flux jumps with k while the NORMAL component is continuous by conservation — but there was **no recovery on the DEFAULT (`Direct`) thermal path** (an opt-in superconvergent one landed later, below), so the averaging has no REACH and the blend is confined to the shared node (checked rather than assumed: `Fields`/`SampleOnto`/`WriteVtu` read the nodal array, `ElementFlux` is per element, and `TemperatureIn`/`ThermalLoad` read TEMPERATURE, which is continuous). **The oracle inverts too**: a SERIES stack makes the flux purely normal, so both materials carry the same vector and the recorded 200 mW/mm² fixture cannot see it (measured 1.6e-11 of 2000) — the exact complement of the structural case, where series makes the STRESS continuous and the strain jump — while PARALLEL puts `T = T_hot·(1 − x/L)` in the element space with `q·n = 0` on both sides of the interface, so the interface condition holds identically and nothing is manufactured. Measured at k = 200/50 (exact flux 5000/1250 mW/mm²): per-region exact to **4.2e-15 linear / 2.1e-14 quadratic** at every one of 150/810 slots, while the one value the node-indexed field reports is wrong by **75% to 225%** for one of the two materials at every interface node. And §3i's fixture trap recurs verbatim and is MEASURED rather than restated — prescribing that linear field on the whole boundary of a series bar imposes the same gradient on both halves (the parallel condition wearing series geometry) and duly reports a 75.0% jump. **And a SUPERCONVERGENT flux recovery landed too — the same machinery §3i runs, at three components rather than six** (`FluxRecovery.Direct`/`Superconvergent` on `ThermalResults`, `Direct` the default; design.md §3d): `SuperconvergentRecovery.Recover<TValue, TField>` is monomorphized over a `SymmetricTensor3` stress and a `Vector3d` flux through an internal `IRecoveryField<TValue>` struct providing the sampling and the value arithmetic (`Add`/`Scale`/`Zero`/`FromComponents`), so the STRUCTURAL path stays BIT-IDENTICAL — the stress instantiation's `Add(a,b)` is `a+b` and `Scale(a,s)` is `a*s`, so the generic IL is the hand-written recovery's, asserted by the recovery's own exact-recovery/switch-back/convergence tests, all unmoved — while the thermal side got a recovery for free. **The convergence table it had to earn**: recovered flux rates **2.34 (linear) / 2.66 (quadratic)** against direct evaluation's 1.43/2.00, **15.3× / 7.8×** lower nodal error at the finest mesh, effectivity approaching 1 (0.979→0.998 from below linear, 1.035→1.020 from above quadratic), with `ThermalResults.ErrorEstimate` the ZZ figure over the scalar thermal compliance `1/k` (which is `k·|grad T|²` by another name), NaN where no interior corner node exists (`ThermalFluxRecoveryTests`). **The p+1 cap is now MEASURED, and the boundary FILL is the dominant cause**: the quadratic recovered rate settles at 2.76 (stress) / 2.66 (flux) rather than at theory 3, and an interior sub-domain norm study lifts the stress rate from **2.758** (whole domain) to **2.883** (central [0.25, 0.75] box, away from the fill) — closing about HALF the gap to 3, agreeing in direction with the recorded exclude-boundary-patches 3.08/2.91 — while the residual below 3 in the interior box is within a single last-pair rate's noise and does NOT establish a separate simplex-theory cap (the tetrahedral Gauss points not being tensor-product Barlow points, if it costs anything, is within noise of the fill's effect; `StressRecoveryTests.TheInteriorSubDomainRateSeparatesTheBoundaryFillFromSimplexTheory`)
- **Directional materials**: `ElasticLaw` (transversely isotropic / orthotropic / fully anisotropic, with a material `Frame3d`) SITS BESIDE `Material` rather than widening it — the deciding argument being that an anisotropic law needs a FRAME and a frame is not a property of the stuff but of how the stuff was laid into this part, so it is per-region analysis data; the `Material` still carries density and name, so a modal solve integrates the same density a BOM weighs the part with. `StructuralModel.SetElasticity(region, law)` is the whole API and every solver inherits it through `FeaAssembly`. **`ConductivityLaw` is the THERMAL twin, and being a separate type beside `ElasticLaw` (NOT a combined `MaterialLaw`) was the repo owner's call** — the two laws share the frame CONCEPT but not a frame VALUE (a laminate's conduction axes need not be its stiffness axes), so one object carrying both would make the name a claim it cannot keep, while a second per-region dictionary a `ThermalModel.SetConductivity(region, law)` fills is the shape `SetElasticity` already established. A symmetric PD 3x3 conductivity tensor (`FromMaterial`/`Orthotropic`/`Anisotropic`) rotated into global coords ONCE by a rank-2 CONGRUENCE `R·K·Rᵀ` — SIMPLER than the elastic Voigt rotation, no engineering-shear trap — so the flux is `q = -K·grad T` and a laminate conducts along its fibres. **The isotropic path is bit-identical**: every consumer branches on `IsIsotropic` (only `FromMaterial` sets it) — the conductance assembly to `k·∫grad N·grad N`, the element flux to `grad·-k`, the averaged/superconvergent recoveries and the `ErrorEstimate`'s complementary norm `q·K⁻¹·q` to their `|q|²/k` forms — asserted as a bitwise element comparison AND a bit-identical whole solve, with the general tensor path separately agreeing with the scalar form to round-off on a `k·I` tensor. SPD verified by a Cholesky NAMING the failing minor (heat up its own gradient is unphysical) and asymmetry refused by name (Onsager reciprocity); `FromMaterial` does NOT check, so the zero-conductivity refusal stays at the solve where it names the property. Verified exactly (a uniform gradient on a bar's whole boundary is a constant-gradient field in the linear-tet space): off-axis effective conductivity `kx·cos²θ + ky·sin²θ` reproduced to round-off (40/31.25/22.5/5 at 0/30/45/90°), and **CROSS-CONDUCTION** — the flux carries a component across the imposed gradient (q_y = −37.9/−43.8 at 30/45°, exactly zero on-axis), the thermal analogue of §3h's shear-extension coupling and the one behaviour no isotropic k can produce — with the oracle rotating the gradient into the material frame, applying the diagonal K and rotating the flux back, sharing no line with `R·K·Rᵀ`; a manufactured-order study with a fully off-diagonal rotated tensor keeps the orders unchanged (linear 2.01/1.01, quadratic 3.04/2.02 in L2/energy). Directional thermal EXPANSION is `ElasticLaw`'s already (`WithThermalExpansion`); a `MaterialLaw` merging the elastic and thermal laws is DECLINED by name
- **Cancellable**: every solve entry point takes Core's optional trailing `ProgressCancel`, and it is honest because `SparseCholesky.Factorize` honours it (per eliminated column) — adding it here first would have advertised a cancellation that cannot reach 99% of the work. `SparseSymmetricCG.Solve` takes one too, so the promise holds whichever method runs
- **Several load cases share one factorization**: `StructuralSolver.SolveAll(cases)` assembles and factors ONCE and substitutes per case — the direct solver's classic second argument, which this library could not honour until it existed. Measured 3.5–3.8× for four cases and 6.94× for eight; `Solve` is now literally `SolveAll([model])[0]`, so there is one implementation and the single-case answer is bit-identical
- **Fatigue post-processing**: `FatigueAnalysis.Evaluate(a, b, curve)` — S-N life and Goodman/Gerber safety factors over the two static extremes of a proportional load history (the `SolveAll` pair), arithmetic and not a solver. Signed von Mises decomposition (trace sign; the reversed-pure-shear blind spot is structural to ANY scalar equivalent and is the named boundary where critical-plane methods begin), `SnCurve`/`FatigueMaterials` with the endurance limit DERIVED from the line's own knee, and fields whose spellings are the design: infinite life = NaN, sub-cycle life floors at one cycle, life publishes as log10(cycles) because the colour mapping is linear (native log display filed). The safety factor is the radial load multiplier, verified by its own definition — scale the loads by it and the critical node lands ON the line
- **Variable-amplitude fatigue (rainflow)**: `Rainflow.Count` (ASTM E1049's three-point algorithm verbatim; the standard's Fig. 6 worked example transcribed cycle for cycle) + `FatigueAnalysis.Evaluate(TransientResults, curve, options)` — Miner's-rule damage over every node's signed von Mises history from a transient run's stored states, mean correction per counted cycle through the SAME `EquivalentAlternating`. The filed design questions answered by arithmetic: every node in one pass (a node's series is 8 bytes per stored state, small beside the fields each state retains — but the counting sees the STORED states, so a fatigue run stores every step), and the open end is an option (`AssumeRepeating`: one-shot residual halves per E1049, or one period rearranged to the largest-magnitude extremum with halves paired by exact key). Oracles: the total-variation identity `Σ2·count·range = Σ|Δ|` exact on a pseudo-random history, and a constant-amplitude history through the real `TransientResults` seam degenerating to the static pair BIT-equal (damage exactly count/life). Publishes damage per history pass + log10(repetitions) + the spectrum **load factor** — `FatigueAnalysis.LoadFactor`, the multiplier on the WHOLE history at which the counted spectrum reaches its damage target, with `RainflowFatigueOptions.DesignRepetitions` picking the target exactly as `FatigueOptions.DesignLife` does (null = infinite life, R = a Miner damage of 1/R; a knee-less curve is refused by name there for verbatim the static path's reason). **Whether a closed form exists is a property of the SPECTRUM and stating the boundary is most of the design**: damage is a sum of power-law terms, so if every equivalent amplitude were LINEAR in the multiplier the sum would scale as k^(−1/b) and the answer would be exactly `(R·D)^b` — but the endurance KNEE makes cycles JOIN the sum as k grows (piecewise coefficient; the closed form then overstates, measured 4.5%) and a tensile mean makes it `k·a/(1 − k·m/S_ut)`, not a power of k at all and divergent at `k = S_ut/m`, a hard static-failure ceiling the bracket meets naturally (understates, 12.9%). So ONE bracketed solve for every case and **the closed form kept as the test ORACLE**, where it agrees BIT FOR BIT on a knee-less curve and to two ulp on a steel spectrum above its knee — an implementation using it would have made those tests tautologies. **The infinite-life target IS closed form throughout, because it is not an accumulation**: each cycle reaches the endurance limit at its own static radial factor, so the spectrum reaches it at the smallest of them — literally `FatigueAnalysis.SafetyFactor` per cycle, one rule rather than a second formula, which is why a one-cycle spectrum answers bit-identically to the static pair and why the default costs one more pass over cycles that already exist (3.5 ms on a 135-node/241-state run against 18.2 ms, 5.2×, for a stated target). **The check with the most teeth is neither the closed form nor the apply-it oracle**: a spectrum of ONE cycle reaches damage 1/R exactly when that cycle's life is R, which is the static radial factor against `StressAt(R)` — the algebra reduces term for term — so a bisection over a Miner sum and a line intersection must agree for EVERY mean and both corrections, including the tensile-mean region where the general spectrum has no closed form (≤ 1e-9 across −100…400 MPa, above and below a factor of 1); beside it the apply-it oracle runs through the SOLVER (re-solve the history with every case scaled by the factor: the critical node's damage lands on 1.0000e-4 against a 1e-4 target), the infinite-life factor is bracketed from BOTH sides (damage exactly 0 a nanometre under, count/10⁶ over), and the factor is exactly inverse in the load scale. Two boundaries named rather than smoothed: the knee puts a genuine STEP in the damage function so `D(k) = target` has no solution when the target lands inside one (the crossing itself is reported; the step is the flat-line model's artefact, Miner–Haibach filed), and a node whose history never MOVES carries no counted cycle so it reads NaN however large its steady stress — rainflow measures CYCLES, and a steady load is the static pair's question
- **Marin factors**: `SnCurve.WithFactors(surface, diameter?, reliability)` over `MarinFactors.Surface/Size/Reliability` (transcribed verify-against-datasheet; the textbook worked values 0.798/0.858/0.814 ARE the transcription tests — a re-typed formula agrees with its own mistake, and the first run caught three hand-approximated values off in the third decimal). The corrected curve is a DERIVATION, never an edit and never a whole-line scale: the factors multiply the ENDURANCE LIMIT (initiation-governed, long-life), so the line re-fits through its own 10³-cycle point — Basquin's validity floor as the pivot — and `k·S_e` at the unchanged knee; S_ut untouched, exactly-1 returns the pristine object verbatim, and off-table reliabilities/out-of-data diameters refuse by name (the classical 0.6 large-shaft floor is an assumption this library does not make silently). **The knee-less (aluminium) correction landed** — `WithEnduranceFactorAt(factor, referenceLife)`/`WithFactorsAt(finish, referenceLife, …)`: a knee-less line falls forever, so the factor is applied at a STATED reference life (5e8 rotating-beam convention, REQUIRED not defaulted since the reference life is the claim), re-fitting through the same 10³ pivot and staying knee-less; verified as the identity that the corrected strength at the reference life is exactly `factor·pristine`, with a knee'd curve refused (its reference IS its knee). **Miner–Haibach landed** (`SnCurve.WithHaibachSlope()`): the flat endurance line is a constant-amplitude idea, so a derived mode continues it past the knee at the shallower slope `b' = b/(2+b)` (classical `k' = 2k−1`) — a sub-limit stress then has FINITE life (0.9·limit spectrum reads 1.125e-4 damage where the flat line reads exactly 0, the STEP in `D(k)` becoming continuous) and the plateau is gone (`HasEnduranceLimit` false), so the infinite-life factor requires a design life, the SAME rule aluminium earns (one rule not two); identical to the flat curve below the knee
- **Block Lanczos** (`ModalSolveOptions.BlockSize`/`BucklingSolveOptions.BlockSize`, default 1 = the incumbent scalar path byte for byte): b vectors per step carry a multiplicity up to b by construction — the scalar machinery recovers an exact double and provably stops one copy short of a triple, both now MEASURED on synthetic exact-multiplicity pencils since no mesh fixture can carry one
- **Topology optimisation (SIMP)**: `TopologyOptimizer.Minimize(model, TopologyOptions)` → `TopologyResult` — compliance minimisation over one density per element by optimality criteria, the answer to *where should the material go*. **The feature is the LOOP**: the assembly, the factorization and the element strain energy already existed, and the only change to a shared path is an optional per-element scale on `FeaAssembly.Stiffness` (null SKIPS the multiply, so every incumbent caller assembles the same bits). `DesignStudy` is the COMPLEMENT — it is derivative-free because a parameter change can alter topology, where here the topology changing IS the answer and gradients are mandatory. **`TopologyResult.Release` turns the field into a usable solid in TWO MEASURED stages** (`ReleasedTopology`/`TopologyReleaseOptions`/`TopologyReleaseStage`; docs `examples/fea-topology.md` now leads with the canonical **MBB beam**, which optimises into the iconic arch): the extracted iso-surface is stair-stepped at element scale and full of slivers (the MBB's 3 908 faces / 2 950 slivers / ≈0° min angle), so smoothing FAIRS it (`LaplacianMeshSmoother`) and remeshing RE-TRIANGULATES to a uniform edge length (`Remesher`) — and **every stage's cost is returned rather than hidden**. Smoothing is deliberately gentle (three steps at strength 0.1) because a stair-step is material on the convex side of the mid-surface, so fairing a THIN structure shrinks it: a full step melts thin members (measured **−42%** of the volume) where the default costs ≈**−6%** and moves the farthest vertex ≈0.73 mm, both reported; remeshing REDISTRIBUTES rather than moves (projection target = the smoothed mesh, so it stays within **2e-14** of it) and its whole benefit is triangle SHAPE — slivers **2 950 → 73**, mean smallest-angle **21° → 48°** via `TriangleQuality`, feature-angle detection OFF by default (the "feature detection reads the mesh you give it" lesson, since a tessellated blob's facets meet at large dihedrals). The order is smooth-then-remesh (fair, then redistribute onto the faired surface); the three stages (`IsoSurface`/`Smoothed`/`Remeshed`) are honestly-different outputs each with its own cost, the `Shape.Remeshed`-is-a-graph-node-not-a-hidden-knob argument. **`Release` returns a `HalfEdgeMesh`, not a `Shape`** — `EngrCAD.Fea` is a leaf that cannot see the modelling layer, so `Shape.From(released.Mesh)` is the one line back into the graph and thence `--export`/`EngrCad.Show`. Islands are REPORTED (`ComponentCount`) not silently deleted, with `TopologyReleaseOptions.KeepLargestComponentOnly` the opt-in that drops them (off by default; even on, `ComponentCount` reports what the extraction FOUND); verified by TRADES not pictures (`TopologyReleaseTests`) — the delivered solid is `Validate`-clean, closed, and round-trips through a real binary STL by signed-tetrahedral volume (catches a wrong winding or a hole), the two stage volume deltas add to the whole iso-to-deliverable difference exactly, and the MBB is checked by the property it MUST have (left–right SYMMETRY, which a wrong support breaks 3×) plus a fixed-`r_min` refinement study. **Passive regions and several load cases landed**: `SolidRegion`/`VoidRegion` (`Func<Vector3d,bool>` centroid selectors) PIN material solid or void as a per-element bound, dropped from the OC redistribution — pinning is EXACT (no filter, pinned elements end at 1/floor, whole-domain fraction still met to round-off) and a passive constraint can only RAISE compliance (it shrinks the feasible set; +54.8% for a hole through a cantilever's tension corner, +6.2% for material forced into the idle tip); `Minimize(cases, options)` minimises the weighted-sum compliance `Σ w_i·u_i'Ku_i` over several load cases sharing one factorization (`RequireOneOperator`, the `SolveAll` contract; single-model `Minimize` = `Minimize([case(model,1)])` bit-identical), the weighting being the caller's design decision (min-max filed), verified by SYMMETRY — two mirror loads each asymmetric alone combine equally-weighted into a mirror-symmetric structure (18% of a single case's mirror difference; residual is the Kuhn mesh's own asymmetry), a 3:1 weighting leaning the mass toward the heavier case. **Penalty continuation + symbolic-factorization reuse landed** (2026-08-09): `TopologyOptions.PenaltyContinuation` (opt-in, default OFF, bit-identical off path) ramps `p` from 1 to the target **holding at each level** (`PenaltyHoldIterations`) — the dwell is load-bearing, since a ramp with NO dwell reduces start dependence not at all (the convex phase never settles): on a multimodal MBB (frac 0.3, radius 5) fixed-`p` traps a top-biased start at compliance 85.99 against a bottom-biased 46.63 (two-start spread 0.202) while continuation reaches 46.70/46.68 from both (spread 0.001, 193× less, escaping the bad basin). And the loop reuses ONE `SparseCholesky.AnalyzePattern` symbolic across every iteration (the reduced stiffness's pattern is identical, only values change) — bit-identical to a fresh `Factorize` (a value gather into the same slots), a pure but BOUNDED speedup (1.13× per factorization at 1 152 elements, 1.02× at 10 800 where the numeric pass is the floor)
- Depends on Core + Mesh only; nothing depends on it (a clean leaf) — results leave as `MeshField`s, which is what lets the viewer colour-map them without referencing this project. Next: base excitation, several transient load patterns, and laminate theory + directional failure criteria on top of `ElasticLaw`

### Query layer (LINQ) — v1 implemented
- `SpatialCollection<T>` (items + bounds expression + BVH) with `AsQueryable()`: a custom `IQueryProvider` rewrites expression trees, recognizes `SpatialPredicates` clauses (`.Within(box)`, `.WithinDistance(p, d)`, `.HitBy(ray)`) applied to the registered bounds accessor, answers them from the BVH, and re-applies the full predicate over candidates (interception is a pure optimization). Residual/non-spatial queries fall back to LINQ-to-Objects. `LastQueryUsedIndex` is the diagnostic.
- IMPORTANT: query predicates use the by-value `SpatialPredicates` extension methods, NOT `Aabb.Intersects` — expression trees cannot contain calls with `in` parameters, which the kernel API uses.
- Topology-traversal LINQ exists on mesh handles (`vertex.OutgoingHalfEdges().Where(...)`, `face.AdjacentFaces()`, `Face.Bounds`).
- Future: metadata indexes for B-Rep feature queries (cylindrical faces by radius), expression-tree→SDF compilation.

## Planned solution layout

```
EngrCAD.sln
src/
  EngrCAD.Core/       math structs, tolerances, AABB, BVH, octree
  EngrCAD.Mesh/       half-edge mesh engine
  EngrCAD.Implicit/   SDF primitives, operators, evaluator
  EngrCAD.BRep/       parametric geometry + topology
  EngrCAD.Interop/    conversions between representations
  EngrCAD.Query/      IQueryable provider, spatial/topology LINQ
  EngrCAD.Fea/        tetrahedral meshing for simulation (Core + Mesh only)
  EngrCAD.Modeling/   unified Shape API (rep chosen at the end via To*)
  EngrCAD.Viewer.Core/ UI-free render model (shaders, camera, sections) - no Avalonia/Silk.NET
  EngrCAD.Viewer/     Avalonia + Silk.NET (OpenGL) viewer *library* (EngrCad.Show)
  EngrCAD.Web/        Blazor WebAssembly viewer component (WebGL2, kernel in the browser)
  EngrCAD.Mcp/        MCP server: a model program serves itself to AI assistants (--mcp)
  EngrCAD.Ecad/       code-defined schematics + connectivity model (Core + Modeling only)
samples/
  EngrCAD.Demo/       console showcase: builds a Scene, calls EngrCad.Show
  EngrCAD.WebDemo/    Blazor WASM host for EngrCAD.Web
  EngrCAD.LiveDemo/   live-modeling loop: EngrCad.Run + dotnet watch hot reload
tests/
  EngrCAD.Core.Tests/ (one xUnit test project per src project)
  ...
```

Kernel projects (`Core`, `Mesh`, `Implicit`, `BRep`, `Interop`, `Query`, `Fea`, `Ecad`) must stay free of UI/rendering dependencies; only `EngrCAD.Viewer` references Avalonia/Silk.NET.

## Performance mandates (non-negotiable in kernel code)

- Math types are `readonly struct`; hot paths allocate nothing on the heap
- Use `Span<T>`/`Memory<T>`; temporaries come from `ArrayPool<T>` or `stackalloc`, never `new` per call
- Use `System.Runtime.Intrinsics` (SIMD) for SDF evaluation, ray/primitive intersection, and other batch kernels
- Bulk mesh data uses structs-of-arrays, not arrays-of-objects
- Never compare floats with `==`; all comparisons go through the central tolerance policy in `EngrCAD.Core`
- **A sampling grid in PARAMETER space says nothing about coverage in MODEL space — and the safe way to fix that is to append, never to replace.** The marching tracer seeded both surfaces on a fixed 24×24 (u, v) grid. On an M8 thread band that is ~330 mm long and 0.16 mm tall (aspect ~2000) it puts columns 13 mm apart along the band while spending 24 rows across a strip a sixth of a millimetre wide — so a Ø6 cross-drill's entire window falls between two columns and the intersection returns **zero** branches (the flank band, six). `SurfaceIntersection` now runs a SECOND pass for surfaces past an aspect threshold, re-gridding at the same sample budget with `nu/nv = aspect` so spacing is equal in millimetres; the crest band returns three branches and the flank nineteen. **The isotropic pass still runs first, in its original order**, and `March` skips any seed already covered by a traced branch — so every branch the old grid found is traced from the same seed and comes back bit-identical, and the whole suite (3891 tests) plus every docs render is unchanged. That ordering is the entire safety argument for touching the tracer at all. Two sub-lessons: the extents must be measured as **mean |∂P/∂u| × domain length, never as a chordal polyline** (a chord between two samples of a coiled band measures ACROSS the coil — an 8-sample polyline read 61 mm where the band is 327 mm long), and the speed samples must be taken at cell CENTRES, since `Eval` clamps to the domain and a central difference at a boundary node is silently one-sided and halves what it reports.
- **A traced branch must TERMINATE on the boundary it was heading for, and the scope of that fix is the SURFACE PAIR rather than the seed.** The march breaks its step only AFTER the corrector has left the domain, so an open branch stops up to one whole step short of its rail. That is cosmetic on ordinary geometry and fatal on the surfaces the anisotropic seed pass exists for, because the step is scaled to the QUERY REGION and the band is not: an M8 crest flat is 0.156 mm tall against a 0.161 mm step over a 24 mm box, so ONE step crosses the whole band and branches came back spanning v = [0.481, 0.819] of a band whose rails are v = 0 and v = 1 — reaching NEITHER — while two-point branches were discarded outright. `TryLandOnDomain` solves the terminus as the tracer's own Newton with one coordinate PINNED at its boundary value (`Solve4` with the plane row replaced by `delta[k] = 0`, so the pin is bit-exact and the landing lies ON the rail); the seed comes from `PredictStep` rather than from the corrected parameters, because the corrector usually REFUSED the step — `Eval` clamps a non-periodic parameter, so a step past a rail evaluates the rail's own point, the partials across it collapse and the corrector fails on a singular pivot without the domain test ever running. **Scoping by SEED looks tidier and is wrong**: it leaves the isotropic grid's own branches on an anisotropic band ending strictly inside the face, and a cross-drilled M8 flank band still refused with six of them — the condition that hides a branch from the isotropic grid and the condition that makes the region-scaled step exceed the surface's width are the same condition, and it belongs to the surface. Measured: cross-drilling a threaded rod went from 0 of 13 bores to 8 of 13. **Two riders.** A candidate landing BEHIND the trace must be refused (`SnapTracerEnds`): on a thread band the two rails are a fraction of a millimetre apart, well inside the two-step reach, so a curve terminating exactly on one rail is in reach of the OTHER and appending it doubled the polyline back over itself (domain [0, 0.479] whose second half retraced the first) — and the incumbent "already on the boundary" test cannot catch that, because it reads the MINIMUM over candidates and is silent whenever the true landing's own Newton misses. And **halving the step on a refused interior step was built, measured and REVERTED**: it takes cross-drilling to 10 of 13 and it also reaches whole-solid FILLET bands, which are anisotropic too (long, and only `r·π/2` wide), breaking seven tests and taking the tilted-plane family from 1 of 4 to 0 of 4 — the standing rule that an algorithm able only to trade one refusal for another should not be reached at all.
- **A shared sampling rule may only be changed MONOTONICALLY, and "the tidier rule" is exactly where that bites.** `SampleEdge` gave the `segmentsPerCircle` density only to CLOSED angular edges, so an open one — a circle or an ellipse cut into arcs by a boolean, which is what every split rim is — fell to `curveSamples` and carried the SAME count at every density: a FLOOR, since raising the density then refined the grid around such a rim and never the rim itself (measured on a threaded rod's end-chamfer cone, whose three spiral cuts scaled 5/9/17/33 while the cap circle's arc sat at 25 at 32, 64, 128 AND 256). Replacing `curveSamples` with the angular count is the rule the closed case already uses and it makes the DEFAULT density measurably worse, because at 32/24 a sub-half-turn arc is finer under `curveSamples`: a partial revolve's tessellated volume stopped matching its exact closed form (**2.35451265 → 2.35146969**, a discrete identity turned into an approximation), a slot pocket left its stated chordal-error band, and **19 of 632 Interop tests moved**. Taking the MAXIMUM removes the floor (the chamfer strip's worst facet-vs-surface agreement 0.9935 → 1.0000 at 256) and moves exactly ONE test, the one documenting the floor — because no edge anywhere gets coarser, which is the whole safety argument for touching a rule two faces share. **The residual it does NOT fix is worth keeping as a boundary**: the same strip is 0.1301 against a floor of 0.8315 at 32/24 because its four boundary edges are sampled by two DIFFERENT rules (the helical family's pure angular count against `curveSamples`), so the strip zips a 25-point chain against a 5-point one and fans — and the filed fix, "a density rule measuring a trimmed face against its OWN uv extent", is not expressible, since an edge polyline is sampled once and SHARED by both its faces and that sharing IS the welding invariant. Equalizing the two rules upward was measured and is a trade rather than a fix: it moves the 0.1301 onto the thread BAND as 0.5204 and triples the mesh (4450 → 15772 facets at 32/24).
- **A cross-representation check taken at the VERTICES cannot see a change confined to a RULED face.** Verifying a thread runout against `Sdf.Thread`'s own field, every B-Rep vertex reads |sdf| ≤ 1e-9 — and so does every vertex against the field of the rod with NO runout, at 2.826e-15, which would have passed a runout that was never cut. The runout cone is a `RevolvedSurface` over a STRAIGHT generator, so `NaturalSteps` collapses its v to one cell (a v-chord lies exactly on the surface) and the face contributes no interior vertex at all: every vertex it does contribute sits on its boundary spirals, which lie on the untouched thread flanks, or on the end plane, where the field is zero for either rod. The discriminating sample is the facet CENTROID, which is exactly on the cone for the same reason the face is ruled, and reads −0.19. The general form: **a vertex-sampled oracle is blind to any face whose own tessellation invents no interior point**, which is precisely the exact ones.
- **A nearest-first branch and bound is already its own seed.** Seeding `Bvh.Nearest` with the previous coherent sample's answer (distance is 1-Lipschitz) is provably result-identical given strict `>` pruning and a strictly-greater seed — and buys only **1.12–1.20×** on the most coherent run available, plus a small net *loss* on scattered probes. Descending the nearer child first already reaches a tight bound in O(log n) node tests, so a seed can only save part of the first descent. `MeshSdf` therefore keeps the base class's scalar batch loop, and `MeshSdfBatchTests` pins batch == scalar bit for bit so the next attempt is held to the same bar.
- **Never benchmark an optimization against a baseline you wrote differently.** A prototype of that same seeding measured 1.88×; its baseline went through a `Func` delegate per triangle while the seeded path called the kernel directly. The entire gap was the delegate.
- **An exact predicate's escalation path is hot exactly when the input is STRUCTURED — "escalation is rare" is a claim about the wrong workload.** `Predicates3d.InSphere`'s exact stage ran on `BigInteger` under the recorded reasoning that the cost was confined to the escalation path; but a CAD tessellation's vertices are exactly cospherical BY CONSTRUCTION (every UV-sphere and every cylinder), so the mesher escalates 3.5 times per tet on a sphere and the exact stage was **58% of the mesher's total allocation** (275 of 479 MB). Rewritten as the SAME visibly-the-determinant integer evaluation over sign-magnitude `stackalloc` `ulong` buffers (pooled past a ~1000-bit exponent spread; per-tier word bounds proven from the determinant's degree, not guessed; min exponent over NONZERO coordinates only, since a zero's −1074 exponent would widen every operand ~1000 bits while a positive scale cannot change the sign): **9 125 → 515 ns and 5 698 → 0 bytes per escalated call; `TetMesher` on the r10 sphere 478.7 → 25.8 MB, the h=2 box 191.6 → 37.7 MB** (win-x64, Release, escalation counts identical). Two riders: the exact stage stays locked against the test-side `ExactReference` BigInteger ground truth — an INDEPENDENT cofactor expansion, so agreement is evidence, not tautology — over lattice-sphere/ulp-grid/subnormal/pooled-path families with the pooled fixture asserting via `InSpherePooledEscalations` that it still CARRIES the configuration; and the timing is a MINIMUM over interleaved runs, because the same binary measured 515–2 290 ns under a loaded machine and a mean would have reported whatever the neighbours were doing.
- **A packet query's shared bound is governed by its group's DIAMETER — and the batch seam delivers collinear ROWS, not compact blocks.** The other lever on `MeshSdf`'s 74–85% `Bvh.Nearest` cost was a packet: one traversal per coherent group, per-point pruning at the leaves. Built and measured (`MeshSdfPacketBenchmark`) against the production `Bvh.Nearest` with the same struct metric: a compact 2³ block of grid points wins **1.45×**, the same 8 points laid out as a z-consecutive row **0.86×**, and 64 of them — spanning 1.9 units on a 3-unit model, so the shared bound becomes the whole model and the packet degenerates into a brute-force scan — **0.30×**. A node is visited whenever it could beat the *worst* member's best, so spread kills it. `Sdf.Evaluate(points, distances)` is a flat span every bulk consumer generates z-fastest, and `MeshSdf` cannot regroup a collinear run into blocks; teaching the batch contract to carry "these points form a compact block" is a large API change to buy a win in a shape no caller produces. Tie-breaking never became the question. Two negatives ride along: seeding the packet from one exact query at the group's centre changes nothing and makes the best case *worse* (the nearest-first lesson again), and pruning on **squared** distances throughout — removing a `Math.Sqrt` from every box and triangle test — is 0.94–0.99×.
- **A continuation needs a complete seed set; the only sound way to prove one complete is a cull; and the cull has already produced the visit set, so the continuation adds nothing.** That is why `SurfaceNets` visits a shell around the surface by *culling* rather than by seeding and flooding (`SurfaceCull`). The argument needs one property and no epsilon: an `Sdf` here is 1-Lipschitz, so every point p of a block with centre c and half-diagonal R satisfies |d(p)| ≥ |d(c)| − |c − p| ≥ |d(c)| − R, and |d(c)| > R therefore rules out a sign change anywhere in it. Cell and quad loops keep their exact (j, k) order and only skip runs of tiles, so output is **bit-identical** including face ordering. Two implementation notes: the x/y dilation must be computed per row rather than baked into a stored mask (dilating a block per axis costs a block of extra samples on three sides of the surface — most of what the cull just saved), and the one-cell slack on the radius is cushion for blends, which are only approximately Lipschitz, not part of the argument.
- **An 8× saving in the dominant cost bought 2.5×, because the dominant cost stopped being dominant.** The cull evaluates 28.4% / 15.6% / 12.0% of the grid at resolution 96 / 192 / 256 and buys 1.60× / 2.18× / 2.46× (a `MeshSdf` field, 2.99× at res 96). Measured at res 256 with a field costing one square root per sample, the whole call still takes 132.8 ms against 129.3 ms for the real CSG field: evaluation is now free and the cost is assembly — `HalfEdgeMesh.Build` alone is 39–48%. **Re-measure where the time goes after every win**, or the next optimization goes into the part that no longer matters.
- **The half-edge builder's twin resolution is a COUNTING SORT, and the grid's contribution was the flat buffer, not the adjacency.** Following the finding above into assembly: the cost was a `Dictionary<(int, int), int>` probed twice per directed edge (over a million tuple hashes at resolution 256) plus one `int[4]` heap allocation per quad. Both are gone — every directed edge is filed under `min(from, to)`, so an edge and its reverse share a bucket that holds only one vertex fan (three to six entries), and ONE scan of it finds the non-manifold duplicate and the twin at once; buckets fill in ascending half-edge order, so "already seen" is a `break` and the scan observes exactly what the dictionary probe observed, error-detection order included, which is what keeps every exception message identical. Measured interleaved in one sitting: assembly **3.3–3.7×** (47.5 → 13.4 ms at 129 268 vertices), whole polygonization 1.2–1.5×, allocation at res 256 **145 → 103 MB**, output bit-identical with the golden fingerprints untouched. **Two things worth keeping.** The plan was "build the half-edge structure from the known grid adjacency, keeping the generic `Build` as a debug cross-check" — but the grid does NOT hand over twins for free (a dual edge is a grid FACE, and matching its up-to-four claimants needs an O(n³) face table the streaming window cannot hold), whereas the counting sort is O(n) with no hashing and serves every caller in the repo. Making the generic path fast beat making a second special one, and it left ONE implementation instead of two-plus-a-cross-check. And the honest closing number is the SHARE, not the speedup: assembly went 38–51% → 15–18% of the call, so the builder has stopped being the place to look and the remaining ~175 ms of a 213 ms res-384 polygonize is the grid walk itself.
- **A contract that holds for the inputs you tried is not a contract, and "manifold Surface Nets" was one of those.** `SurfaceNets.Polygonize` is documented as the MANIFOLD variant — one vertex per connected component of a cell's inside corners, the rule that exists precisely to stop thin sheets and saddles going non-manifold — and it still threw `Directed edge 4954 → 4967 appears twice` out of `HalfEdgeMesh.Build` on `Sphere(16).Lattice(Gyroid(12, 1.2))` at resolution 88. **The gap is Marching Cubes' AMBIGUOUS FACE**: a grid face whose inside corners are exactly a *diagonal* pair has all four of its edges crossing, so it carries two quad edges between the same two cells; when BOTH cells join that pair into one component, the two quads land on the same directed edge. That is the ONLY way a duplicate can arise — two instances share a vertex pair, hence a cell pair, hence one face, and a face with just two crossings gives its two instances opposite directions — so the fix is exact rather than a heuristic: test each interior face once, from its + side, and where both cells join, split that component **by the outside blob each crossing reaches**. The split provably always exists, because a cell can never join both an ambiguous face's inside pair AND its outside pair (a path between the inside pair must use an off-diagonal corner of the far face as *inside*, while a path between the outside pair needs both of them *outside*). **Three things worth keeping.** The per-cell map had to be re-keyed from inside CORNER to cube EDGE, since one corner's two crossings can now land on different vertices. **The conservative version of the same fix was 17× larger and moved a golden**: triggering on the cell's own ambiguous face without consulting the neighbour split 204 cells instead of 12 and changed the `torus` fingerprint, where the exact trigger leaves every golden fingerprint and every rendered docs PNG byte-identical — the difference between "fix the broken configuration" and "fix everything that looks like it". And **whether the configuration lands is ALIGNMENT, not tolerance** (the torus-silhouette-pinhole lesson again): the reported lattice fails at resolution 88 and at none of the other nine tried, so the regression test is a family sweep over fields *and* resolutions, with each fixture asserted to still CARRY the configuration so it cannot quietly stop testing it. The finding that settles what the bug was about: a plain `Sphere(10).Shell(0.6)` at resolution 44 reproduces it with 240 such faces. A one-cell-thick wall is the cause; lattices were just where it was noticed.
- **A cell's inside component can bound SEVERAL SHEETS, and giving it one vertex PINCHES the mesh there — the residual the ambiguous-face fix left, and a defect on ordinary models.** The filed entry called this configuration "manifold, so not this bug"; it is a non-manifold PINCH vertex, and once `HalfEdgeMesh.NonManifoldVertices()` existed to count them it measured **984 on `Sphere(10).Shell(0.6)` at resolution 44**, 528 at 56, and up to **3 066 on `Box(10) & Gyroid(8, 0.2)`**. The mechanism is a one-cell-thick wall again: the material is one connected blob while the void inside and the space outside are two, so six crossings form two triangles and averaging all six puts both on one point. **The rule the backlog proposed for it — one vertex per (inside blob, OUTSIDE blob) interface — is NOT the surface-component count, and the counterexample is the ordinary four-isolated-corners cube** (inside `{0,3,5,6}`, Marching Cubes' case 8): every one of its twelve crossings is its own (inside, outside) pair, so that rule returns twelve vertices where the surface is four triangles. The correct refinement is the cube's own **FACE adjacency** — two crossings on a common face are the two ends of one arc of the surface's cross-section there — restricted to one inside component, as a union-find over the twelve cube edges; the interface rule coincides with it only when no face is ambiguous. **It is a 256-entry TABLE, and that is not a micro-optimization**: the partition is a pure function of the eight corner SIGNS, and computing it per cell measured **1.15–1.51×** on the whole polygonization (a thin shell at res 128 67.6 → 80.8 ms, csg at res 128 20.3 → 30.6) where the table measures 1.01–1.07× — the general shape being that a per-cell rule over an 8-bit input is a lookup, and pricing it before deciding is what turned a visible cost into none. It only ever splits, so a cell whose every component is a single sheet is bit-identical, and the blast radius says the trigger is exact: **all three golden fingerprints untouched, 133 of 135 rendered docs PNGs untouched**, and the two that move are the two gyroid figures that carry the defect (339 and 315 pixels of 1.79 million, 0.019%, each inside one small box, the change being that the dark specks the pinch points rendered as along a hole's rim are gone), with FACE counts unchanged (a pinch is a point, so opening it moves no material — `Sphere(16).Lattice(Gyroid(12, 1.2))` at 88 goes 99 722 → 99 746 vertices against the same 99 846 faces). **What is left is the ambiguous-face split's ONE-SIDEDNESS** (the + cell owns the test, so the minus cell keeps one vertex against its neighbour's two and pinches instead): 984 → 240, 2 768 → 642, and one gyroid family barely moved at 1 728 → 1 686. **Two closures were built and both measured WORSE, which is the institutional-memory half**: resolving the ambiguous face by the **asymptotic decider** (the bilinear saddle's sign, `a·c` against `b·d`, which both cells read off the same four doubles) and **cutting** an ambiguous face's pairing wherever the cube already links its two arcs. Both drove the pinch count to zero on every fixture AND both returned open meshes and bow-tie vertices on the same family — because **a cell's grouping has to match what its NEIGHBOURS do, and a face-local pairing rule does not make two different cube interiors agree**, which is the general lesson: in dual contouring the vertex-splitting rule is not a per-cell decision even when it is written as one.
- **A polygonizer that AVERAGES its crossings chamfers every sharp feature BY CONSTRUCTION, and no resolution removes it — the fix is the field's own gradient, and the fixture that hides it is the symmetric one.** Every crossing of a cell lies ON the surface, so their mean lies strictly INSIDE any convex corner: `Sdf.Box(10,10,10)` polygonized put its nearest vertex **0.72 / 0.38 / 0.22** model units from the exact corner at resolutions 16 / 24 / 32 and did NOT converge (0.048 at 48, 0.22 again at 64 — the miss is a property of the averaging rule's alignment with the grid, not of the sampling density). Dual contouring with Hermite data fixes it exactly, because a box corner is the intersection of three planes and the quadratic error function of three independent planes has a unique minimiser: every vertex now reads EXACTLY zero from the box's own field and the volume is exactly the box's, at every resolution, at any placement, and to 4.4e-12 rotated off every axis. **Four things are worth keeping.** (a) **The solve is a REGULARISED NORMAL-EQUATIONS solve and that is not a compromise**: writing the samples as rows of N, `A = NᵀN` is symmetric PSD by construction so its SVD IS its eigendecomposition, which `SymmetricEigen3` already computes with no second implementation, and the usual objection that normal equations square the condition number is bounded because the rows are UNIT normals — κ(A) is a function of the ANGLES alone, and the truncation is exactly the mechanism that answers an ill-conditioned angle by declining to resolve it. Rank IS the feature classification (1 = plane, 2 = edge, 3 = corner) and every unconstrained direction falls back to the MASS POINT, the incumbent averaged vertex, which is what makes this a strict refinement rather than a different algorithm. (b) **The threshold is an ANGLE, derived rather than chosen** — two unit normals separated by α give A the eigenvalues 1 ± |cos α|, so the singular-value ratio is exactly `tan(α/2)` — and the direction of the risk picks the default: raising it returns the incumbent chamfer, lowering it inverts a direction the samples barely constrain and throws the vertex a long way out, a worse mesh against a broken one. (c) **The Hermite POINT is the crossing PROJECTED ONTO THE SURFACE, and a symmetric fixture cannot see that it is not.** A grid crossing is where the field's LINEAR INTERPOLANT along a cube edge vanishes, which is on the surface only where the field is linear along it — and at a feature it is not, since a box's field near a corner is a MAX of three linear pieces, so an edge whose inside sample is governed by a different face crosses zero in the wrong place and the plane built through it carries the wrong OFFSET. One Newton step along the gradient (`p − d/|grad| · n̂`, bounded by one cell) fixes it exactly wherever the field is locally linear, i.e. every planar face and every hard-CSG corner. It hid completely because a box sharing its centre with the sampling region puts its corner at the same fractional position on all three axes, and only then does the linear crossing land on the surface by itself: the symmetric fixture read EXACTLY zero while an asymmetric box read 2.6e-2 and an offset one 3.5e-2 — the recorded "a mirror-symmetric hostile fixture can be secretly benign" lesson in a third costume, and the reason the committed test is a THEORY over three placements. (d) **Where a vertex may go is a measured bound and BOTH textbook answers are wrong**: clamping to the strict cell chamfers a ROTATED box's edges by a quarter of a cell (0.141 / 0.109 / 0.048 at 32 / 48 / 96, converging only linearly) because a cell that sees both faces of an edge need not CONTAIN the edge, so the minimiser on the edge LINE is legitimately just outside and refusing it there chamfers precisely the feature the quadric found; while no clamp at all is unbounded, an under-resolved gyroid throwing a vertex **4.3 cells** out past its neighbours' neighbours. Half a cell is measurably not enough (4.3e-3). One cell — the neighbourhood a cell's own crossings can speak about, i.e. a fit's own data rather than an extrapolation past it — measures 4.4e-12 on every box placement AND bounds the gyroid. **And the strongest available verification is structural rather than numerical**: placement changes WHERE a vertex goes and never which crossings belong to which vertex, so the index buffer handed to `HalfEdgeMesh.Build` is bit-for-bit what it always was and manifoldness is preserved BY CONSTRUCTION — including the recorded pinch-vertex residual, whose count this neither creates nor repairs. The golden fingerprints were split to say exactly that: a TOPOLOGY hash asserted for BOTH placement rules from one row, and POSITION hashes per rule with the pre-feature numbers kept verbatim. Cost 1.6–2.1×, FALLING with resolution (the extra work is per crossing and crossings are a surface quantity); 15 of 151 docs PNGs moved, every one SDF-routed and none B-Rep-only.
- **Adaptive contouring is BOTTOM-UP here, and that is a completeness decision rather than a convenience.** The textbook version is a top-down octree that stops subdividing where the field looks flat, which would save the sampling as well as the faces — and it cannot certify that no feature hides between the samples it took, which is verbatim the argument `SurfaceCull`'s own remarks make against seed-and-flood ("the only sound way to prove a seed set complete is a cull"). Collapsing cells the uniform walk HAS visited inherits that argument unchanged and adds no new one; the cost is stated rather than implied, namely that it saves faces and everything downstream of them and saves nothing in evaluation. What it buys: a box collapses to **SIX QUADS** with its volume still exactly 1000 (a flat region is one plane at any size, so the collapse is provably lossless and spends none of the tolerance), a drilled box at resolution 64 goes 12 008 → 1 160 faces for 0.03% of volume, and a smooth-blend CSG fixture falls 3.3× at resolution 48 and **14.7× at 256** — the saving grows with the grid because the surface it is describing does not. **Two properties are structural and one is checked, and the split is the design.** Cracks are impossible because the connectivity is the uniform walk's face buffer RE-INDEXED and never re-derived, so there is no T-junction to make; the neutral settings are the identity BIT for bit, which is what makes the compaction's original-vertex-order numbering load-bearing rather than cosmetic (first-use order would renumber a mesh nothing was done to). Manifoldness is the one thing argued from data rather than from construction — contracting a connected set of dual vertices is a manifold quotient only when its induced subcomplex is a DISK, which a cluster of cells need not be — so each octant's members are split into connected COMPONENTS first (the same rule, one level up, that gives a cell one vertex per sheet) and any cluster implicated in a repeated corner, a duplicate directed edge or a pinched vertex link is REVERTED. That loop terminates for a stated reason: reverting only ever splits vertices apart, and splitting cannot create a violation — two directed edges that were distinct cannot become equal by un-merging — so every round strictly removes merges and the empty clustering is the original, valid mesh. **The honest caveat is that the tolerance means less on curvature than it says**: two adjacent nearly-tangent planes have a tiny quadric residual whatever the merged point does along their near-intersection, so what actually bounds the damage on a sphere is the cluster's own bounding box rather than the stated length (measured, 0.018% of volume at a tolerance of 0.001 cells). Filed.
- **A field that is not 1-Lipschitz breaks three consumers at once, all in the direction that DROPS geometry silently — so the operator must declare its constant rather than the consumers assume one.** `SurfaceCull`, the narrow-band octree and `SdfProjectionTarget` all reason *a value of |d| proves there is no surface within |d| of here*, which is true only when the field changes no faster than the point moves. A twist, a bend and a taper shear space, so it is false by exactly their largest singular value; unwidened, each of the three would quietly skip a block, a node or a step containing real surface. `Sdf.LipschitzBound(region)` is that factor, the three widen by it, and **it is 1 for every field that existed before**, so multiplying by an exact 1.0 leaves the incumbent behaviour bit-identical (verified the only way it can be: **no committed docs PNG moved**). Three riders. It takes a REGION rather than being a scalar because a twist's local factor grows with distance from the axis, so no finite constant is valid over all of space — a consumer knows what it is about to sample and asks once, and an infinite answer means "cull nothing", which is always correct. **Overriding it is mandatory for any node that wraps a child, even a 1-Lipschitz one**, because the failure mode is not a wrong formula but a MISSING override — a wrapper that says nothing claims 1 for a twisted subtree — so the test buries a twist inside every wrapper in the catalogue and includes one built to forget, asserted to be caught. And **it surfaced a pre-existing gap nobody had reached**: a SAMPLED GRID is measurably steeper than what went into it, by up to √3, because each first difference of the trilinear interpolant spans one cell so each partial inherits the source's bound and all three can reach it at once — attained rather than merely permitted, since baking `max(x, y, z)` (1-Lipschitz) onto the unit cell gives `1 − (1−x)(1−y)(1−z)`, whose corner gradient is exactly (1, 1, 1). So `Sdf.Sampled` had been violating the assumption the cull rests on; nothing in the repository baked a grid and then polygonized it, which is why it never showed.
- **An oracle whose own error is comparable to the effect is not an oracle — and the tell is that it convicts the case you know is exact.** Measuring the ellipsoid approximation's error started with a dense scan over the ellipsoid's own parameterization, refined by bisection. It reported a **86% relative error for a SPHERE**, where the formula reduces algebraically to `|p| − r` and is exact: near the surface the scan's resolution error swamps the true distance it is dividing by. The exact oracle is a closed-form condition instead — the closest point satisfies `q_i = p_i r_i²/(r_i² + λ)`, so `Σ p_i²r_i²/(r_i² + λ)² = 1` is ONE scalar equation, strictly decreasing, bisectable to machine precision with no seed — and with it the sphere row reads 0.000 and the real result appears: the approximation is a **genuine lower bound OUTSIDE** (reported/true = 1.000 / 0.983 / 0.916 / 0.675 / 0.544 / 0.238 at aspect 1 / 1.25 / 1.67 / 3 / 4 / 10, never above 1) and **over-reports DEPTH inside** (1.000 / 1.081 / 1.369 / 2.076 / 2.673 / 6.585). That distinction is invisible to a test that only bounds |error|, and it is the one that matters: outside-lower-bound is the engine's contract, inside-over-report is why the projection target divides its step.
- **A published closed form is a claim about a solid, and it may be a different solid than the one you asked for.** Quilez's `sdPyramid` is the standard square-pyramid distance and it is exact — for the pyramid **with its base removed and its lateral faces extended downward**. It computes the distance to the lateral surface and uses the base plane only for the SIGN, so wherever the base FACE is the nearest feature it reports the lateral distance: measured on a 10-wide, 12-tall pyramid, **5.831 against a true 3.0** directly below the base centre, and −4.423 against −0.5 just inside it. Both are OVER-estimates, the one direction this engine cannot absorb. The tell was that it matched an "infinite pyramid" oracle exactly at the same points it missed the real one by 90% — so the formula was not buggy, it was answering a different question. `Sdf.Pyramid` therefore takes the minimum over its own six boundary triangles through Core's `Distance3d.ClosestPointOnTriangle` (the shared rule, asked rather than restated): six Voronoi-region tests where a closed form would cost one, and the price of a field that means what the rest of the engine assumes.
- **A bound derived by a triangle inequality can be loose in a way no substitution fixes, and the repair is to keep the COMMON FACTOR instead of splitting.** The ellipsoid's Lipschitz bound was `2 + (rmax/rmin)²` from two independently worst-case substitutions, and it reported **3 for a SPHERE**, whose field is exactly `|p| − r`. What the split throws away is that every component of `∇V` carries the SAME factor `w_j = p_j/r_j²`, so `|∇V| ≤ k1·max_j |…| = max_j |(2 − μ) + u(μ − 1)|` with `u = 1/k0` and `μ = ρ²/r_j²` — and for equal semi-axes μ is `r²/r²`, the same double divided by itself, so the u-term vanishes IDENTICALLY and the bound is 1 with no special case. Being bilinear, its maximum over a rectangle of ranges is at a corner, so a region's own k0 range costs four evaluations. Measured/reported goes 1.00 / 0.67 / 0.45 at aspect 1 / 1.11 / 1.25 against 0.33 / 0.29 / 0.28. **And the derivation's stated regime turned out to be a real restriction rather than a formality**: the field is genuinely DISCONTINUOUS at the centre of a non-spherical ellipsoid — `V(t·d) → −|Ad|/|A²d|`, which is −rmax down the longest axis and −rmin down the shortest, measured −10 and −1 a nanometre from the centre of a 10×1×1 — so no finite constant covers a region containing it, `u` is capped at the regime rather than reporting infinity, and what the consumers rest on there is the weaker property the field does keep (its magnitude never exceeding a bounded multiple of the true distance). A secant test's exclusion should then BE that regime rather than a proxy for it: `|p| ≥ rmin/2` is a sufficient condition for a different statement and admits points down to k0 = 0.05 at aspect 10.
- **A guard's precondition can be NARROWER than the prose suggests, and checking which is how a filed defect turns out not to be one.** `Displace`'s `Bounds.Expanded(amplitude)` was filed as "right only where the child's magnitude is a true distance", with a CSG difference near its tool's fictitious faces as the standing counterexample. The property it actually needs is `{child < t} ⊆ Bounds.Expanded(t)` — the child never reporting LESS than the per-axis escape from its own bounds — which the whole CSG family satisfies BY INDUCTION: an exact primitive gives at least its own escape, a union's minimum is at least the escape from the union of the boxes, and an intersection's maximum is the per-axis maximum of its operands' escapes, which IS the escape from the intersected box. Measured 0.0 over fifteen nodes including that difference and a tangent-sphere intersection. What genuinely breaks it is the other family: a NON-isometric domain operator widens its bounds relative to the field it reports, and `Sphere(1).Taper(1, 3)` reads **0.667 at a point 1.0 outside its box**. So the answer was an explicit-bounds overload plus a catalogue-wide measurement of the property, not a caveat.
- **A premise measured on the WRONG quantity reads plausibly and protects nothing — and a certified bound costs one line more than a sampled one.** The strut lattice''s three-wide neighbourhood is complete because a copy two cells out is at least one whole cell away while the nearest strut the window DOES visit is nearer than that. The first test measured the distance to the query cell''s OWN struts instead, which is a sufficient condition and not the one the code relies on: face-centred cubic read **0.9875 cells** — passing, with 1.25% of margin — while its true own-cell figure is exactly 1.0 at the far corner, because its cell deliberately carries only the three LOW faces'' diagonals and the corner is covered by the NEIGHBOUR''s copy. Measuring the field''s own distance-to-axis instead reads what the argument needs, and adding half the sampling grid''s cell diagonal back (a distance function is 1-Lipschitz) turns the grid scan from evidence into a certificate. The companion lesson is that a mutation test can be RIGHT to fail: dropping every neighbour changes nothing at all for simple cubic and BCC (their cells are symmetric about the centre, so a neighbour always TIES), so "the guard must be shown to fire" became a per-kind claim rather than a blanket one.
- **A symmetric round leaves TWO spellings of one lattice point, and a deduplication cannot see it.** Folding a generated Kelvin cell''s struts by `cell·Math.Round(mid/cell)` leaves a midpoint at +cell/2 where it is and one at −cell/2 where it is; both are the same point modulo the lattice, so the two survived as distinct keys and the unit cell came back with **36 struts where the bitruncated cubic honeycomb has 24**. The fold has to be HALF-OPEN — `cell·Math.Floor(mid/cell + 0.5)` — and the tell was a count with a known closed form (2 polyhedra × 36 edges ÷ 3 sharers), not a picture: 36 struts renders as a perfectly convincing Kelvin foam.
- **When four lanes want four different lists, make the lanes AGREE — do not pad the lists and do not gather.** The strut lattices' batch path was filed as blocked by a data-dependent candidate list, with two candidate shapes: pad every sub-cell's list to the longest and evaluate the union, or gather per lane. Padding throws away exactly the pruning that made a query affordable (up to 648 segments against a handful), and a width-agnostic gather does not exist for `Vector<double>` and would spend six scalar loads a lane a candidate to save a few flops. The third shape is a counting sort: partition the batch by sub-cell, then walk each bucket's points a register at a time with the STRUT broadcast as a scalar, so the struts become constants and the points become the vector. Measured **2.0–2.8×** over the six kinds; the partition is O(n) against an inner loop tens of candidates deep, so it is a few per cent. Bit-identity comes from the fold and the bucket index going through one rule both paths ask, and from `Vector.Min`'s ±0 tie-break being unable to reach a result whose every quantity is a sum of squares squared again.
- **Warm every case before measuring ANY, or the first row measures the tiering.** Warming per case inside the measurement loop is the shape that looks careful and is not: the first case measured eats the promotion of both arms. Simple cubic read **0.95× cold and 2.01× properly warmed** on the strut batch A/B — and 0.95× is exactly the number a plausible causal story wanted (three struts a cell, short candidate lists, "the partition must cost more than it saves"), so a gate was nearly derived to exclude the case that did not need excluding. A measurement artefact in the first row is the one most likely to be mistaken for a property of the input.
- **Where a prune is DONE decides the cost; per query it can be worth nothing.** Answering a strut lattice by scanning its 27-copy neighbourhood cost 0.9–4.7 µs a sample, enough to turn one polygonization into minutes. Two per-query strategies were built and measured and both lost: a running-minimum prune over the struts'' own boxes is barely better than no prune (4.1 against 4.7 µs on Kelvin), because every CELL''s box is the whole cell — its struts span it by construction — so the cheap outer test is nearly vacuous; and a BVH over the block wins on the big sets and LOSES on the small ones (0.90 against 0.44 µs for simple cubic), so it is not a uniform answer either. Pruning ONCE, at construction, into per-sub-cell candidate lists is: 132–429 ns a sample across the six kinds, 7.1×/4.6×/6.1×/5.6×/3.0×/2.9× the BVH through the same production entry, against a gyroid sheet''s 75 ns. The selection is exact rather than heuristic — the distance to a segment is CONVEX, so its maximum over a box is attained at a corner, and the smallest such maximum over the struts bounds how far a point in that sub-cell can be from the whole block.
- **A direct-vs-iterative crossover measured on ONE operator is a measurement of that operator's conditioning, not of the two algorithms.** `SparseCholesky` vs `SparseSymmetricCG` was benchmarked on grid Laplacians **plus an identity shift**, which is strongly diagonally dominant — so CG converged in an *n-independent* ~35 iterations at every size, and the recorded verdict ("past ~14k unknowns one-shot CG wins") was really "CG wins on a well-conditioned system", which is not news. Drop the shift for a pure Dirichlet Laplacian, the regime an FEA stiffness matrix lives in, and at 62 500 unknowns CG needs **858 iterations / 750 ms** against AMD-ordered Cholesky's **221 ms factor + 5.8 ms per solve** — the direct solve wins on the FIRST right-hand side, by 3.3×, the exact opposite conclusion from the same code at the same size. Vary the CONDITIONING as well as the size, or the table describes a fixture. (AMD itself: 4.6–13.4× on factor time, 3.5–8.3× on fill, never a loss — but opt-in, because a fill-reducing permutation changes the summation order and every committed upstream number was measured natural.) **The FEA work then confirmed this rule by contradicting the verdict**: on 3D elasticity over tetrahedra, Jacobi-preconditioned CG beats AMD-ordered Cholesky in three of four cases at a few thousand unknowns (37/153/334 ms against 65/524/342), and the unblocked up-looking factorization takes **79 s** for a 46 800-DOF system against 323 ms to assemble it — a third operator, a third answer. AMD's own leverage grows the other way: **11.3× less fill and 191× faster** (19 383 964 → 1 723 544 entries, 64 834 → 340 ms) on a 6 552-DOF quadratic system, so the ordering is what makes second-order FEA possible at all while the factorization itself is the remaining wall.
- **Which lever to pull on a slow algorithm is a MEASUREMENT of its structure, not a property of its name — and for sparse Cholesky the obvious lever is provably the wrong one.** `SparseCholesky.Analyze` reads the deciding numbers off the symbolic pass (5–520 ms where the factorization is 0.1–134 s): stored entries of L, the numeric pass's exact update count, the longest column, and the heaviest root-to-leaf path of the elimination tree. On 3D elasticity the **parallel ceiling — total work over critical path, i.e. what a perfect schedule on unlimited processors with free synchronisation could win — is 1.0× natural and 1.6–1.9× AMD.** Tree parallelism cannot pay, and the reason is structural rather than incidental: in 3D the top separator's columns are a constant fraction of all the work and they form a CHAIN, so there is almost nothing to overlap. The same table says where the work went instead — the longest column is 1 400–3 748 entries under AMD — which is exactly what a supernodal BLAS-3 kernel exists for, so blocking is the lever and scheduling is not. Two riders: the update count predicts factor time at **~1.0–1.3 ns/update** across a 15× size range and both element orders, so a caller can be told the cost before paying it; and `Analyze` shares the symbolic pass with `Factorize` rather than restating it, because a prediction that disagrees with the run is worse than no prediction.
- **An asymptotic guard justified by the caller you had is not a guard, and a new consumer is exactly the event that invalidates it.** `SparseMatrixBuilder.Pack` stable-sorted each row with an INSERTION sort, defended in its own comment by "assembly rows are short (a vertex's ring), so the quadratic worst case never bites" — true of the mesh Laplacians the class was written for, and false of the 3D FEA assembly that is now its heaviest consumer, where a 10-node tetrahedral mesh puts **612 raw entries** in its worst row (every element touching a node contributes 30 columns to each of that node's rows). Measured with the old sort transcribed verbatim and the two alternating in one sitting: **1.31× / 1.30× / 1.89× / 7.25×** at row lengths 6 / 24 / 90 / 612, and end to end a quadratic FEA assembly's packing went **250 ms → 31 ms** and stopped growing superlinearly with the entry count. The replacement keys each entry as `(column, add-index)` packed into one long, so an ordinary primitive sort puts duplicates in add order and **stability stops being an algorithm choice** — output is bit-identical because a stable sort's dedupe sums the same terms in the same order. Two riders: packing a pair into a long for SORTING is sound where packing one for HASHING is the recorded `long.GetHashCode == lo ^ hi` trap (a comparison reads all 64 bits); and nothing got slower, including at the vertex-ring length the guard was written for, which is what made it a free swap rather than a trade.
- **Measure the PHASES before parallelising the loop — the loop's shape is not its cost.** FEA assembly looks embarrassingly parallel per element, and the backlog said so; measured, **computing the element stiffnesses is 6–18% of assembly** and the rest is the scatter into the builder, which is a shared write whose ORDER decides the last bits of every summed entry and so cannot be parallelised without giving up bit-identical output. Assembly is itself ~4% of a quadratic direct solve and ~13% of a large iterative one, so a perfect parallelisation of the parallelisable half is worth under 2% of a solve — declined with the number, and the shape that would be correct (per-block builders merged in BLOCK order, which reproduces the serial add sequence exactly) recorded rather than built. The same measurement retired the item's second half: the reaction pass does recompute every element stiffness, and the whole pass is 5–18 ms, so caching it would spend 95 MB on the largest linear case to save single-digit milliseconds. What the measurement DID find was one layer down (the insertion sort above), which is the general shape of these: the honest phase split moved the work somewhere nobody had proposed.
- **A progress fraction must count WORK, and whether that differs from counting iterations is a property of the operator rather than of the algorithm.** `SparseCholesky.Factorize` now takes a `ProgressCancel` and reports the numeric pass's exact inner-loop update count — available for free, since the symbolic pass has already counted every column of L and column j is used by exactly as many rows as it has off-diagonal entries, so the total is `Σ c_j(c_j+1)/2` before the first multiply. The interesting half is the measurement that the obvious justification is only sometimes true: on a **dense** factor the halfway column has spent 12.5% of the arithmetic (cubic accumulation), so column number would read 50% and be wrong by four times — but on a **banded** one, which is exactly the natural-ordered 2D grid Laplacian this class was written for, the two agree at 0.468. So the re-weighting is free where it changes nothing and load-bearing where it does, and the regression test uses the dense fixture on purpose while recording the banded number beside it. Two riders: the accumulator sits behind the null check because it is the innermost loop in the library, and reports are throttled to 200 steps because a factorization has as many columns as unknowns and a UI repainting 46 800 times would cost more than the arithmetic it narrates. `SparseSymmetricCG.Solve` deliberately reports NO fraction — an iteration count is not progress, since the residual falls at a rate nobody knows in advance and the cap is a stall detector.
- **A shared rule can be the WRONG rule for a new consumer, and the boundary layer is the one place `PolygonFan` must not be asked.** The repo's standing answer to "which way does a quad split" is the shorter 3D diagonal with a relative tie guard — right everywhere it is used, and precisely wrong for a boundary-layer prism, because a layer quad on a flat wall is an exact RECTANGLE whose two diagonals are mathematically equal. So the tie guard would fire on essentially every element of the stack rather than on the 408-of-960 minority it was written for, and the choice would fall to round-off; two prisms sharing a quad could then split it differently, which is non-conforming, and every solver would integrate over a gap it cannot see while returning a plausible answer. The rule there is combinatorial (Dompierre: the diagonal contains the smaller INPUT-SURFACE vertex index — symmetric in the two, so neighbours agree without communicating). The general lesson is not "prefer combinatorial rules": it is that **a shared rule's justification travels with it, and a consumer made entirely of that rule's documented degenerate case is a consumer it does not serve.**
- **The party that decides an interface's triangulation is the one that must be ASKED, not predicted.** The boundary layer's first design built the offset wall, handed it to the isotropic fill, and built prisms against the triangulation it had handed over — which is wrong on a box, because recovery works per planar PATCH exactly so that Delaunay may pick its own diagonal across a coplanar quad. Measured: 16 orphan boundary faces on a 20³ box, 266 on a sphere. The fix is to run the stage in two passes and read the interface triangulation BACK off the finished fill; the fill chooses, the stack conforms.
