Same shape as the `ExactSampleParameters` findings — restating what another stage decides is how all of these went wrong — and the payoff is that the conformity check becomes free: weld by exact position, and every interface triangle is then used by two elements and vanishes from the boundary, so **"every boundary face has a known tag" IS the statement that the two meshes conform**, with no second check to keep in step.
- **A quality report that cries wolf on correct output is worse than no report — but the partition that fixes it has an honest limit, and the limit must be stated.** `TetQuality`'s sliver rule (minimum dihedral below 10°) calls every legitimate boundary-layer element degenerate: a tetrahedron cut from a prism 0.15 mm thick and 1 mm wide has a minimum dihedral under a degree and a radius-edge ratio in the tens, and it is exactly right. So elements are partitioned by measured stretch and each half gets the rule that means something for it. Two sub-lessons. **A full whitening is a vacuous quality measure**: `M^{-1/2}` maps every non-degenerate tetrahedron to a well-shaped one, so scale only the THINNEST principal axis (up to the middle eigenvalue) — that restores a layer element while leaving a needle exactly as bad. And **no local geometric measure can separate a layer element from an accidental sliver**, because the two are AFFINELY EQUIVALENT — the stack element is four nearly-coplanar points too. What distinguishes them is whether the thin direction is shared with the neighbours and with the physics, which is intent, not geometry. Hence `AnisotropicCount` is reported BESIDE the stretched quality rather than instead of it: on a mesh nobody asked to be anisotropic, any value above zero is the finding.
- **A degeneracy guard must ask the assembly's OWN arithmetic, not restate it — the "one shared rule" lesson, in a solver.** The FEA solver refuses an element whose Jacobian is non-positive in double precision, because such an element contributes a stiffness of the wrong SIGN rather than an inaccurate one. (They exist: the tet mesher guarantees positive orientation by the EXACT predicate, and a sliver can satisfy that while its floating-point volume underflows — measured, two of 31 214 on a 100×10×10 beam.) The first version tested the CORNER triple product `(b−a)×(c−a)·(d−a)`; assembly integrates the **isoparametric Jacobian**, which is the same mathematical quantity by different arithmetic. They disagree in the last bits, so elements passed the guard and were then integrated as exactly zero — surfacing as a 10-node mid-edge node with **no stiffness at all**, on an element whose corner volume read a healthy 1.2e-15. Restating a test is how all three occurrences of this got it wrong (`ExactSampleParameters`' two, now this); ask the same code.
- **`MaxElementSize` is a MINIMUM element size, not a bound on the element count — measured, and the honest change is a report field, not a refusal.** Filed as a hypothesis (a coarse `MaxElementSize` might not bound the mesh in the presence of a fine curved feature) and settled by the experiment the todo named — `Box(60,20,8) − Cylinder(4,40)`, bore held fixed, `MaxElementSize` swept: the element count is NON-MONOTONE (83 897 → 68 550 → 142 911 → 90 537 → 101 557 for h = 20/14/10/8/6, then 340 508 at h = 4) where a size-bounded mesh has count ∝ h⁻³, so `count·h³` FALLS from 671 M to 22 M rather than staying flat, and even the coarsest request (h = 20) gives 84 k elements, 66× a uniform edge-20 mesh. The cause is that `RefineBoundaryToSize` only SPLITS a boundary facet larger than the target and never coarsens a finer one, so where the Ø8 bore is tessellated to ~0.5 mm facets the SURFACE, not `MaxElementSize`, sets the local element size; the mesh is BOUNDARY refinement (bSteiner 151 968 against qSteiner 1 424 at h = 4), whose density the surface fixes, with interior quality refinement contributing ~0 for h ≥ 10. A REFUSAL would be wrong — the mesh is correct, merely finer than a "coarse" request implies — so the change is `TetMeshDiagnostics.MinBoundaryFacetSize` (the finest boundary facet's circumradius, the surface's own floor), which a caller compares to its request and, when the gap is large, coarsens the SURFACE tessellation rather than raising `MaxElementSize`. The observability half was already met: every mesh and solve entry point takes a `ProgressCancel` honoured down to the Delaunay build and the refinement loops, so "40 silent minutes" is a caller passing no deadline, not a missing seam. Same shape as `TetQuality`'s cries-wolf rule — the mesher answers a request literally and the honest report is what says the request could not mean what the caller hoped.
- **B-Rep face provenance is a BY-PRODUCT of tessellation, not a second pass — so feeding the tet mesher from the model costs no change to the tessellation.** `TetMesher` takes a `HalfEdgeMesh`, so B-Rep face identity reaches its boundary-condition tags (`FacetTags`, keyed by `TetFacet.SourceTriangle`) only if the caller does the "which triangle is on which face" bookkeeping the `BrepQueries`/`FaceRef` selector vocabulary exists to avoid. `MeshWelder.WeldPolygons` gained a tagged overload that rides a per-polygon tag onto the surviving faces (welding drops no non-degenerate polygon and reorders no face), so `BRepTessellator.TessellateWithProvenance().Mesh` is BIT-FOR-BIT `Tessellate`'s output and `FaceProvenance[f]` names the face mesh-face f came from; `TessellateForTetMesh` is the whole bridge — a triangulated mesh plus per-TRIANGLE tags, the triangle-per-face count read from the welded faces' DEGREES alone (the diagonal choice never enters, both triangles of a quad sharing a face), so the mesher's own `Triangulated()` is a no-op that keeps the tags lined up with `SourceTriangle`. The oracle is bit-identity twice: the mesh equals a plain `Tessellate`, and a structural solve whose support and load are named by `Facets.Tag(faceId)` is bit-for-bit the same solve named by a geometric selector — because both resolve to the same facet set — with a drilled-plate test that the bore wall tags to the one cylindrical face and the caps to their planes. The general shape: exposing a mapping a stage already computes internally beats making the caller reconstruct it, and "bit-identical to the untagged path" is the check that the exposure changed nothing.
- **A convergence ORDER measured against a different model's answer stalls at the modelling difference.** The cantilever's tip deflection converges at a measured 1.86 for BOTH element types, which looks like a formulation defect and is not: the built-in end carries a stress singularity that caps the rate whatever the element. The theoretical rates only appear on a problem with no singularity, which is what the method of manufactured solutions is for — choose a smooth u, put it through the elasticity operator analytically for the body force that makes it exact, prescribe it on the whole boundary, and both orders then land on theory (2.00/1.00 linear, 3.03/2.02 quadratic in L2/energy). Two riders: the manufactured field must be CUBIC or a quadratic element reproduces it exactly and reports an infinite order; and the body-force load needs a degree-5 quadrature rule, because under-integrating a load caps the study at the QUADRATURE's order and looks identical to a bad element.
- **A quadrature rule that gets the TOTAL right can still produce a SINGULAR matrix, and the total is the check everyone reaches for.** A capacity (mass) matrix integrates `N·N`, degree 2p, two degrees above the conductivity/stiffness's `grad N · grad N` at 2(p−1) — so the rule that is exact for one is not exact for the other, and this is the shape of error that hides. Integrating a 4-node capacity with the conductivity's one-point centroid rule makes every one of its sixteen entries exactly `ρcV/16`: a rank-one matrix that cannot be factored, **whose entries sum to exactly ρcV**, the body's true heat capacity. Measured spread between entries: 0. So "does the capacity sum to the right thing" — the obvious sanity check, and the one a reviewer asks for — passes it. **The modal work generalized the failure and found the control that actually has teeth.** An n-point rule can only produce a matrix of RANK n, so the stiffness's rule gives a 4-node mass of rank 1 and a 10-node mass of rank 4 (measured, both), while `Σ_ij N_i N_j = (Σ_i N_i)² = 1` exactly makes the total right *whatever* the rule — the pass is structural, not luck. And the control is not "compare against a higher rule" but **the ROTATIONAL INERTIA**: a rigid rotation is linear in position, so every element order represents it exactly and `uᵀMu` must equal the tetrahedron's own `ωᵀIω`, which `MeshMassProperties`' closed-form polyhedral moments supply from another project entirely. The production rule agrees to **2.2e-16…9.4e-15 relative**; the one-point rule reports **−2.4e-27 against a true 1.4e-10** — no rotational inertia at all, because every entry being equal collapses `uᵀMu` to the square of the MEAN nodal value and the mean of a rotation about the centroid is exactly zero. A body with the right mass and no inertia would spin up under any torque and put every torsional frequency at infinity.
- **An estimator that cannot estimate must say UNKNOWN, and the arithmetic's answer is the dangerous one.** The Zienkiewicz–Zhu error estimate is the energy-norm distance between the element stress field and its superconvergent recovery — but where no patch can be assembled (a mesh with no INTERIOR corner node), the recovered field IS the finite-element field and that distance is the distance from something to itself: measured on a 24-element box, **9.9e-15 against a true error of 0.313**. Reporting the number would call the mesh perfect on precisely the mesh too coarse to assess, which is the worst direction for a mesh-quality indicator to be wrong in, so `RelativeError` is **NaN** there (the spelling `HarmonicResponse.TruncationError` already established for "not small, unknown") and `FallbackNodes` counts the partial case so a recovery that quietly did not happen cannot look like one that did. Two more findings from the same work: **the boundary caps the RATE and the signature is a HALF order** — including one-sided boundary patches made the quadratic sequence read 2.77 then 2.47, drifting AWAY from a theory of 3, and excluding them put it on 3.08/2.91, because a boundary layer is an O(h) volume fraction so an error one order worse there enters a global L2 norm as `h^(p+1)·√h`, which is distinguishable from a formulation defect (a whole order) by exactly that half; and **excluding them is not the end of it**, since the nodes near a box's edges and corners then get no value at all (228 of ~2 000) and those are where a peak stress sits, so they are filled from the nearest patch by a breadth-first walk rather than left out. **And which of the two candidate caps dominates the residual 2.76 (not 3) is a MEASUREMENT, not a redesign** — the boundary FILL (its extrapolation, second-order in distance) versus the SIMPLEX superconvergence theory being weaker on tetrahedra (the Gauss points are not the tensor-product Barlow points). An interior sub-domain norm separates them: restricting the recovered-error norm to a central box away from the fill lifts the quadratic stress rate from **2.758** (whole domain) to **2.883** (central [0.25, 0.75] box), closing about HALF the gap to 3 and agreeing in direction with the exclude-boundary-patches 3.08/2.91 — so the **fill is the dominant cause**, and the residual below 3 in the interior is within a single last-pair rate's noise, not a separate simplex cap. **The same estimator now serves heat flux** — `SuperconvergentRecovery.Recover<TValue, TField>` is monomorphized over a `SymmetricTensor3` stress and a `Vector3d` flux, keeping the structural IL bit-identical (`Add(a,b)` is `+`, `Scale` is `*`), and the flux recovery's quadratic rate lands at the same-shaped **2.66** below theory 3.
- **A manufactured field must be manufactured, INCLUDING the load that makes it exact — and the case needing no load is the one that hides the omission.** A recovery consistency test prescribed a quadratic displacement field on the boundary with no body force; a quadratic field is not a solution with zero body force, so the solve returned something else and the test reported a 20% "recovery error" belonging entirely to the fixture. Its LINEAR twin passed from the start precisely because a linear field's second derivatives vanish and its body force really is zero — so the half that works is the half that cannot catch the mistake. Same family as the graded-conduction-mesh and stationary-probe traps.
- **A set of PAIRWISE bounds is not the condition it is usually quoted as, and the honest test is the factorization itself.** The classical orthotropic material restrictions are quoted as `|nu_ij| < sqrt(E_i / E_j)` per pair, plus "a determinant condition" that gets dropped; measured, three equal moduli at `nu = 0.7` satisfy every pairwise bound (`sqrt(1) = 1`) and still give a negative determinant, i.e. a material that RELEASES energy under some strain. `ElasticLaw` therefore refuses by attempting a Cholesky of the compliance and naming the leading minor that failed — the Cholesky **is** the statement "positive definite", where checking the bounds one at a time re-derives it in a form easy to get subtly wrong, and without it the symptom is a factorization failure deep in the solver rather than a message about the material (`BrepBoolean.Verified`'s argument, one layer up). Two riders: the same routine serves a 3x3 block living inside a 6x6 array and a whole 6x6, so it must be TOLD its **stride** rather than infer it from the order — inferring gave a perfectly valid isotropic compliance a "not positive definite" refusal, a shared helper reading the right numbers in the wrong places; and the MINOR Poisson's ratios are derived from the compliance symmetry `nu_ji/E_j = nu_ij/E_i` rather than accepted as inputs, since supplying both is how a datasheet transcription comes to contradict itself and the contradiction would make the matrix non-symmetric with nothing to catch it.
- **A transformation whose two halves use different conventions must be DERIVED, and its oracle must not share the derivation.** Rotating a constitutive matrix is `D_g = K D_m K'` with K the Voigt STRESS transformation — the K-on-both-sides form (not `K D K⁻¹`) following from the strain vector transforming by `K⁻ᵀ`, which itself follows from the strain energy `s'e` being invariant. Two lines, worth doing, because the engineering-shear convention makes stress and strain transform by DIFFERENT matrices and swapping them leaves a symmetric, positive-definite, entirely plausible matrix. So the verification oracle is built by **3x3 tensor rotation** instead — rotate the stress into the material frame, apply the compliance there from the engineering constants, rotate the strain tensor back — sharing nothing with the production path but the physics, with the classical off-axis modulus formula as a third reading of the same number. And the assertion with teeth is the **shear-extension coupling** (−7.9e-4 at 30°, exactly zero on axis): it is the one behaviour no isotropic law can produce, and the one a transposed rotation would lose while leaving every symmetric measure intact.
- **A single-node restraint is a STATIC idiom and it is wrong in dynamics.** A 3-2-1 scheme pins isolated degrees of freedom to remove rigid motions, and in statics that is sound: Saint-Venant confines the disturbance to the pinned node's own neighbourhood. In a modal solve the same pin creates a GENUINE mode — the whole body translating along the released direction while a few elements around the node deform — whose frequency is set by the mesh rather than by the part. Measured on a simply-supported 100×12×8 beam whose axial translation was pinned at one node: a spurious mode at **5 540 Hz**, sitting between the second and third bending modes, carrying 96% of the axial effective mass, and duly failing a comparison against the second bending frequency by 26%. The fix is a constraint the wanted modes satisfy IDENTICALLY rather than one that merely happens to be small: holding `u_x = 0` along the beam's own centroidal line removes the axial family and adds no bending stiffness, since pure bending has `u_x = −z·w′(x)` measured from the neutral axis. Same shape as the thermal fixtures' lesson — a restraint, like a fixture, can quietly change what is being measured.
- **Ritz values converge from the outside in, but NOT in lock step — so lock a contiguous prefix, never "whatever has converged".** Shift-and-invert Lanczos can have the second eigenvalue converged while the first is still moving, and accepting the converged ones in whatever order they arrive then returns eigenvalues 2 and 3 as "the two lowest". Measured on a 19 440-DOF cantilever asked for ONE mode: **4 997.9 Hz returned for a first bending mode of 834.9 Hz** — the second bending pair, a number entirely plausible on its own. Two further rules from the same work: full reorthogonalization is what stops ghost duplicates AND what makes a genuine multiplicity invisible (a single-vector Krylov space holds one vector per eigenspace, and a square shaft's two identical bending modes are the common case), so converged modes must be locked into the deflation set and the run restarted, targeting one more mode than asked for so a missed copy has a run to appear in; and the scratch buffer holding the next Lanczos vector's `M`-product must not be shared with the Rayleigh–Ritz step, which cost a whole iteration breaking down after exactly four steps rather than a wrong answer. **The block-Lanczos landing added the trap the prefix rule cannot catch: an unconverged COPY of a repeated eigenvalue hides HIGH in the spectrum** — its Rayleigh quotient mixes toward the complement, so it surfaces past the next DISTINCT eigenvalues, the walk legitimately accepts those, and a +1 target fills while a copy is still buried (a block of four on a synthetic quadruple stopped at `{1, 1, 1, 2}` believing itself done, four iterations short of its own budget). The extra targeting must scale with the vectors advanced per step — wanted + b, reducing to the incumbent +1 at b = 1. Two block-start lessons ride along: a block must NOT reuse the scalar start-pattern family (piecewise affine in the index with one shared slope, so a block's projections onto a coordinate-concentrated eigenspace are structurally near rank-deficient however the seeds are chosen — an LCG stream instead), and the stream must be CENTERED (components in [0.5, 1.5) put every column near all-ones at pairwise cosine ~0.9, starving the block's later principal directions by a measured factor 20).
- **When the right-hand matrix goes indefinite, the free parameter is the METRIC, not the shift.** The modal solver puts a small negative shift under the spectrum because `K − σM = K + |σ|M` is then positive definite — which works ONLY because M is positive semi-definite. A geometric stiffness is indefinite (tension stiffens, compression softens, and one bending prestress does both), so no sign of σ makes `K − σ·Kg` reliably factorable, and the escalation loop is worse than inapplicable: multiplying a failing shift by 100 pushes it FURTHER from definiteness. The resolution is that `A^-1 B` is self-adjoint in the A inner product AND in the B inner product whenever A and B are symmetric, so Lanczos may run in either and the only requirement is that the one it runs in be definite. In buckling that is **K**, so the operator is `K^-1(-Kg)`, the factorized matrix is K itself (literally the static solver's factorization, on every model), and **the shift is exactly zero on every model** — because the substitution θ = 1/λ has already turned the wanted eigenvalue into an extreme one, which is the entire job shift-and-invert exists to do. ARPACK's "buckling mode" reaches the same place from the other side (`OP = (A − σM)^-1 A` with metric `B = A`) and REQUIRES σ ≠ 0, since at zero that operator is the identity; inverting the other matrix removes the requirement along with the choice. `LanczosEigen` needed exactly one generalization — the metric became a parameter separate from the right-hand matrix — and passing one matrix for both keeps the modal path bit-identical.
- **A residual that is a TOTAL cancellation has a floor of `eps·κ(K)`, and whether you hit it depends on whether the operator SMOOTHS.** Both eigensolvers measure `|Kφ − λBφ|/(|Kφ| + |λ||Bφ|)`, which at convergence is the round-off left by two nearly equal products. A modal solve escapes the floor because its Krylov vectors come out of `K^-1 M`, which suppresses the high-frequency content K amplifies before K ever sees it; a buckling solve's operator is `K^-1 Kg` and a geometric stiffness is derivative-like, so the two halves roughly cancel in frequency content and the vectors keep exactly the part that sets the floor. Measured on one slender column: every mesh up to 9 310 DOF reaches 1e-10 and a **23 166-DOF one stalls at 1.76e-9**, so the modal 1e-9 default would refuse an ordinary refinement — hence a 1e-7 buckling default, which costs nothing (an eigenvalue is accurate to the SQUARE of the residual over the gap; the same column accepted at 1e-5 returns 15 437.12 N against a finer mesh's 15 437.99 N). And **an empty eigen-result has two unrelated causes** — nothing wanted exists, or a candidate was there and the tolerance was in the way — which want opposite responses from a user and which an empty list cannot distinguish, so `LanczosEigen` reports the best candidate it did NOT accept and both solvers' refusals name which case they hit. **The follow-up question — does a K⁻¹-norm residual remove the floor — is now MEASURED, and the answer generalizes: a measure that escapes a cancellation can do so by ceasing to measure.** `|K⁻¹(Kφ − λKgφ)|/|φ|` does escape (8.2e-11 where the standard measure stalls at 1.9e-9, near-constant across refinements where the standard floor grows with κ(K)) — because it IS the shift-invert operator's residual, the very quantity full reorthogonalization drives to round-off — and for exactly that reason it saturates at its own ~1e-10 floor within a dozen Lanczos steps while the standard measure still reads 1.2e-7, so past that point it cannot tell a 1e-7-grade vector from a 1e-9-grade one. The eigenvalue drift between the earliest and most converged acceptance is 1e-15…5e-13 relative, so a tighter default had nothing to buy; the 1e-7 default and the backward-error residual stand (`FeaBenchmark.WhetherAKInverseNormResidualEscapesTheBucklingFloor`).
- **A verification fixture can make a convergence study measure nothing, and it does not look broken while it does.** Two occurrences in one session, both caught only because a measured order came out impossible rather than merely poor. **(a)** Grading a hollow cylinder's rings GEOMETRICALLY puts the nodes at equal intervals of `ln r` — the coordinate the exact conduction profile is linear in — *and* makes every ring-to-ring conductance equal, since each annular ring is a radially scaled copy of the last and a 2D conductance is scale-invariant. The exact nodal values then satisfy the discrete equations identically: 4.3e-14 on a 120 K drop at every refinement level, and a "convergence order" of **−2.50 then −1.27**, which is what the ratio of two round-off figures looks like when mistaken for a signal. **(b)** A CUBIC manufactured solution on a uniform mesh is reproduced exactly at the NODES by both element orders, because the finite-element stencil's truncation error is proportional to a fourth derivative a cubic does not have — 2.2e-13 on a field of scale 113. The same superconvergence appears in the 1D slab-with-generation case for both orders at once, for two *different* reasons (a parabola is in the quadratic space; the linear discrete equations reduce to a central difference a quadratic satisfies exactly), where a study asserting a refinement ratio of 4 measured **0.72**. The fixes are respectively uniform spacing, a quartic field, and measuring INSIDE the elements — where the ratios come out 4.00.
- **WHERE a convergence error is sampled decides which error component it measures — and the CONTROL that exists to catch a broken study can be broken the same way.** A time integrator's error has two parts, a phase lag and an amplitude decay, and a single-instant probe sees only whichever one that instant exposes. Read at a whole number of periods the exact cosine is STATIONARY, so a phase lag `d` enters as `u0·d²/2` rather than `u0·d` and an O(dt²) scheme measured **3.9997** against a theory of 2 (an impossible order, which is the only symptom). Moving the probe to a QUARTER period fixes the phase reading and breaks the other half: there the amplitude multiplies zero, so the deliberately FIRST-order scheme — the row whose entire job is to show the study can tell 1 from 2 — measured **1.344**. Neither point is wrong; neither is complete. The error has to be a NORM over the run, on a time grid common to every refinement level (a finer run is also a finer SAMPLING, and comparing maxima over different sample sets measures that too). Same family as the graded-conduction-mesh and cubic-manufactured-solution findings above, with the extra sting that the safeguard failed in the same direction as the thing it safeguards.
- **A prediction about what a future consumer will need is a HYPOTHESIS, and the consumer arriving is the experiment.** `todo.md` and design.md §3f both recorded that direct time integration would be "the ONE consumer that genuinely wants `C` as a matrix rather than as per-mode ratios". Building it showed the opposite: under proportional damping every appearance of C is a product `C·x = α(M·x) + β(K·x)` — two matvecs against matrices already held, and cheaper than one against an assembled C, since a mass matrix's node-pair block is a scalar times the identity while a stiffness's is full — or a scalar multiple folded into a combined matrix. The filed design would have cost a third sparse matrix to buy a slower operation. The rule is not "do not file predictions" but **write them as claims to be tested rather than as decisions already taken**, and delete them in the same pass that settles them.
- **A measured order below theory may belong to the DOMAIN rather than to the elements, and which one is a measurement.** A hollow cylinder's conduction error converges at 1.28 (linear) and 2.00 (quadratic) against theory 2 and 3 — but the fixture's rings are polygons, so refining the mesh refines the *body*, and the boundary condition is constant along each chord where the true logarithmic profile is not. Refining the angular direction as `n²` instead of `n`, which drops the chord sagitta from O(h²) to O(h⁴), lifts the quadratic sequence to **2.28** and its finest error from 5.8e-4 to **1.1e-4** while leaving the linear one **unchanged** at 1.28 and 1.0e-3. So the two are limited by different things: the quadratic element is good enough that the geometry holds it back, and the linear one is still limited by its own approximation. Same shape as the cantilever's clamped-end singularity capping both orders at 1.86 — which is why a convergence ORDER is measured against a manufactured solution on a box, and an analytic curved case is reported as ACCURACY.
- **A "zero" that is a sum of large cancelling products has its floor set by the summation, not by the quantity.** Asserting that a rigid motion carries no strain energy, `U = ½uᵀKu` measured 2.3e-9 where the strain it came from measured 3.3e-16 — six orders apart, because U is a sum over elements of products of O(1) displacements with O(1e6) stiffnesses that cancel almost totally. The bar for each has to be relative to what a NON-rigid motion of the same size would produce (`E·(u/L)²·V` for the energy), not to the other assertion's scale. Reported relative: 1e-14 for the strain, 1e-15 for the energy.
- **An ambient-occlusion bake is a NEAREST-hit query, and a ray that ESCAPES cannot be pruned.** Two negatives worth keeping about the AO bake's 12.3 s. First, the obvious lever is not there: occlusion accumulates as `1 − t`, so a hit darkens in proportion to how close it is, and the any-hit early-out that "is this ray blocked" invites would be a different renderer (measured 0.055 darker over the vertices that see an occluder at all; a boolean bake is literally the search-radius-to-infinity limit of this one). Second, the exact lever that IS available — descending the nearer child box first — pays only in proportion to how OCCLUDED the geometry is, because an escaping ray never sets the bound below 1 and therefore prunes nothing: **1.19× on a gyroid lattice (mean occlusion 0.70), 1.04× i.e. nothing on a smooth CSG blob (mean occlusion 0.95)**. And the ray count is not a free knob either — halving 16 → 8 is 1.6–1.9× but changes **59 of the 87 rendered docs PNGs**, so it is a decision about appearance, not about performance. The no-less-honest-bake rule survives all three.
- **A block-pointer table costs 8 bytes per block whether or not the block is ever touched.** That is 0.2% of a block's samples and irrelevant up to a 1024³ grid — and 134 MB, allocated before a single sample is evaluated, at 4096³. Hence flat below a threshold, two-level above it, rather than one shape everywhere.
- **Interleaving a grid algorithm's phases is what drops the memory bound from O(n³) to O(n²)** — but the face ordering must then be reconstructed deliberately (bucket by whichever loop variable was outermost in the un-streamed version, then concatenate), or a "bit-for-bit identical" claim quietly holds for vertices and fails for faces.
- ****A fold COUNT is not a tessellation quality metric — measure the worst normal dot.** The cross-drilled bore wall that rendered as a visibly crumpled fan had **zero** strictly-inverted triangles before the fix and zero after; what was wrong was a worst facet-vs-surface dot of **0.0198**, an 88.9° sliver, which a fold count reports as a clean mesh. And the count is not the convergence test either: volume excess over the analytic value ran 61.19/18.60/13.40/11.25 at 32/64/128/256 segments-per-circle, i.e. ratios 3.29/**1.39/1.19** — stalling near 11 and never converging — against 76.20/21.49/5.97/1.82 (ratios 3.55/3.60/3.27, quadratic) after. **Ear clipping is STRUCTURALLY forced into a fan wherever a boundary run is exactly uv-collinear**, which is the normal case, not a pathology: both ring loops of an `ExtrudedSurface` band pull back to a bit-identical v (measured: distinct v bits = 1, 32/32 exactly-collinear uv triples), so `IsEar`'s `<= 0` rejects every corner along both chains and only the unrolled rectangle's four corners are clippable — so no change to the shortest-diagonal metric could have helped, and `Refine` then bisected the fan chords into slivers. Nor would the existing merge walk (`ZipBand`) have worked: it pairs chains by u, so a dense breakout curve against a coarse ring gets fanned from one far vertex and inverts where the curve turns back (measured uv cross −5.9e-4). A monotone sweep is the fix. A fold test must also never take the surface normal at a triangle's CENTROID.** A centroid sits a sagitta (~1e-3 at display quality) inside a curved surface, far past the 1e-6 inverse-evaluation tolerance, so `TryProjectPoint` fails and the assertion silently checks nothing — the first run of the mitered-fillet fold test reported zero folds when there were 808. Average the normals at the triangle's three **vertices**, which are exact surface points by construction.
- **A grid quad's diagonal is now chosen by GEOMETRY — and the tie guard was the hard half.** `PolygonFan` is the one rule (`Triangulated`, `SignedVolume`/`FaceFanStart`, `MeshMassProperties`, `MeshConnectedComponents`, `RenderMesh`, STL/3MF/AMF): a quad splits along its **shorter 3D diagonal**, n-gons keep the corner-0 fan. That resolves the recorded left-hand-thread finding exactly — the mirrored rod's deficit was 3× its twin's purely because mirroring swapped which diagonal a corner-0 fan picked, and the two now measure IDENTICALLY (1.5963 / 0.3997 / 0.1001 at 32/64/128 segments for both, against a mirrored 4.7643 / 1.1974 / 0.3002 before). **Three things the work taught.** (a) **An exact comparison would have reintroduced the same defect**: a great many grid cells have *mathematically equal* diagonals — every UV-sphere quad is mirror-symmetric about its own meridian — so their computed squares differ only in the last ulps, and **408 of the 960 quads of `UvSphere(40, 26)`** flip on round-off at a ratio of 1.000000000000, with the two triangulations equal to twelve digits of the volume deficit. Letting an ulp decide measurably perturbed decimation and remeshing; the fix is a *relative* tie guard (1e-12 on squared lengths, scale-free tier), and the collateral test failures vanished with it — which is what "a test needing a looser band is a bug" looks like when you follow it. (b) **The win is CONSISTENCY, not universally less error**: on a saddle cell the two triangulations bracket the surface (a twisted lofted column reads 244.37/255.27 at 16/12 and 249.66/250.34 at 256/192 — equal in magnitude, opposite in sign, both converging on 250), and one docs assertion was quietly reading the SIGN of the corner-0 fan rather than a property of the solid. (c) **`MeshMassProperties` was 1.5 out of 69.5 adrift** because it walked the half-edge arrays with its own corner-0 fan — a shared rule only holds if every consumer asks it, and the test that asserts volume/render/triangulate/mass-properties agree is what found it. Cost: **18 of 87 rendered docs PNGs move**, all in the non-planar-quad families (SDF/Surface Nets, threads, lofts).
- **A polygon's winding may be reversed freely; its FAN DIAGONAL may not.** Re-winding a quad `[a, b, c, d]` as `[d, c, b, a]` is the same cyclic polygon the other way round, so it is correct for orientation — and wrong for geometry, because everything downstream triangulates by fanning from vertex 0, which moves the split from a–c to b–d. On a skewed non-planar grid cell those are not equally good triangulations. `BRepTessellator`'s reversed-face flip did exactly this, so every *subtracted* tool's face got the worse diagonal: measured on an M8 B-Rep threaded hole (sheared helical grid, cell diagonal ratios up to 40:1) **5 544 of 30 912 facets faced INWARD at a worst facet-vs-surface agreement of −0.163**, while the identical geometry unsubtracted — a threaded rod — was clean at 0.99976. The fix is to rotate the reversal so vertex 0 stays put (`[a, d, c, b]`). The lesson generalizes past this bug: **a quality audit must fan polygons exactly as the consumer does**, because auditing the quad as a unit would have reported this mesh perfect.
- **The general algorithm beat two special cases, again.** The strip path's two documented gaps — a band end sampled at more than two points (a curved cross edge) and a band whose chains meet at a point — were both closed by replacing the rung-counting split with the **monotone stack sweep** already used for slabs, which handles a tied run of end vertices and a single apex as its ordinary start and finish. The load-bearing detail is the tie-break: take the LAST of the tied minimum run and the FIRST of the tied maximum run, so a whole tied run lands on ONE chain — split across both, the merge interleaves the sides at equal keys and the sweep is handed collinear points to triangulate. Every band in the docs tessellates bit-identically either way (all 52 rendered PNGs unchanged), which is what a generalization done right looks like. **Neither shape is reachable from the `Shape` API yet** — `Sphere − Box` corner cuts and cone-apex cuts are refused earlier by the exact boolean, and eighteen further candidates reached neither — so they are covered by hand-built faces in `TrimmedBandGapTests`.
- **"Refinement is not a convergence mechanism" was half the rule; the other half is that refinement may not make a face WORSE — and two residuals filed against the BASE triangulation turned out to be refinement's doing.** Interior rows demoted `Refine` to residue duty, correctly, but residue duty still let it do damage wherever the base's quality is capped by something refinement cannot see. The measured case is a boundary COARSER than the interior grid: a marching-tracer rim keeps whatever sample count the tracer's arc-length step gave it however fine the grid around it becomes, so an interior edge from that rim to a dense natural row is oversized by the step metric and gets bisected — and lifting the midpoint onto the surface swings the two halves past it, replacing a correct facet with an inverted one. `Refine` now refuses such a split, leaving the parent facet (oversized and correct — the fidelity trade its own remarks already permit); the test compares each child's facet-vs-surface agreement against `min(parent, 0)`, which needs no constant and states both halves at once (an agreeing facet may not become opposing, an already-opposing one may not get worse), with a degenerate child scoring −1 and refused alongside. Agreement is read at the facet's own **uv** centroid — legal here and only here, since the standing rule against centroids is about a 3D centroid sitting a sagitta off the surface so inverse evaluation fails, which cannot arise when the uv is known. **Results**: `Torus(12,4) − plane − Ø3 bore` went 2 / 0 / 0 / 1 / 1 / 14 / 53 folds at 16/32/48/64/96/128/192 to **0 at every one**, and the drilled sphere — a corpus member, audited only to 96/48 — went **127 folds at 192/96 (worst −0.9367) to none**. **The diagnostic lesson is the bigger one.** The torus was filed as "the periodic-band tier pairs its chains by u, which is exactly the configuration that inverts" — measured, the merge walk is reached **zero** times on that solid at any density and both chains are u-monotone. A tier was blamed for a stage that ran after it, and what settled it was simply running the pipeline with `refine: false`: the base is fold-free at every density tried. **When a pipeline stage is suspected, run the pipeline without it before theorising about it.** What remains is fidelity, not orientation, and is filed: beside a coarse traced rim the facets stay near-perpendicular (worst 0.009 refined against 0.18 unrefined on the torus; 0.0079 against **0.9144** on the drilled sphere's pole face), so refinement still makes those faces worse, just no longer inside out — and the unrefined base being the better mesh is the sign the real fix is a row path covering the coarse-rim region rather than another rule in `Refine`. **The blunt version was built, measured and rejected**, which is what makes that boundary a decision rather than an omission: strengthening the guard to "may not make any facet agree WORSE than its parent" does exactly what the fidelity numbers ask — on `Box(20,20,20) − Sphere(12)`, the corpus's hardest shape and another residual filed as a missing level path, refinement goes idle, the worst dot matches the base at every density, and **96/48 CLEARS the corpus floor (0.9814 vs 0.9808)** where the shipped result sits at 0.9240 — and it breaks two things: at the COARSEST density refinement genuinely helps (16/8 measures 0.8369 base against 0.8832 refined) so that row regresses below its own committed floor, and `BandCrossingTool_ConvergesWithTessellationDensity` stalls (volume steps 9.236e-3 then 8.741e-3 where the test requires them to shrink). **Refinement helps where the base is coarse and hurts where the base is already at grid density**; a rule that cannot tell those apart is not the fix, so the guard stays at the inversion test, which is the unambiguous part.
- **"Collinear is not a turn" must mean straight to within ROUND-OFF, or the rule never fires — and the facet it lets through is degenerate only in uv.** The monotone sweep pops at a convex turn and deliberately treats collinear as no turn, precisely because a ring's samples are collinear in uv and popping there emits zero-area facets. The intent was right; the test was `cross > 0`, exact — so on a constant-parameter boundary run, where the true cross IS zero, the decision fell to the pullback's own noise. What comes through is not a harmless degenerate facet: **uv-collinear is not 3D-collinear**, so three consecutive samples of a curved rim span a REAL facet whose normal is the rim's binormal rather than the surface's — the trap the tier order exists to avoid, arriving through the tier that was supposed to be the cure, and invisible to every sliver guard because the facet is 3D-fat. It was found by a FINGERPRINT, not a symptom: every fold on a threaded rod's 45° lead-in chamfer read facet-vs-surface agreement of **exactly −0.7071 = −cos 45°**, the angle between the cone and the end plane the fan lay in. `TurnsIntoInterior` now tests the dimensionless **sine** of the turn (`|cross| ≤ 1e-9·|b−a|·|c−b|`), and that constant is not tuned: dividing by the two edge lengths SEPARATES the populations rather than shrinking both, since the noise is absolute in uv while a genuine turn scales with the chord — measured ~4e-12 for a jitter turn against ~1.6e-2 for a real one at 64 segments/circle (~4e-3 at 256), ten orders apart. Radians are dimensionless, which is why this guard is deliberately ABSOLUTE where the ladder's default is relative; the comparison that matters is local to the triple. Declining to pop is always safe — the vertices stay on the stack and are fanned later from the opposite chain, already the path an exactly-collinear run took. **The oracle that proves it exact is the facet COUNT, not the fold count**: scanning 5% steps of the thread depth on M6×1 / M8×1.25 / M10×1.5 / M12×1.75 with both ends chamfered, 10 of 76 depths folded (0/4/3/3 per size, at unrelated fractions — an alignment phenomenon, not a threshold), and after the guard **exactly those 10 rows change while the other 66 stay byte-identical**, every changed row keeping its facet count to the unit (16 526 → 16 526, …). Folds went N → 0 and worst agreement −0.7071 → 0.513…0.730, inside the 0.562…0.979 band the never-folding depths already occupied — one population, so nothing was traded. Same shape as `PolygonFan`'s tie guard (408 of 960 UV-sphere quads decided by an ulp) and `MeshDecimator`'s absolute-epsilon-on-an-area: a predicate is only as meaningful as the scale it is compared against.
- **Both loud trimmed-face refusals are UNREACHABLE from the `Shape` API, and that was worth finding out rather than guessing.** Pole-bounded single-chain bands with holes and |winding| > 1 loops are blocked one layer up, by the exact B-Rep boolean. The near miss is instructive: a latitude cut DOES give a sphere a pole-bounded cap with a single winding loop, and drilling that cap off-axis does NOT put a hole in it — the boolean re-splits so the bore's rim lands on the two-ring band below, which the slab sweep already handles. Cutting lower, or the same on a cone or a torus, fails earlier with "produced an unclosed solid"; |winding| > 1 needs a curve wrapping a periodic surface twice, which only a helical intersection gives and those are refused before tessellation. `TrimmedFaceRefusalTests` locks the verdict AND drives both refusals on hand-built faces, so the backstop's messages cannot rot while nothing reaches them.
- **A non-converging error whose sign FLIPS is a fixed floor, not a tolerance.** A bore drilled into an extruded SIDE face misses the inscribed-ngon volume by −7.4e-4 / −5.3e-5 / +4.7e-5 / +6.5e-5 at 32/64/128/256 segments, while the identical hole in the top cap lands within 1e-13. The cause is not the trimmed triangulation, which was the standing assumption: the rim on the side face is a **57-sample `PolylineCurve3d` baked in by the marching tracer**, because plane-as-a-bounded-extrusion ∩ cylinder is not one of `SurfaceIntersection`'s analytic pairs, and a 57-gon is a floor no `segmentsPerCircle` can lower — the sign flips when the analytic reference's own n-gon crosses it. Same shape of finding as the cross-drilled housing's breakout curves. The fix is a promotion in `SurfaceIntersection`, not a sampling rule.
- **Refinement can make a mesh WORSE — resolved by interior rows in the base triangulation, and the numbers prove the diagnosis.** The base has to carry the surface's curvature itself: trimmed bands now take the natural grid's own sample rows into their BASE (`RowedStrip`/`RowedPeriodicBand`/`RowedPoleFan` — level paths threading between scallops, anchored on existing boundary vertices, never inventing a boundary point; full-period rows with closure duplicates for winding bands; seam chords pre-split with bit-identical twins since a seam is an unrolling artifact internal to the face; a uv-area identity as the closing guard with the rowless sweep as fallback, so the rowed path can never be worse). The drilled sphere `Sphere(10) − Cylinder(3, 40)` went **43 948 facets / 12 folds / worst −0.2022 → 3 244 / 0 folds / 0.9994** (32/24), stopped refusing at 128/96, and converges at ratios 4.35 / 5.08 — it is now a corpus member with an analytic napkin-ring volume row. Two rules that made it work: **`Refine`'s step metric is per-axis (max-norm), never the 2-norm** — the natural grid's own cell diagonal spans one step in EACH axis and measures √2 under a 2-norm, so refinement would bisect the very grid that defines the quality bar; and **pole-fan edges are refinement-exempt** — the pole's u is arbitrary, so a fan edge's uv u-span is an artifact, not curvature (refining a *flat* vase disk's fan bent it into 467 folds at −1.0). With rows in place Refine measures IDLE on 16 of 19 corpus members' trimmed faces and keeps only its honest duty: residual coarse columns between honest samples.
- **The corpus quality gate is `TessellationCorpusQualityTests` + `TessellationQuality`** — 35 constructions (drilled/cross-drilled plates, spherical cavities, a drilled sphere, threaded rods and holes, a clearance thread, lofts, shells, drafts, mitered and whole-solid fillets, chamfers, variable-law fillets incl. a partial run, vases, partial revolves, sweeps, tori, cones, curved-face shells and drafts, sketch pockets, Bézier engraving, wedges, a side-wall breakout, a drilled breakout) audited facet-by-facet against their exact surfaces at 16/8, 48/24 and 96/48, on **unwelded per-face polygons** (`BRepTessellator.TessellateByFace`, an internal seam — welding is what destroys the facet→face attribution the measurement needs). One floor for every surface family, `cos(3·2π/n)`: three natural grid steps, the allowance being for facets where two INDEPENDENTLY sampled boundaries meet. A per-family floor tuned to what each happened to measure would pass everything and protect nothing. It also separates **degenerate slivers** (facet height below `1e-13 × the face's extent` — relative, since an absolute epsilon on a cross product is an area threshold) from **folds**: "the normal is noise" and "the normal points the wrong way" are different defects with different fixes, and both get asserted. First run caught three defects; all three are fixed (the third by the interior-rows upgrade above). Now **35 constructions** — the newest is `clearance thread`, an eroded rod half of whose bands are ARC generators, so both cap loops chain `HelicalArcCut3d` beside `SpiralArc3d` (0.457 / 0.979 / 0.995 against floors of 0.383 / 0.924 / 0.981); before it came `drilled breakout` (see below), joining `side-wall breakout` (a bore through a sketch extrusion's walls and out of its top, the construction the bounded-patch conic CLIP unblocked: clean at all three densities with an analytic disc-less-segment volume row), the drilled sphere joined once rows took it past the floor at every density, the curved-corner work added a shelled cup, a shelled cone, a shelled elbow, a drafted cylinder and a variable-radius fillet, and the variable-law partial run joined with an analytic (1−π/4)·L·(r₀²+r₀r₁+r₁²)/3 volume row, all clean at all three densities with `KnownOffSurface` still empty. **ONE construction sits below the floor and is locked with committed baselines rather than dropped**: `Box(20,20,20) − Sphere(12)` (the cavity breaks out of all six faces) went **101 246 facets / 266 folds / worst −0.2426 → 4 608 / 0 folds / 0.7024** at 48/24 and tessellates at 96/48 where it used to refuse; what remains is a narrow column at each hole rim's u-extreme where the rim tangent goes vertical and no level path can anchor (`SpherePiercingEverySide_HasNoFoldsAndABoundedResidual`, filed in todo.md). **`drilled breakout` used to be the second and is now an ordinary member, and the way it was diagnosed is the part worth keeping.** A BLIND `Shape.Drill` whose flat pole cap breaks out of the top face was structurally CLEAN — no folds, no degenerate slivers, volume converging quadratically — while its bore wall's worst agreement ran **0.107 / 0.694 / 0.840** against floors of 0.383 / 0.924 / 0.981. What said where the fault was is the corpus member `side-wall breakout`, the SAME cut made by a `Shape.Cylinder`, which read **0.9992 / 0.9999 / 0.99998**: the two differ in exactly one variable, the tool, so it was the drill's `RevolvedSurface` bore wall rather than the breakout — and a plain drilled-THROUGH hole passes, so a drill's revolve band is fine in general. The cause was that a plane PARALLEL to a revolve's axis reached no analytic arm, so the two straight cuts a breakout makes arrived as a **49-sample tracer polyline at every density**; `SurfaceIntersection.TryCylindricalBand` closed it (0.994 / 0.957 / 0.989 on 92 / 220 / 424 facets against 374 / 1 008 / 3 682). The test that pinned it asserted BOTH sides — below the floor AND clean — so the fix failed it naming the promotion, which is what a locked residual is for; what replaces it is a same-grade comparison against the cylinder route it was diagnosed against.
- **"All the rendered PNGs are byte-identical" is the right oracle for a change that must not move geometry, and the WRONG one for a change that legitimately re-decomposes it.** Carrier clipping stops a face being split along geometry its partner does not share, so face counts fall by design (18 → 11, 20 → 13 on the two ambient-occlusion fixtures) while the solid stays the same solid — same polygon count, same volume to nine decimals. But the triangulation of a face-with-one-hole is not the triangulation of eight rectangles, so the flat render mesh's vertices sit in different places and their baked occlusion differs: **7 of 106 docs PNGs moved, every one a B-Rep-boolean scene, none with a changed silhouette**, and the two committed AO fingerprints had to be re-taken. What survives as an oracle is what the faces MEAN — fewer, better-shaped faces at the same volume is an improvement, a changed silhouette would not be — so the argument has to be made in the commit rather than delegated to a byte comparison. Measure the delta, look at both images, and say which it is. **And never read the output directory of a build that has not said it is done** — the first DocsGen pass was polled mid-run, reported four, and three more landed after; two further runs from a clean checkout reproduced all seven bit-identically, so the renderer was deterministic and the *observation* was the thing that was wrong.
- **Zero-area triangles are not merely useless — near a tangency they are actively WRONG.** A sliver clipped along a boundary curve takes that curve's binormal as its normal, so wherever a trim curve is tangent to a neighbouring face (every fillet's tangency line) its orientation is decided by rounding, and no epsilon fixes it. See the trimmed-face tier order above.
- **Refinement is not a convergence mechanism.** The midpoint-split pass terminates on a monotone-decrease rule, so wherever that rule cuts a cascade the mesh keeps a coarse patch forever. A tessellation whose accuracy depends on refinement has no convergence guarantee — measured, the ear-clipped fillet's volume error stopped improving past curveSamples 96 and then wandered. Get the base triangulation right; use refinement only for genuine curvature between honest samples. **This rule is now enforced structurally in the trimmed tiers**: interior rows put the natural grid into the base, and the corpus measures refinement idle wherever the rows engage — where refinement still visibly inflates a mesh, the finding is a missing row path, not a refinement bug.
- **A recursion whose depth is a property of the INPUT has no "big enough" stack.** Learned from the (now deleted) BSP boolean, and the rule outlives the code: `CsgNode`'s splitting plane was the first polygon's plane, so a convex body — a sphere above all — built an essentially degenerate chain of depth O(polygons). Two 32k-triangle spheres overflowed the CLR stack **inside `Build`** (not `Invert`, as this file long assumed) and killed the process; measured threshold, a 3000-node chain built in 419 ms and a 4000-node chain crashed. Rewriting all five walks with explicit stacks fixed the crash and bought honesty rather than speed — the completed run returned an *open* shell in 74.9 s — which is what settled the decision to retire it.
- **A batch of refinement points must be INDEPENDENT, or the bound that makes refinement finite silently evaporates.** Delaunay refinement terminates because a tetrahedron's circumcentre is, by the empty-circumsphere property, at least its circumradius from every existing vertex — so points pack no denser than the local radius and a bounded domain holds boundedly many. That guarantee is against the triangulation *as it was when the circumcentre was computed*. Inserting a batch straight off the priority queue voids it: the second point has no relationship to the first, so two candidates land arbitrarily close, make a sliver, and feed the next round. Measured on a 4-unit box asked for 1.5-unit elements: 200-, 800- and 3000-point budgets all exhausted without converging; requiring each accepted point to be half a circumradius from the others accepted in the same pass converges to 836 elements identically at every budget. The same shape of error as seeding a priority queue off incomplete quadrics — a quantity used after the thing it depends on has moved.
- **A refinement rule that responds to a defect must be LOCAL, or the response is the next defect.** The boundary-recovery answer to a face that failed to lie on the surface was to split every sub-triangle of the PATCH it touched; on a box that quadruples a whole face per round, so one interior insertion near a wall cascaded the entire surface and exhausted a 500 000-point budget. Ruppert's encroachment ball — split exactly the sub-triangles whose diametral ball contains the offending vertex — is the smallest region that provably has to change. It also needs a size floor: without one, a circumcentre sitting essentially ON the surface encroaches forever, each split halving the balls while the point stays inside.
- **Seed a priority queue only after the quantity it is keyed on is COMPLETE.** Folding QEM seeding into the quadric-accumulation loop keys the whole initial queue off *partially accumulated* quadrics. Every mesh still came out closed, manifold and at exactly the requested face count — the sole symptom was a **2.4× worse approximation error at light decimation**, where the cheapest (earliest) choices dominate. A committed per-case error baseline is what caught it; without one it would have shipped invisibly.
- **Feature detection reads the mesh you give it, not the surface you meant.** A coarse tessellation of a smooth surface has large dihedrals — `UvSphere(12, 8)` facets meet at ~30°, so the remesher's default 30° feature angle pins much of it and refinement cannot help (splitting an edge leaves the halves coplanar with their parents, so the dihedral is unchanged). Pass 0, or a larger angle, when remeshing tessellated curvature; the default protects CAD creases, which is the more dangerous failure to get wrong.
- **A bit-identity test between a restricted path and the full one is VACUOUS until you prove the restriction fired — and the proof has to be a counter, not a proxy.** Restricting face-aligned reprojection's accumulation to the faces incident to the remesher's active set is sound and bit-identical, and the test asserting so passed **with the restriction deliberately broken** (the membership test truncated to one of a triangle's three vertices, which drops faces that genuinely contribute). The fixture — a coarse 20×13 sphere at a 0.22 target — never let a single face be skipped, **42 996 of a possible 42 996 even at 60 passes**, so both runs were the same whole-mesh walk and the comparison agreed with a broken implementation as happily as with a correct one. A proxy for "did the active set shrink" was tried first and was also wrong: comparing queue output against SWEEP output finds a difference for unrelated reasons. What works is an internal counter (`RemeshResult.FacesAccumulated`) asserted to be strictly smaller BEFORE the positions are compared — the same shape as the Surface Nets rule that a regression fixture must assert it still CARRIES the configuration it exists to test. With a fixture that does go quiet, both failure modes are caught: a dropped contributing face, and a changed visit ORDER — and note that the second differs only in the last bits (`…4258002` against `…42581595`), so only a bit comparison sees it, which is why float addition's non-associativity forces the ascending scan rather than a gathered list.
- **Establish a mechanism by SUBTRACTION before building the fix the symptom suggests — and the plausible half of a two-part fix can be the harmful half.** The remesher's longest edge stalls near 2L while its distribution converges to 95% in band, and the recorded diagnosis (a collapse leaving a fresh 2L edge for the next pass) was wrong. Disabling one stage at a time settles it in three runs: with **flips** off the same run ends at *exactly* the 1.33L split threshold with nothing out of band, while with smoothing AND projection off the maximum is unmoved at 2.07L — so the splits were always doing their job and the flip stage, whose predicate is pure valence arithmetic with no length term, was swapping the short diagonal of an elongated quad for the long one. No within-pass re-visit queue was needed at all (the filed suggestion): there is no cascade, since one split-only round over a converged mesh clears all 58 out-of-band edges with exactly 58 splits and a second round finds nothing. **Two further rules came out of the fix itself.** (a) **The obvious form of a guard can be the wrong one**: refusing *every* flip that would leave an out-of-band edge strands the sliver whose only remedy was a flip from 2.5L to 1.5L — measured, a worst triangle angle of **0.02°** on a remeshed box against **31.7°** for the monotone form that also permits a flip strictly shortening the edge it replaces (exact `<` on squared lengths, a monotone-decrease rule and not a tolerance; permitting equality would let a pair of flips ping-pong). The monotone form is also the one with a statable invariant — a flip can no longer raise the longest edge — where the strict form merely sounds stronger. (b) **Reordering the sweep to split before flipping measured actively harmful and was dropped**, though the argument for it sounds airtight ("an out-of-band edge has a definite remedy, so a combinatorial heuristic should not pre-empt it"): a flip *is* a remedy for a long edge, since the other diagonal of an elongated quad is the short one, so splitting instead pins the bad configuration and adds a vertex to it — in-band share 92.4% → **85.6%**, worst angle 0.89° → **0.17°**, and slower. The incumbent collapse → flip → split order was already right. **And the honest closing note is the metric**: everything the remesher reports is edge LENGTH, which says nothing about slivers — the box fixture sits at 95.1% in band with a worst angle of 5.57°, and the strict guard reached 99.7% in band at 0.02°.
- **A saving whose opportunity is a ONE-DIMENSIONAL subcomplex of a surface is not a saving, and the control arm is what proves the instrument.** Face-aligned reprojection restricts its accumulation to the faces incident to the active set under QUEUE scheduling and walks every face under SWEEP — filed as "with every vertex active the restriction could only add a membership test per face", which conflates a *candidate* with a vertex the pass WRITES. Under sweep every vertex is a candidate, but a PINNED one is never written (the read loop skips it), so a face with three pinned corners contributes to nothing and skipping it is legal: built, and **bit-identical on all eight fixtures** (0 differing vertices), so what is declined is the payoff rather than the soundness. The bound is structural, not fixture-specific — **a pinned set is a one-dimensional subcomplex of the surface** (boundary loops and crease chains) and a face needs ALL THREE corners in it, so the skippable set is the faces a curve fully contains, a vanishing fraction of a two-dimensional mesh. Measured over fixtures chosen to MAXIMISE pinning (a box whose twelve creases all pin, a patch whose whole rim pins, a coarse sphere at the default 30° angle): **0.23% to 9.17%**, the ceiling being a cylinder whose n-gon caps put a fully pinned rim around a one-triangle-deep fan. **No timing separated it from noise, and the control arm is what says so rather than a p-value**: an arm skipping **0.00%** of faces — where the two builds differ only by the test itself — measured **0.795×**, so the harness's band is wider than the whole effect and every apparent win in the column (1.147×, 1.116×, 1.068×) sits inside it. Reverted. **Two riders.** The filed scoping was backwards in the instructive direction — it offered the saving to "a small `FixedVertices` set", and a small pinned set is exactly the case that skips nothing; the saving needs a LARGE one, and even a maximal one reaches single digits. And the test that pinned the incumbent behaviour was VACUOUS: it ran on a closed featureless sphere with `FeatureAngleDegrees = 0`, whose `ConstrainedCount` is **0**, so "sweep walks every face" held there however the accumulation were written — it now runs on the cylinder (98 constrained) and asserts the fixture carries pins BEFORE asserting the count, the same shape as the restriction's own counter rule one bullet down.
- **Where the standard algorithm exists, take it over the reference library's heuristic.** g3's `MinimalHoleFill` is four iterative edge-flip passes whose own comments describe strong ordering effects, non-convergence, hard pass caps to stop oscillation, and a forced interior-vertex-removal stage with a debugger break in it. The Barequet–Sharir/Liepa dynamic program solves the same problem deterministically and globally optimally in O(n³)/O(n²) — trivial at realistic rim lengths. Its dihedral measure is `atan2(|a×b|, a·b)` on **raw** normals: exact at any magnitude, no normalization and no epsilon anywhere.
- **Degeneracy guards on areas and determinants must be RELATIVE, never absolute** (the BSP lesson, occurrences two through five — `SketchRegion`'s bounding-box reject compares SQUARED distances, so its conservativeness slack is relative too: `1e-15`, about 9 ulps against the bound's own ≤4 ulps of arithmetic error, the scale-free tier and never the weld tier; `MeshDecimator` was the third and fourth — `MeshDecimator` was the third and fourth: it normalized face normals with `TryNormalize(Tolerance.Default)`, an absolute 1e-9 applied to a *cross product* i.e. an area, so below ~1e-4 scale every face read as degenerate, contributed no quadric, and decimation became arbitrary — measured **91% volume error** on a 1e-5-scale sphere, now 3.1%; its normal-flip guard likewise compared a squared area against an absolute 1e-24. Both are now `1e-13 × extent`, the exact boolean's scale-free tier, and `Remesher` was written to that rule from the start — every threshold relative to target edge length, every degeneracy guard to its square, and guards reading the **sign** of the dot of two unnormalized cross products): `Sketch`'s enclosed-area, arc-chord and `ArcThrough` circumcenter guards were absolute 1e-12 and therefore wrong in *both* directions — a legitimate 1 µm × 0.5 µm pocket was rejected as enclosing no area, while a 1000 × 1e-10 sliver passed; three metre-scale points 1e-10 off a line built a radius-1.25e15 circle. They now scale by extent² / magnitude² (`Sketch.RelativeDegeneracy`), because an area is quadratic in scale. Deliberately still absolute, with reasons in code: sketch closure (the 1e-9 weld tier) and sweep angular guards (radians are dimensionless).
- **An exact predicate applied to a quantity that is round-off answers CONFIDENTLY and WRONG — and a tangent evaluated AT a tangency is exactly such a quantity.** The curved 2D arrangement orders the edges at a node by the exact `Orient2dSign` of their departure directions, which is right everywhere except where it matters most: at a tangency the two directions differ only by arithmetic noise. Measured, a disc tangent to a plate's straight edge from outside gave the arc a departure of **(−1.22e-16, −1)** — the x sign is nothing but the error in `sin(π)` — which put it on the wrong side of the plate's exactly vertical edge, so the tightest-turn walk closed **no face at all** and the union came back **empty**, not merely inaccurate. Only the CURVATURE carries information there, so a second pass re-orders each cyclic run of tangentially tied departures by it (cyclic because a tangency along +x puts one departure at the very start of the fan and its partner at the very end). **The tie band is DERIVED, not chosen**: a vertex may sit up to the snap tolerance from the true tangency point, and displacing a point by δ along a circle of radius r rotates its radial — hence its tangent — by δ/r = δ·|κ|, so the band is `snap·max(|κ₁|, |κ₂|)` plus a few-ulp arithmetic floor. That floor is all that remains for two straight edges, so genuinely distinct line directions stay decided exactly. Same shape as design.md §5's `DepartureAngle` note: **Shewchuk exactness is exactness about the coordinates you hand it.**
- **A closed curve's END must be its START, exactly — a parameterization that merely evaluates to the same place is not closed.** `sin(2π)` is not 0 in doubles, so a full-turn arc built as `centre + r·(cos θ, sin θ)` at θ₀ and θ₀ + 2π lands ~2e-16·r apart. That gap is not cosmetic: a +x parity ray whose ordinate falls INSIDE it counts the seam piece's two endpoints on opposite sides, and a point measurably inside a disc reads as outside — found by a disc ∩ rectangle that returned empty where the analytic answer was a 61.4-unit circular segment. Two rules fix it and both are load-bearing: a full turn's end point IS its start point (an exact substitution, not a snap), and an arc's first and last y-monotone piece take their ordinate from the **stored endpoints**, never re-derived from the angle — which is also what makes a chain's parity consistent across every joint, since the next edge starts at exactly this edge's stored end. The same shape of bug as the boolean's canonical-crossing rule: recomputation must be bit-reproducible, not merely close. **It recurred in `SketchRegion` (2026-08-03) — the same two rules, in the OTHER 2D parity machinery, because the fix had been applied to `Arc2d`'s pieces and not to `ArcSeg`'s.** `ArcSeg.MonotonePieces` built every piece's endpoints from `PointAt`, so a full circle's last piece ended `r·2.449e-16` below where its first began, leaving a band of ordinates covered by NEITHER right-branch piece: `Sketch.Circle(70.5)` read `(60, 0)` as −10.5 and `(60, −1.47e-14)` as **+10.5**, ten units from any boundary, the magnitude right and only the parity flipped. It is reachable without anyone writing a tiny number — sampling a full turn INCLUSIVELY (θ = 2πi/n, i up to n) lands the last sample exactly there — and it surfaced as an **odd 121 boundary transitions** around a 60-tooth ring, combinatorially impossible on a closed curve. Two lessons ride along. The **fix is scoped by `IsFullCircle`**, since a partial arc's two ends are genuinely different points, and that scoping is pinned by a test that passed before the fix and must keep passing. And **the workaround was the evidence**: the discovering test sampled `[0, 2π)` to route around it, and the repair inverts that — it now samples INCLUSIVELY on purpose so the seam is exercised deliberately, with an odd transition count as the guard. A parity count is the assertion with teeth here precisely because its impossibility needs no knowledge of where the seam is.
- **When a "boolean instability" and a "tessellation artifact" are both plausible, PROVE which by asking the mesh, not by tightening an epsilon.** The 2D boolean carried a filed defect for a long time — "leaves ~1e-7-area pinholes at near-tangency; a cell-classification fix, not an epsilon one" — repro'd by a 64×48 torus silhouette seen side-on. It is the opposite: 780 of 780 probe points inside that 1.45e-5 hole are covered by **zero** facets, tested with the exact `Orient2d` over every triangle, so the union returned is the correct union of what the boolean was given, and the *tessellated solid's own shadow* has the hole. In the band |z| ∈ [r·cos(π/n_minor), r] — the minor polygon's scallop, 4.28e-3 deep at n = 48 — the discrete tube only reaches that height near its minor-polygon VERTICES, and the major discretization breaks that thin band into lenses that need not overlap (the hole measured 1.16e-3 deep, a quarter of the scallop). It is not systematic either: holes at 64×48 and none at 32×24, 96×72, 128×96, 64×96 or 128×48, because whether two neighbouring lenses overlap is an *alignment* question, not a tolerance one. **A silhouette is the shadow of the mesh you give it, not of the surface you meant** — the remesher's feature-detection rule in new clothes. The test now asserts the strong form (every hole is uncovered by every facet), which is what separates "the boolean lost material" from "the tessellation has no material there".
- **Splitting a face by curves ONE AT A TIME and splitting it by all of them AT ONCE are different algorithms, and which one is needed is a property of the curves, not a preference.** A cascade works — and is what B-Rep booleans have always done — for exactly one reason: a curve that enters and leaves through the face's boundary closes its own arrangement, so a later curve meets an earlier one's segments as ordinary boundary edges of the fragment it is splitting and curve–curve crossings come out for free. It structurally cannot work the moment a curve TERMINATES INSIDE the face, because the FIRST curve applied has no partner segments to end on: its arrangement has a dangling edge, and a dangling edge cannot be traced. `FaceSplitter.SplitByCurves` therefore owns the routing and `SplitByCurve` is the one-curve call into it, so the incumbent path is reached *through* the general one rather than beside it. **The gate that picks between them is "a curve stops strictly inside the face WHERE ANOTHER CURVE IS THERE TO MEET IT", and the partner clause is the load-bearing half** — it separates a CLIPPED curve, whose terminus is a real corner the neighbouring face's curve continues from, from a TRACER-TRUNCATED one, whose terminus is an artefact of the marching step and has nothing to meet. Tracer truncation is common, so a gate without the clause engages the new path on ordinary geometry: measured on `Torus(12, 4) − plane − Ø3 bore`, a documented refusal either way, routing it to the arrangement made it fail as "two band-bottom boundaries with no top between them" instead of as the boolean's own "unclosed solid" — one stage earlier and less informatively. **An algorithm that can only trade one refusal for another should not be reached at all.** Three more rules the simultaneous path needed. **A junction where two curves meet END TO END is not a crossing and must not be reached for by loosening a transversality test** — they touch, and whether a crossing test fires there depends on the angle they happen to meet at; coincident endpoints are matched explicitly, at the seam tier. And **the gate is tested before a single coedge moves**: `SplitEdge` patches every loop using the edge, including neighbouring faces', so a split that refused halfway would leave them half-edited — the rim-surgery all-or-nothing rule, in the boolean core.
- **A loop WRAPS a periodic surface by net DRIFT, not by SPAN — and a span test is wrong in the direction that hides.** Three sites decided "is this a band boundary?" by whether the pulled loop's u reached three quarters of the period; a contractible loop can reach that far and come back, and the case that found it is not exotic — a threaded rod's end-chamfer facet on its cone spans **272°** and closes with a net drift of **0.02 rad**. What follows is not an inaccuracy but a category error at each consumer: `FaceSplitter.TraceFaces` hunts a partner boundary to pair the facet with, wrap-splitting lets a wrapping cut fabricate a phantom band out of it, and `BrepBoolean.ProbePoint` takes the band path — which probes halfway toward the surface's own v domain edge, correct for a ring-bounded band and catastrophic here, because the probe lands OUTSIDE the fragment and the facet is classified away as if it were the other solid's material. It is now one rule, `FaceGeometry.LoopWrapsPeriod`, asked by all three (the "one shared rule only holds if every caller asks it" lesson, applied before the third copy could drift). The span stays as the cheap first half of an AND: no loop can drift a period without spanning one.
- **A shortcut justified "by construction" is a claim about the INPUTS, and the input that breaks it is usually the one the feature exists for.** `BrepBoolean.ProbePoint` skips the parity check on a pole-bounded face because "everything between rim and pole belongs to the face by construction" — sound, and it silently assumed the loop IS the rim. The moment another solid CUTS the cap the loop still wraps but is no longer level, so the loop's AVERAGE v names a v the face no longer reaches at every u: measured on a blind `Shape.Drill` whose flat pole cap breaks out of a plate's top face, the probe came back **0.106 above that face**, the surviving fragment was classified away, and the whole boolean refused with "3 of 19 edges are used by 1 face(s)" — a crack along the entire rim, three stages from the cause. Restating the shortcut's own theorem fixes it and needs no new test: a single loop that wraps the period separates the pole from everything else, so every v strictly between the pole and the loop's CLOSEST APPROACH is inside at every u — which reduces to the incumbent average exactly when the loop is level, i.e. for every case that already worked. **Two riders worth as much as the fix.** The obvious repair is the wrong one: `FaceGeometry.ContainsTwoSided` exists precisely for pole faces, and it errs toward inside BY DESIGN, so it accepts the bad point — a verifier chosen for the right reason and wrong in the direction that matters. And the entry filed against this named a different stage entirely (a missing planar-carrier recognizer for the coaxial annulus); building that recognizer left the boolean's output **bit-identical**, because the intersection there is a straight CHORD and a traced polyline along a straight curve is exact at every point, not merely at its vertices — so the tracer's only defect is end truncation, which `SnapTracerEnds` already removes.
- **A one-sided parity ray is correct exactly where the trim CLOSES in the direction it is cast, and which way that breaks is decided by the GENERATOR'S DIRECTION.** `FaceSplitter`'s arrangement fires one ray along +v, which a pole leaves open: a profile leaving the axis puts the rim at v = max so the ray crosses it (and every drill tool has always split), while a profile RETURNING to the axis puts the rim at v = min, the ray crosses nothing, and the identical cap reads as having no interior at all. Measured on a cylinder built as ONE full-turn revolve, whose two caps differ in precisely that: `SplitByCurve` returned a single fragment — rim edge dutifully split, no interior edge made, no second face traced — for **56 of 112** (offset, azimuth, curve-kind) chords, and they were exactly the 56 on the cap whose generator ends on the axis. `FaceGeometry.ParityRayPointsDown` is the rule and the splitter ASKS it. **The boundary is a measurement rather than caution**: `FaceGeometry.Contains` deliberately does NOT ask it, because made pole-aware there the parity changes which STRUCTURE a split produces rather than merely which points read as inside — a sphere's cap then accepts an interior bore circle as a HOLE where the incumbent reading sends the same cut down the band path, and a pole-bounded face carrying a hole is a trimmed tier that does not exist, so three documented constructions stopped tessellating by name. An algorithm that can only trade one refusal for another is not reached at all, so that correction waits on the tier and the upward ray there is load-bearing rather than merely incumbent. And `ContainsTwoSided` is not the substitute it looks like: it errs toward INSIDE by design, right for a keep-or-drop decision and wrong for an arrangement, where accepting a segment that should have been dropped puts a phantom edge into the trace.
- **A last-resort search over a bounding BOX says nothing about a region that is thin.** `BrepBoolean.ProbePoint`'s fallback was a uniform 12×12 uv grid over the pulled loops' extents — the recorded "a sampling grid in parameter space says nothing about coverage in model space" lesson, here about a region's SHAPE rather than a band's aspect, and reached even though the box itself was near-isotropic. Measured on a bore grazing a plate's top face at a half-chord of 0.35: the fragment being classified away is an L — a 0.23 rad wedge joined to a 0.048-tall ring — and the grid's 0.63 × 0.083 step lands in neither arm, so the whole boolean refused for want of one point on a face it was about to discard. **The loops ARE the region's own resolution**, so the fallback steps off the fragment's own boundary instead: perpendicular in uv from each edge midpoint, BOTH signs (which removes the orientation convention rather than getting it right — a reversed face's loops wind the other way), on a geometric ladder of step fractions so an arbitrarily thin fragment is still reached, widest clearance winning so it stands near a boundary only when there is nowhere else to stand. It runs strictly after the grid and therefore only where the code previously threw, which is the whole safety argument. With the cylindrical-band carrier it took the grazing breakout family from refusing at a half-chord of 0.35 to closing at 0.24 — **exactly where the `Shape.Cylinder` route stops too**, so what is left is one near-tangency (the bore's entry rim against the plate's top EDGE, a circle against a line) rather than two limits for two reasons.
- **Two units that are each individually right cannot be caught by any test — only by making them ONE.** `Materials.Steel.Density` said 7.85e-9 (tonne/mm³) while `PartMassProperties`' remarks said 7.85e-6 (kg/mm³), a factor of 1000, and BOTH shipped, in docs, for as long as there were two `Material`-shaped ideas. Nothing could catch it: neither figure is wrong, only the pairing is, and a kernel that carries no units at runtime has nothing to compare.
